Inference for location and height of peaks of a standardized field after selection
Alden Green, Jonathan Taylor, [0.5em] Department of Applied Mathematics, Statistics, Johns Hopkins University

Abstract
Peak inference concerns the use of local maxima (“peaks”) of a noisy random field to detect and localize regions where underlying signal is present. We propose a peak inference method that first subjects observed peaks to a significance test of the null hypothesis that no signal is present, and then uses the peaks that are declared significant to construct post-selectively valid confidence regions for the location and height of nearby true peaks. We analyze the performance of this method in a smooth signal plus constant variance noise model under a high-curvature asymptotic assumption, and prove that it asymptotically controls both the number of false discoveries, and the number of confidence regions that do not contain a true peak, relative to the number of points at which inference is conducted. An important intermediate theoretical result uses the Kac-Rice formula to derive a novel approximation to the intensity function of a point process that counts local maxima, which is second-order accurate under the alternative, nearby high-curvature true peaks.



1
Introduction

Peak inference
(
21
;
50
;
12
;
35
;
10
)
refers to a class
of statistical methods designed for multiple testing problems with a spatial or temporal component, in which the goal is to detect and localize regions where an
underlying signal is non-zero. This is a fundamental goal in neuroimaging analyses, where such regions correspond to areas of the brain that activate in response to an external stimulus. It is also of interest in scientific applications such as astroimaging
(
32
)
and climate modeling
(
40
)
.

It has been argued that the classical approach of conducting
inference simultaneously across all points is ill-suited for identifying regions that contain signal
(
11
;
12
)
. In contrast, peak inference methods treat the data as discrete observations of an
underlying smooth process, and conduct inference only at
local maxima (“peaks”) of this process, rather than at every
observed location or time point. Intuitively, the location of peaks identify promising areas where signal may be present, while peak height estimates the strength of the signal which in many problems is a useful measure of effect size. The task of the statistician is to conduct formal inference based on the location and height of peaks.

Previous work on peak inference has primarily dealt with
detection
: does a given peak, observed in the data, indicate the presence of underlying signal? This detection problem is well-studied
in the neuroimaging literature, with previous work showing how to calibrate tests conducted at
peaks to control the FWER
(
21
;
50
;
42
)
or FDR
(
12
;
35
;
10
)
under a null hypothesis that no true signal is present at an observed peak. The statistical foundation of this work is Random Field Theory (RFT)
(
1
)
,
which provides a mathematical framework to quantify the probability
of observing peaks of a certain height, or clusters of a certain size,
under the null. These methods use the Kac-Rice formula

(
34
;
2
)
applied to critical points of centered smooth
Gaussian and related processes (which of course include peaks, i.e. local maxima).

Having using peaks to identify regions likely to contain underlying signal, an obvious next question is
localization
: where exactly is the signal? In this article, we consider this problem in a smooth signal plus constant variance noise
model in which there is a well-defined notion of ground truth: the
local maxima of the signal, which we will call
true
peaks
. While one can imagine different instantiations of the localization task, we will take the perspective that these true peaks are (perhaps implicitly) the targets of a peak inference method. This is the same perspective as adopted by
15
, but they consider a setting where all observed peaks basically correspond to true peaks, and there is no need for selection. We consider the more realistic setting where peaks must be screened to control the number of false discoveries. Concretely, we
look to use only those peaks declared significant by a peak detection method to estimate and construct confidence regions for the
location and height of nearby true peaks.

1.1
Summary of contributions

We propose a straightforward, three-step procedure for peak detection followed by
localization. The first two steps constitute a peak
detection algorithm in the spirit of existing RFT methods
(
10
)
. First, the
random field is thresholded at a preliminary level to identify a
candidate set of interesting peaks. Second, a formal significance
test, calibrated using an asymptotic RFT approximation, is conducted
at each peak larger than this threshold, to control for the probability of false
discovery.

The third and novel step is the localization step: for each
observed peak at which the null is rejected, we produce a confidence ellipse for
the location and a confidence interval for the height of a nearby
true peak. Of course, for these confidence regions to be valid one must account for the fact that inference is conducted only at peaks that pass the detection step. We account for this by using the conditional distribution of peaks given selection to calibrate our inferences, so that this localization step is an example of post-selection inference
(
45
)
,

Our primary contribution
is to provide rigorous theoretical guarantees for each step in this
procedure, and for the procedure as a whole. We work within a smooth signal plus constant variance noise model and derive
our results in an asymptotic regime where the signal’s curvature
around its maxima grows large. Our main results can be summarized as follows:

•

Null discovery control
. The peak detection portion of our method controls the asymptotic probability of falsely discovering a peak at which no signal is present.

•

Consistency of point estimates
. Most peaks at which inference is conducted are either rejected by our peak detection significance test, or in fact asymptotically consistently estimate a true peak.

•

Conditional coverage
. Each confidence region produced
by our method attains asymptotically nominal
coverage, conditional on the event of selection.

•

Marginal coverage
. Finally, we introduce and analyze an overall miscoverage
rate – the expected number of regions that fail to cover the location or height of a nearby true peak, relative to the total number of points at which inference is conducted – and show that our method controls miscoverage
at the nominal level, providing a guarantee on the marginal
performance of our procedure.

Figure
1
illustrates our method, and the notion of overall miscoverage it asymptotically controls, in a cartoon example.

(a)

Field and pre-thresholded peaks.

(b)

Peaks discovered by TG test.

(c)

Confidence regions for location.

(d)

Confidence regions superimposed on the signal, with
\epsilon_{n}
balls around true peaks.

Figure 1
:
Illustration of our method for peak detection and localization. The top left, top right and bottom left panels show Steps 1-3 of our method, respectively. The bottom right panel superimposes confidence ellipsoids for location over the true signal, which is sparse with 3 true peaks. Out of
13
observed peaks (colored grey),
7
survive the initial pre-thresholding step (black), of which
4
are declared significant (red). One of these is a false discovery, in the sense of not being within distance
\varepsilon_{n}
(defined in Section
2.3
) of any true peak. Out of the remaining
3
true discoveries, one of the corresponding confidence ellipsoids fails to cover the nearby true peak. Thus the miscoverage proportion for location – the number of confidence regions that do not cover a true peak, out of the total number of points at which inference is conducted – is
2/7
.

These theoretical guarantees do not require that peaks be detected with asymptotic power one, and so they apply even in the challenging (but realistic) case where a true peak may or may not be discovered from data. In this regime, peaks that are discovered will be subject to selection pressure. For instance, the height of a peak which survives a preliminary thresholding step will be a biased estimate for the value of the signal at that point
(
16
)
. Indeed, our significance test rejects the null only if the height is above some larger cutoff, introducing further bias. We will show that selection also affects the distribuition of the location, though in a more subtle way. Overall, our analysis precisely quantifies the various effects of selection, and our methods carefully construct confidence regions that
account for selection pressure
in order to achieve the desired conditional and marginal (mis)coverage.

In neuroimaging applications effect sizes are often weak and inferential methods derived from limiting approximations may have poor behavior in practice (
15
, see also the discussion at the end of
53
). To address this, previous work on signal detection using RFT has derived corrections to asymptotic distributions of maxima that achieve higher-order accuracy under the null
(
44
;
42
;
9
;
10
)
. In this work, we develop
second-order accurate
approximations to post-selection peak distributions, under a smooth, high-curvature alternative. We demonstrate via simulation that the corrections used to achieve asymptotic second-order accuracy lead to improvements in empirical performance.

Finally, although our theoretical guarantees account for selection pressure, when the probability of selection is small and selection pressure is strong, the post-selection peak inference problem becomes inherently challenging. This leads to a breakdown in coverage for peak location, and very wide intervals for peak height. To address this, we propose
randomized peak inference
, which applies peak thresholding and detection to a randomized version of the data, and then uses the full un-randomized data for post-selection inference. Inspired by data splitting
(
13
;
26
;
25
)
, this randomization ensures that some information is always left for post-selection inference of peaks. We demonstrate empirically that under strong selection pressure, randomization improves coverage for a true peak’s location, and leads to smaller intervals for the true peak’s height.

1.2
Previous work

There is a long line of existing literature on signal
detection using maxima, with tests calibrated via RFT (e.g.
21
;
50
;
51
;
52
;
42
), but this work does not typically consider what happens under the alternative, nearby true peaks. Some notable exceptions include
35
;
10
who consider
FDR analysis of the detection problem described above, and
15
who construct confidence regions for the location of true peaks
in a regime where detection is essentially deterministic. Our work
addresses what lies between these two scenarios:
how should
we localize signals when detection is non-trivial?

We properly handle this intermediate case by conditioning on the event that a peak is detected, thus placing our problem squarely within the modern tradition of post-selection, or conditional, inference (e.g.
29
;
45
;
27
;
47
). Our problem is different from the ones considered in these previous works in a number of ways, but a particularly important difference is that our smooth signal plus noise model is not an exponential family, and as a result, the effect of selection on the distribution of peaks is in certain ways quite different than the selection effect in more well-studied post-selection inference problems.

To elaborate on this point slightly, consider the file drawer problem which served
as a canonical post-selection inference problem in
20
. In the file drawer problem, researchers decide to
report a confidence interval for a population mean
\theta
only if a sample mean
\bar{X}
exceeds some threshold
t
. This problem has a close feel to inference for the height of a true peak
\mu_{t^{*}}
, given that a nearby observed peak
\hat{t}
has height
Y_{\hat{t}}
exceeding a significance cutoff
u
. (This notation will be formally defined in Section
2
.) However there no obvious analogy to inference for peak location
in the file drawer problem. Indeed, we will see that the limiting distribution of the
location is Gaussian, in contrast to the usual Truncated Gaussian distributions
that appear in the file-drawer problem, and (in one form or another) in most previous work on post-selection inference. Even for the height, the distribution of
Y_{\hat{t}}
is only asymptotically Truncated Gaussian, and higher-order asymptotic accuracy requires corrections that have no counterpart in the file drawer problem.

Our approach adapts the well-studied Kac-Rice formula
(Rice 1945; Adler and Taylor 2007)
for the intensity of local
maxima of null smooth Gaussian processes to include the possibility of a growing signal. Concretely, we develop a local expansion of this intensity nearby true peaks, and subsequently use this to obtain a second-order accurate approximation to the conditional distribution of observed peaks. Many connections can be drawn between these results and the classical higher-order theory of maximum likelihood estimation. In particular, our asymptotic expansions will be reminiscent of a number of classical higher-order accurate formulae for the limiting distribution of a maximum likelihood estimator
(
5
;
14
;
6
;
37
;
38
)
. Moreover, the limiting variance of peak location in our problem depends on the observed curvature near the peak, and the difference between this observed curvature and its marginal expectation depends on the strength of the local selection
effect. The relevance of observed curvature suggests connections to
the literature on 2nd order efficiency and statistical curvature
(
18
;
17
)
. We comment on these connections in more detail in Section
3
.

Finally, there have been some recent proposals for signal localization via formal statistical inference that do not target peaks or use asymptotic approximations to peak distributions.
40
;
8
;
7
;
30
use RFT to conduct asymptotic inference for upper-level sets rather than peaks, while
41
;
23
consider the same general problem of signal localization, but propose different methods that are not based on RFT.

1.3
Outline

In Section
2
we propose a method for peak detection and post-selection inference derived using tools from RFT, and summarize the theoretical properties of the method. Sections
3
-
5
contain our main results. In Section
3
we give an asymptotic expansion of the distribution of local maxima in the neighborhood of true peaks. In Section
4
this result is used to establish that local maxima selected by our peak detection tend to successfully localize signal, by consistently estimating a true peak. In Section
5
we use the asymptotic distribution of local maxima to derive confidence regions for the true height and location of a nearby peak. Section
6
proposes the randomized variant of our method, which has better asymptotic coverage for location and narrower confidence intervals for height. Section
7
contains experiments with simulated data, and we conclude with some discussion in Section
8
.

2
Peak detection and post-selection inference via Random Field Theory

We begin with a concrete proposal for peak detection and localization via post-selection inference in a smooth signal plus constant variance noise model. In explaining this proposal we also provide a roadmap for the theory that follows, and give informal statements of our main theoretical results along with pointers to their formal counterparts.

2.1
Signal-plus-noise model

Consider a sequence
(Y_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of random fields, each distributed according to

Y_{n,t}=\mu_{n,t}+\epsilon_{n,t},\quad t\in\mathbb{R}^{d},

(1)

where the signal
\mu_{n}\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, and the noise
\epsilon_{n}
is a Gaussian process with mean zero and covariance kernel
\mathrm{Cov}[\epsilon_{n,t},\epsilon_{n,s}]=K(t,s)
not depending on
n
. We observe the restriction of
Y_{n,t}
to a compact domain
\mathcal{T}_{n}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, which is assumed to have non-empty interior. The
true peaks
of the signal are strict interior local maxima of
\mu_{n}
,

T^{*}_{n}=\{t\in\mathrm{int}(\mathcal{T}_{n}):\nabla\mu_{n,t}=0,\nabla^{2}\mu_{n,t}\prec 0\}.

Our targets of inference are the locations
T_{n}^{*}
and heights
\mu_{T_{n}^{*}}=\{\mu_{n,t^{*}}:t^{*}\in T_{n}^{*}\}
of true peaks. Throughout, we will assume that
K(\cdot,\cdot)
is known and that the field has been standardized, meaning
K(t,t)=1
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. We will also assume that
K(\cdot,\cdot)\in C^{5}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
, which – combined with the smoothness assumptions placed on the signal – implies that the field
Y_{n}
is almost surely
C^{2}(\mathcal{T}_{n})
. Furthermore, to avoid pathological situations we will always assume that

\displaystyle\sigma_{1}^{2}:=\inf_{t\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\lambda_{\min}(\Lambda_{t})>0,\quad\textrm{where}\quad\Lambda_{t}:=\mathrm{Cov}[\nabla\epsilon_{t}],\quad\textrm{and}

(2)

\displaystyle\tau_{2}^{2}:=\inf_{t\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\inf_{x\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\mathrm{Var}[x^{\prime}\nabla^{2}\epsilon_{t}x|\epsilon_{t},\nabla\epsilon_{t}]>0.

As a result
\epsilon_{n}
is almost surely nowhere flat, and so almost surely there are a finite number of observed peaks within
\mathcal{T}_{n}
. Moreover, the Hessian
\nabla^{2}\epsilon_{n,t}
is not a deterministic function of
\epsilon_{n,t},\nabla\epsilon_{n,t}
. Throughout we will drop notational dependence on
n
whenever convenient.

Our theoretical results are asymptotic in nature, and rely on the curvature of
\mu
growing around true peaks
t^{*}\in T^{*}
as
n\to\infty
. One measure of the curvature of
\mu
at a peak
t^{*}\in T^{*}
is the minimum eigenvalue of the negative Hessian
\lambda_{t^{*}}:=\lambda_{\min}(-\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}})
. We denote
\delta_{t^{*}}:=1/\lambda_{t^{*}}
and assume throughout that
\delta_{n}:=\sup_{t^{*}\in T^{*}}\delta_{t^{*}}\to 0
. We allow for the domain
\mathcal{T}_{n}
to grow with
n
, since this is sometimes a natural asymptotic assumption in applications of RFT, but all of our asymptotic results hold even if
\mathcal{T}_{n}
is fixed.

It is worth making several comments on this model and asymptotic setup. Models such as the smooth signal plus noise model are typical in both theory and application of peak inference
(
35
;
10
;
15
)
. For example, in neuroimaging applications it is accepted that neighboring voxels are spatially autocorrelated
(
50
;
22
)
, and that signal is spatially smooth
(
11
)
and moreover additional smoothness is often introduced by a pre-processing step that smooths the data in order to increase SNR
(
50
;
39
)
. When the data are pre-processed by smoothing, the mean function
\mu
– and hence the targets of inference
T^{*}
and
\mu_{T^{*}}
– can depend on the smoothing algorithm. Henceforth we simply accept this, and do not attempt to infer on some underlying ground-truth; for some work that addresses how smoothing affects the type I error of peak detection methods, see
35
.

Model (
1
) restricts the noise distribution to be marginally stationary, since the variance of
\epsilon_{t}
does not depend on
t
. Constant error variance is a common assumption in applications of RFT, where inferential procedures are often applied to standardized or “test statistic” fields
(
50
;
36
;
51
;
35
)
. Most of the calculations that follow can be generalized to allow for non-constant error variance. However the resulting expressions are more complex and many of the implications for peak estimation and inference will change, and so we defer this to future work.

There are two important concrete examples where the high-curvature asymptotic Assumption
5
is satisfied. The first is
i.i.d asymptotics
, in which
Y_{n}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}Y^{(i)}
is
\sqrt{n}
times the sample mean of
n
i.i.d random fields
Y^{(i)}\sim N(\mu^{(1)},C)
defined on a fixed domain
\mathcal{T}
, so that if
\min_{t^{*}\in T^{*}}\lambda_{\min}(-\nabla^{2}\mu^{(1)}_{t^{*}})>c>0
then
\delta_{n}\to 0
at rate
1/\sqrt{n}
. The second is
strong signal asymptotics
, where
\mu_{n}=\sum_{j}a_{j,n}\phi_{t_{j}^{*}}(t)
is modeled as a superposition of unimodal functions, each
\phi_{t_{j}^{*}}(\cdot)\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
being compactly supported with a unique local maximum at
t_{j}^{*}
, and
\inf_{j}a_{j,n}\to\infty

(
35
;
10
)
. In this case
\delta_{n}\to 0
at rate
1/(\inf_{j}a_{j,n})
.

2.2
Peak detection via the Truncated Gaussian test

The first high-level objective of peak inference is
detection
, which can be formalized as a test of the null hypothesis

H_{0,t}:\mu_{s}=0\;\textrm{at all}\;s\in B(t,r)\;\textrm{for some}\;r>0.

35
;
10
propose tests of
H_{0,t}
that use RFT to derive p-values which properly account for selection of peaks
t\in\widehat{T}=\{t\in T:\nabla Y_{t}=0,\nabla^{2}Y_{t}\prec 0\}
. We consider a slight variant of the method of
10
that we call
peak detection via the Truncated Gaussian (TG) test
. Our method first screens peaks via a thresholding step, restricting attention to

\widehat{T}_{v}=\{t\in\mathcal{T}:Y_{t}>v,\nabla Y_{t}=0,\nabla^{2}Y_{t}\prec 0\}.

(3)

Pre-thresholding in this manner is common in neuroimaging analyses
(
11
;
54
;
12
;
10
)
, in part because it helps identify only those signals of practical, rather than purely statistical, significance.

The next step of the method is to perform a hypothesis test at each pre-thresholded peak, rejecting
H_{0,t}
if the value of the field
Y_{t}>u
for some significance threshold
u>v
. This test is calibrated using RFT, which provides an exact formula – given later in (
21
) – for the conditional density of
Y_{t}|t\in\widehat{T}_{v}
under
H_{0,t}
. Asymptotic analysis of this formula shows that as the pre-threshold
v\to\infty
, this conditional distribution approaches a Truncated Gaussian limit with a shifted mean, having asymptotic survival function
1
1

1

More precisely, if
H_{0,t}
is correct then
|\mathbb{P}(Y_{t}>u|t\in\widehat{T_{v}})-\mathbb{S}_{v}(u,d/v)|=O(1/v^{2})
; see Lemma
4
.

\mathbb{S}_{v}(u,d/v):=\frac{\Psi(u-d/v)}{\Psi(v-d/v)}.

(4)

Thus we calibrate the TG test by setting the threshold
u
equal to the
(1-\alpha)
th quantile of this distribution: that is, we set
u=u_{{\rm TG}}(\alpha,v)
where
\mathbb{S}_{v}(u_{{\rm TG}}(\alpha,v),d/v)=\alpha
. Our first result states that this procedure asymptotically controls the
null per-comparison error rate (null-PCER)
2
2

2

Throughout the notation
N(S)
refers to the cardinality of a set
S
.

{\rm PCER}_{0}(u,v):=\frac{\mathbb{E}[N(t\in\widehat{T}_{u}:\textrm{$H_{0,t}$ is true})]}{\mathbb{E}[N(\widehat{T}_{v})]}.

(5)

In words, this is the expected number of peaks at which the null is falsely rejected, over the expected number of peaks at which a hypothesis test is conducted.

Result 1

(Proposition
1
, informal)
.

Fix
\alpha\in(0,1)
and let
v_{n}\to\infty
. Under the regularity conditions of Section
3.1
,

\lim_{n\to\infty}{\rm PCER}_{0}(u_{{\rm TG}}(\alpha,v_{n}),v_{n})\leq\alpha.

In fact the rate of convergence is second-order:
{\rm PCER}_{0}(u_{{\rm TG}}(\alpha,v_{n}),v_{n})\leq\alpha+O(v_{n}^{-2})
.

To be clear,
35
;
10
have already shown that peak detection via RFT
can successfully control type I error in testing
H_{0,t}
, building (in the latter case) on
9
, who study approximations to the normalized overshoot distribution of
Y_{t}|t\in\widehat{T}_{v}
under high-threshold asymptotics. These approximations are both more accurate and more complicated than (
4
).
The novelty in Result
1
comes from recognizing that second-order accuracy can be obtained by applying a simple mean-shift (the
d/v
term in (
4
)) in the Truncated Gaussian reference distribution.

PCER is less stringent than FWER or FDR, which are the other typical criteria used in calibrating peak detection methods. We believe that a suitable choice of significance cutoff
u
would control FWER or FDR, and that this could be shown using analysis similar to that of
35
;
10
, but do not pursue this direction further.

2.3
Peak estimation and rates of convergence

The output of a peak detection method is a set of
discoveries

\widehat{T}_{u}=\{t\in\widehat{T}_{v}:Y_{t}>u\}
. Discoveries can be used to estimate the location of true peaks, most basically by viewing
\widehat{T}_{u}
themselves as point estimates of true peaks. To measure the accuracy of these estimates, we introduce the

\varepsilon
-per-comparison error rate

(
\varepsilon
-PCER):

{\rm PCER}(\varepsilon,u,v):=\frac{\mathbb{E}[N(t\in\widehat{T}_{u}:{\rm dist}(t,T^{*})>\varepsilon)]}{\mathbb{E}[N(\widehat{T}_{v})]}.

(6)

In words, this is the expected number of points
declared significant despite being distance
\varepsilon

from any true peak, over the expected number of points at which inference is conducted. The
\varepsilon
-PCER is a qualitatively different criterion than null-PCER that is stricter when
\varepsilon
is small. Nevertheless, our second result shows that if the reciprocal curvature
\delta_{n}\to 0
, then peak detection via the TG test controls
\varepsilon
-PCER at rate
\varepsilon_{n}=\widetilde{O}(\delta_{n})
.

Result 2

(Theorem
2
, informal)
.

Fix
\alpha\in(0,1)
, let
v_{n}\to\infty
and define
\varepsilon_{n}=\delta_{n}\sqrt{6\sigma_{1}^{2}\log\delta_{n}^{-1}}
. Under the signal-plus-noise model of Section
2.1
with high-curvature asymptotics
\delta_{n}\to 0
, and assuming the regularity conditions of Section
3.1
and
4
,

\lim_{n\to\infty}{\rm PCER}(\varepsilon_{n},u_{{\rm TG}}(\alpha,v_{n}),v_{n})\leq\alpha.

In other words, most peaks selected by pre-thresholding are either rejected by the TG test, or in fact consistently estimate a true peak
t^{*}\in T^{*}
at an
\varepsilon_{n}
-rate of convergence. We will refer to such discoveries as
\varepsilon_{n}
-consistent discoveries
, and say that a true peak
t^{*}
is
\varepsilon_{n}
-consistently discovered
by an observed peak
\hat{t}
(or more colloquially, that
t^{*}
is “nearby”
\hat{t}
) if the distance between them is at most
\varepsilon_{n}
.

2.4
Post-selection inference for height and location

The final objective of a peak inference procedure is to
localize
the signal by producing confidence regions for the location of a nearby true peak, if one happens to exist. To achieve this, we derive the asymptotic distribution of
\hat{t}\in\widehat{T}_{u}
, given that it is the unique
\varepsilon_{n}
-consistent discovery of a particular
t^{*}\in T^{*}
. Since we are also interested in constructing confidence intervals for the height
\mu_{t^{*}}
, we also examine the distribution of
\widehat{Y}:=Y_{\hat{t}}
under the same event. In this analysis, RFT again plays a central role: we calculate these limiting distributions through asymptotic expansion of an exact Kac-Rice formula
(
34
;
2
)
for the peak intensity function.

The informal results stated below rely on some key quantities used throughout. Let
H_{t|y},G_{t|y}
denote the symmetric, positive semi-definite matrices

H_{t|y}:=-\nabla^{2}\mu_{t}+K_{21}(t,t)(\Lambda_{t}^{-1}\nabla\mu_{t})+(y-\mu_{t})\Lambda_{t},\quad G_{t|y}:=(-\nabla^{2}\mu_{t})\Lambda_{t}^{-1}(-\nabla^{2}\mu_{t})+(y-\mu_{t})(-\nabla^{2}\mu_{t})

(7)

with
K_{21}(t,t)
denoting the covariance
3
3

3

For readers familiar with
RFT in the non-stationary setting as in
2
, these terms describes the Levi-Civita
connection of the metric induced by the random field
\epsilon_{t}
. Thus, the first two terms in
H_{t|y}

are nothing but the Riemannian Hessian of
\mu
at
t
. We have decided to work in fixed coordinates
here to ease exposition somewhat.
between
\nabla^{2}Y_{t}
and
\nabla Y_{t}
. Notice that at points
t^{*}\in T^{*}
the gradient
\nabla\mu_{t^{*}}=0
and so
H_{t^{*}|y}=-\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}}+(y-\mu_{t^{*}})\Lambda_{t^{*}}
. Finally, we define
\bar{u}_{t^{*}}:=\max\{u,\mu_{t}^{*}\},\bar{H}_{t^{*}}=H_{t^{*}|\bar{u}_{t^{*}}}
and
\bar{G}_{t^{*}}:=G_{t^{*}|\bar{u}_{t^{*}}}
.

The quantities
u_{t^{*}}
and
\bar{H}_{t^{*}}
have natural interpretations: roughly speaking,
\bar{u}_{t^{*}}
is the asymptotic equivalent of
\widehat{Y}
conditional on
\hat{t}\in\widehat{T}_{u}
consistently discovering
t^{*}
; while
\bar{H}_{t^{*}}
is the asymptotic equivalent of the Hessian
-\nabla^{2}Y_{t}
conditional on the same event. (See Section
11.3
for a more precise claim to this effect.)

Result 3

(Theorem
3
, informal)
.

Under the smooth signal-plus-noise model of Section
2.1
with high-curvature asymptotics
\delta_{n}\to 0
, and the regularity conditions of Section
3.1
, given that
\hat{t}\in\widehat{T}_{u}
is the unique
\varepsilon_{n}
-consistent discovery of
t^{*}\in T^{*}
, the following approximations hold as
n\to\infty
:

•

The conditional density of the height
\widehat{Y}
is about

p(y)\approx\mathbf{1}(y>u)\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\frac{\exp\big(-\frac{1}{2}(y-\mu_{t^{*}}-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}(\bar{H}_{t^{*}}^{-1}\Lambda_{t^{*}}))^{2}\big)}{\Psi\big(u-\mu_{t^{*}}-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}(\bar{H}_{t^{*}}^{-1}\Lambda_{t^{*}})\big)}.

•

The conditional density of the location
\hat{t}
given
\widehat{Y}=y
is about

p(t|y)\approx\frac{\sqrt{\det(G_{t^{*}|y})}}{\sqrt{(2\pi)^{d}}}\cdot\exp\Big(-\frac{1}{2}(t-t)^{\prime}G_{t^{*}|y}(t-t^{*})\Big).

The densities
p(y)
and
p(t|y)
are examples of post-selection distributions, since they condition (among other things) on the event that a peak
\hat{t}\in\widehat{T}
nearby
t^{*}
is also a discovery, i.e. that it has height
\widehat{Y}>u
. In each case, the approximations are accurate up to second-order relative error. We defer a more detailed interpretation of these post-selection distributions to Section
3.4
, where we compare them to the marginal distribution of the height and location of a peak
\hat{t}\in\widehat{T}
nearby
t^{*}
, which does not condition on selection. This comparison reveals several effects of selection, some of which (to the best of our knowledge) have not previously been observed in work on post-selection inference.

On a more practical level, we can construct asymptotically pivotal quantites and valid confidence regions by taking the limiting distributions in Result
3
and plugging in estimates for nuisance parameters. Concretely, to infer the height
\mu_{t^{*}}
we propose to use the
Truncated Gaussian (TG)
pivot

\widehat{\mathbb{S}}_{\mu_{t^{*}}}(\widehat{Y}):=\frac{\Psi(\widehat{Y}-\mu_{t^{*}}-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}(\widehat{H}^{-1}\Lambda_{\hat{t}}))}{\Psi(u-\mu_{t^{*}}-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}(\widehat{H}^{-1}\Lambda_{\hat{t}}))},

(8)

and to infer the location
t^{*}
we propose to use the (studentized)
Wald
pivot

\widehat{W}_{t^{*}}(\hat{t})=(\hat{t}-t^{*})^{\prime}\widehat{H}\Lambda_{\hat{t}}^{-1}\widehat{H}(\hat{t}-t^{*}),

(9)

where we have written
\widehat{H}=-\nabla^{2}Y_{\hat{t}}
for the Hessian of the field at
\hat{t}
. The TG pivot is asymptotically pivotal, and the Wald pivot is also asymptotically pivotal provided that
u-\mu_{t^{*}}
– which controls the probability of discovery – not be too large.

Result 4

(Theorem
4
, informal)
.

Under the conditions of Result
3
,

\widehat{\mathbb{S}}_{\mu}(\widehat{Y})\overset{d}{\to}{\rm Unif}(0,1).

If additionally
(u-\mu_{t^{*}})_{+}\delta_{n}\to 0
, then

\widehat{W}_{t^{*}}(\hat{t})\overset{d}{\to}\chi_{d}^{2}.

As an immediate consequence, the confidence regions

I_{\hat{t}}:=\big\{\mu\in\mathbb{R}:\frac{\alpha}{2}\leq\widehat{\mathbb{S}}_{\mu}(\widehat{Y})\leq 1-\frac{\alpha}{2}\big\},\quad C_{\hat{t}}:=\big\{t\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\widehat{W}_{t}(\hat{t})\leq q_{\chi_{d}^{2}}(1-\alpha)\big\},

(10)

have asymptotically nominal conditional coverage: given that
\hat{t}
is the unique
\varepsilon_{n}
-consistent discovery of
t^{*}
,
C_{\hat{t}}
will contain
t^{*}
, and
I_{\hat{t}}
will contain
\mu_{t^{*}}
, each with probability approaching
(1-\alpha)
. Notice that achieving conditional coverage is conceptually distinct from controlling either null- or
\varepsilon
-PCER, and that conditional coverage can be achieved even when
u\neq u_{{\rm TG}}
.

2.5
Overall coverage guarantees

In addition to providing conditional coverage, our overall method for peak detection and localization by post-selection inference – summarized for the reader’s convenience in Algorithm
1
– also controls a marginal notion of miscoverage, which we call the
per-comparison miscoverage rate
(PCMR). To define PCMR let
t^{*}(t):=\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{t^{*}\in T^{*}}\|t-t^{*}\|
denote the true peak closest to
t\in\mathcal{T}
: then the PCMR for location and height respectively is

{\rm PCMR}_{T^{*}}(u,v):=\frac{\mathbb{E}[N(t\in\widehat{T}_{u}:t^{*}(t)\not\in C_{t})]}{\mathbb{E}[N(\widehat{T}_{v})]},\quad\textrm{and}\quad{\rm PCMR}_{\mu_{T^{*}}}(u,v):=\frac{\mathbb{E}[N(t\in\widehat{T}_{u}:\mu_{t^{*}(t)}\not\in I_{t})]}{\mathbb{E}[N(\widehat{T}_{v})]}.

(11)

In other words,
{\rm PCMR}_{T^{*}}
is the expected number of confidence regions which fail to contain the location of a nearby true peak, over the expected number of points at which inference is conducte, and an analogous interpretation holds for
{\rm PCMR}_{\mu_{T^{*}}}(u,v)
.

Result 5

(Theorem
5
, informal)
.

Under the conditions of Result
2
,

\lim_{n\to\infty}{\rm PCMR}_{\mu_{T^{*}}}(u_{{\rm TG}}(\alpha,v_{n}),v_{n})\leq\alpha.

(12)

If additionally
\sup_{t^{*}\in T^{*}}(u-\mu_{t^{*}})_{+}\delta_{n}\to 0
, then

\lim_{n\to\infty}{\rm PCMR}_{{T^{*}}}(u_{{\rm TG}}(\alpha,v_{n}),v_{n})\leq\alpha.

(13)

These overall bounds on marginal miscoverage are implied by the various results described above. The logic is simple. Among null selections, only an
100\alpha\%
(in expectation) will be declared significant. Among
\varepsilon_{n}
-consistent discoveries, only a
100\alpha\%
(in expectation) will produce confidence regions that do not cover the truth. And under suitable regularity conditions the contribution of all other discoveries is negligible. Thus, the overall PCMR is at most
\alpha
.

Algorithm 1

Peak detection and localization via post-selection inference

0:

Field
Y
, gradient covariance
\mathrm{Cov}[\nabla\epsilon_{t}]
, pre-threshold
v\in\mathbb{R}
, significance level
\alpha\in(0,1)
.

1:

Select peaks
\widehat{T}_{v}
by pre-thresholding.

2:

At each peak
t\in\widehat{T}_{v}
, test the null hypothesis
H_{0,t}
using the TG test, making discoveries
\widehat{T}_{u_{{\rm TG}}(\alpha,v)}
.

3:

For each significant peak
\hat{t}\in\widehat{T}_{u_{{\rm TG}}(\alpha,v)}
, return a confidence interval
I_{{\hat{t}}}
for the height, and a confidence ellipsoid
C_{\hat{t}}
for the location, of a nearby true peak.

3
Local expansion of intensity via the Kac-Rice formula

Our various criteria for error are stated in terms of expectations involving the point process

N_{\mathcal{I}}(\mathcal{B})=N(t\in\widehat{T}\cap\mathcal{B}:Y_{t}\in\mathcal{I}),

which counts the number of local maxima in
\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathcal{T}
with height in
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathbb{R}
. The main result of this section, Theorem
1
, establishes an approximation
\bar{\rho}(t,y)
of the intensity
\rho(t,y)
of this point process that is locally accurate around true peaks
t^{*}\in T^{*}
up to second-order relative error. This approximation is subsequently used to derive both the asymptotics of
\varepsilon_{n}
-PCER (in Section
4
), and the asymptotic post-selection distributions summarized in Result
3
(in Section
5
). Thus our guarantees on consistent peak estimation, and conditional and marginal asymptotic miscoverage, all ultimately flow from Theorem
1
.

3.1
Preliminaries

Regularity conditions and asymptotics.

In addition to the basic signal plus noise model of Section
2.1
, we will work under certain regularity conditions. These conditions assert that the signal is smooth and well-conditioned in some fixed radius ball around each true peak, and that all peaks belong strictly to the interior of
\mathcal{T}
.

(A1)

Define
\mathcal{T}_{r}^{*}:=\bigcup_{t^{*}\in T^{*}}B_{d}(t^{*},r)
. There exists a constant
c_{1}>0
such that the mean
\mu\in C^{4}(\mathcal{T}_{c_{1}}^{*})
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

(A2)

The covariance
K(\cdot,\cdot)\in C^{5}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Moreover, there exists a constant
C_{1}<\infty
such that
\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\|K(t,\cdot)\|_{C_{5}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}<C_{1}
.

(A3)

There exists a constant
c_{2}>0
and a constant
C_{2}<\infty
such that for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

\sup_{t^{*}\in T^{*}}\lambda_{\max}(-\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}})\cdot\delta_{t^{*}}\leq C_{2},\quad\sup_{t^{*}\in T^{*}}\sup_{t\in B(t^{*},c_{2})}(\|\nabla^{3}\mu_{t}\|,\|\nabla^{4}\mu_{t}\|)\cdot\delta_{t^{*}}\leq C_{2}.

(14)

(A4)

There exists a constant
c_{3}>0
such that
{\rm dist}(T^{\ast},\partial\mathcal{T})>c_{3}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

Additionally, as a reminder, our local expansions will be asymptotically accurate as the curvature of
\mu
grows around peaks
t^{*}
.

(A5)

Let
\delta_{n}:=\sup_{t^{*}\in T^{*}}\delta_{t^{*}}
. Assume
\delta_{n}\to 0
.

Assumptions
1
-
5

are comparable to classical assumptions made in the asymptotic
analysis of M-estimators
(
48
)
, but they allow
for the signal to have multiple peaks and for the domain

\mathcal{T}
to change with
n
. The amount of smoothness assumed
on
\mu
and
C
in Assumptions
1

and
2
is used to obtain
second-order accuracy. Assumption
3

ensures that
\mu
is well-conditioned in a neighborhood of
each peak. Note that an implication of
Assumption
3
is that all true peaks
are well-separated, meaning there exists some constant
c>0
such that
\inf_{t^{*}\neq s^{*}:t^{*},s^{*}\in T^{*}}\|t^{*}-s^{*}\|>c
.

Finally, in order to more concisely state our upper bound on the relative error between
\bar{\rho}(t,y)
and
\rho(t,y)
, we make an additional technical assumption.

(A6)

There exists a constant
C_{3}<\infty
such that for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

\sup_{t^{*}\in T^{*}}\lambda_{\max}\big(\bar{H}_{t^{*}}\big)\delta_{n}\leq C_{3}.

(15)

This assumption is made strictly for ease of exposition, since it means that the single asymptotic parameter
\delta_{n}
will ultimately govern asymptotic error. Note that (
15
) is violated if
\sup_{t^{*}\in T^{*}}(u-\mu_{t^{*}})\delta_{n}\to\infty
i.e. if the threshold is too large relative to the signal; however the TG test would be extremely unlikely to detect such a peak in the first place. We emphasize that all subsequent local expansions are correct even if Assumption
6
does not hold, the upper bounds on error simply become more complicated.

Notation.

It will be useful to introduce some notation to more compactly write the local expansion
\bar{\rho}(t,y)
. This local expansion will depend in part on higher-order derivatives of
K(s,t)
. These are encoded in the tensors
K_{ij}(s,t):=\mathrm{Cov}[\nabla^{i}\epsilon_{s},\nabla^{j}\epsilon_{t}]
, which (written in Euclidean coordinates) are
(i+j)
-arrays, having entries
[K_{ij}(s,t)]_{ab}=\mathrm{Cov}[D^{a}Y_{s},D^{b}Y_{t}]
for multi-indices
a,b
. We use the following compact notation for array-vector composition: if
A
is a
k
-array and
v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a vector, then
A(v)
will denote the
(k-1)
-array with entries

A(v)_{a_{1},\ldots,a_{k-1}}=\sum_{j=1}^{d}A_{a_{1},\ldots,a_{k-1},j}v_{j},

Additionally,
A(v,v)=(A(v))(v)
,
A(v,v,v)=((A(v))(v))(v)
, and so forth. We let
\dot{A}_{t}
denote the derivative of an array-valued mapping
A_{t}:\mathcal{T}\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times d\times\cdots\times d}
, which has entries
[\dot{A}_{t}]_{i_{1}i_{2}\dots i_{k}m}=[D^{m}A_{t}]_{i_{1}i_{2}\dots i_{k}}
.

We will use
C
to represent a constant that may change from line to line, and that may depend on the covariance
K(t,s)
and the dimension
d
(and sometimes, on the level
\alpha
used in our inferential procedures), but that does not depend on
n
. We will use the phrase “for all
n
sufficiently large” to mean “for all
n\geq C
where
C
is constant that may depend on
K(t,s),d
and
\alpha
.” Finally, for convenience we will let
c_{0}=\min\{c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}\}
and
C_{0}=\max\{C_{1},C_{2},C_{3},C_{4}\}
.

3.2
Kac-Rice formula

In developing an approximation to the intensity of
N_{u}(\mathcal{B})
, our starting place will be the Kac-Rice theorem as stated in
2
, which is valid under the smoothness conditions of Section
2.1
and gives the exact formula

\mathbb{E}[N_{u}(\mathcal{B})]=\int_{\mathcal{B}}\mathbb{E}[\det(-\nabla^{2}Y_{t})\cdot\mathbf{1}(Y_{t}>u)\cdot\mathbf{1}(\nabla^{2}Y_{t}\prec 0)|\nabla Y_{t}=0,Y_{t}=y]\cdot f_{\nabla Y_{t}}(0)\,dt.

(16)

Here we have adopted the notational shorthand
N_{u}(\mathcal{B})=N_{(u,\infty)}(\mathcal{B})
, and write
f_{\nabla Y_{t}}(\cdot)
for the Gaussian density of
\nabla Y_{t}
. Since we are interested in the joint distribution of location and height, it is useful to rewrite this as
\mathbb{E}[N_{u}(\mathcal{B})]=\int_{\mathcal{B}}\int_{u}^{\infty}\rho(t,y)\,dy\,dt
where the intensity function

\displaystyle\rho(t,y)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[\det(-\nabla^{2}Y_{t})\cdot\mathbf{1}(\nabla^{2}Y_{t}\prec 0)|\nabla Y_{t}=0,Y_{t}=y]\cdot f_{\nabla Y_{t},Y_{t}}(0,y)

(17)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[\det(-\nabla^{2}Y_{t})\cdot\mathbf{1}(\nabla^{2}Y_{t}\prec 0)|\nabla Y_{t}=0,Y_{t}=y]\cdot f_{\nabla Y_{t}}(0)\cdot f_{Y_{t}}(y),

and in the second line we have used the assumption that
\epsilon_{t}
has constant variance – and thus
\mathrm{Cov}[\nabla\epsilon_{t},\epsilon_{t}]=0
and
\nabla\epsilon_{t}
and
\epsilon_{t}
are independent – to factorize
f_{\nabla Y_{t},Y_{t}}(0,y)=f_{\nabla Y_{t}}(0)\cdot f_{Y_{t}}(y)
.

3.3
Local expansion of intensity

Our approximation
\bar{\rho}(t,y)
to
\rho(t,y)
is a second-order accurate local expansion of (
17
) around a true peak
t=t^{*}
, and around the limiting height
y=\bar{u}_{t^{*}}
.To define the distance at which this expansion is valid, we introduce the notation

\varepsilon_{n}=\delta_{n}\sqrt{6\sigma_{1}^{2}\log(\lambda_{n})},\;\Delta_{n}:=\sqrt{6\log(\lambda_{n})},

(18)

where we recall
\lambda_{n}=1/\delta_{n}
. (The choice of constant
6
in the definitions of
\varepsilon_{n}
and
\Delta_{n}
is arbitrary, and the results should continue to hold for any constant
>4
.) The first-order terms in the local expansion are

\displaystyle T_{01}^{\rho}(y)

\displaystyle:=T_{01}^{\det}(y)=(y-\bar{u}_{t^{*}})\mathrm{tr}\big(\bar{H}_{t^{*}}^{-1}\Lambda_{t^{*}})

(19)

\displaystyle T_{10}^{\rho}(h)

\displaystyle:=T_{10}^{\det}(h)+T_{10}^{\nabla Y}(h)=\mathrm{tr}\big(\bar{H}_{t^{*}}^{-1}\dot{H}_{t^{*}|\bar{u}_{t^{*}}}(h)\big)-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}\big(\Lambda_{t^{*}}^{-1}\dot{\Lambda}_{t^{*}}(h)\big)

\displaystyle T_{30}^{\rho}(h)

\displaystyle:=T_{30}^{\nabla Y}(h)=h^{\prime}\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}}\Lambda_{t^{*}}^{-1}\{\nabla^{3}\mu_{t^{*}}(h,h)\}-h^{\prime}\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}}\Lambda_{t^{*}}^{-1}\{\dot{\Lambda}_{t^{*}}(\Lambda_{t^{*}}^{-1}h)\}h

\displaystyle T_{21}^{\rho}(h,y)

\displaystyle:=T_{21}^{Y}(h,y)=\frac{1}{2}(y-\bar{u}_{t^{*}})h^{\prime}\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}}h,

where
\dot{H}_{t^{*}|\bar{u}_{t^{*}}}(h)=-\nabla^{3}\mu_{t^{*}}(h)+(\bar{u}-\mu_{t^{*}})\dot{\Lambda}_{t^{*}}(h)-\Gamma_{t^{*}}(\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}}(h))
denotes the derivative of the mapping
t\mapsto H_{t|\bar{u}_{t^{*}}}
evaluated at
t=t^{*}
. The multi-index notation in (
19
) suggests terms in a Taylor expansion, while the superscripts indicate correspondence to terms in (
16
). The relative error incurred by the approximation is determined by

\displaystyle\mathrm{Err}_{Y}(h,y)

\displaystyle:=C\Big(\big(|y-\bar{u}_{t^{*}}|+|\bar{u}_{t^{*}}-\mu_{t^{*}}|\big)\lambda_{n}\|h\|^{3}+\lambda_{n}^{2}\|h\|^{4}+|y-\bar{u}_{t^{*}}|^{2}\lambda_{n}^{2}\|h\|^{4}\Big)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Err}_{\det}(h,y)

\displaystyle:=C\Big(\big(|y-\bar{u}_{t^{*}}|^{2}+1\big)\delta_{n}^{2}+\|h\|^{2}\Big)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Err}_{\nabla Y}(h)

\displaystyle:=C\Big(\|h\|^{2}+\|h\|^{4}\lambda_{n}^{2}\Big).

Theorem 1
.

Under Assumptions
1
-
6
, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
sufficiently large the following statement holds: at all
t^{*}\in T^{*}
,
h\in B_{d}(0,\varepsilon_{n}),y\in\bar{u}_{t^{*}}\pm\Delta_{n}
,

\displaystyle\frac{\Big|\rho(t^{*}+h,y)-\bar{\rho}(t^{*}+h,y)\Big|}{\bar{\rho}(t^{*}+h,y)}\leq C\Big(\mathrm{Err}_{\det}(h,y)+\mathrm{Err}_{Y}(h,y)+\mathrm{Err}_{\nabla Y}(h)\Big):=\mathrm{Err}_{\rho}(h,y).

where

\displaystyle\bar{\rho}(t^{*}+h,y)

\displaystyle:=

(20)

\displaystyle\mathbf{1}(y>u)

\displaystyle\cdot\frac{\det(\bar{H}_{t^{*}})\big(1+T_{01}^{\rho}(y)+T_{10}^{\rho}(h)-\frac{1}{2}T_{30}^{\rho}(h)+T_{21}^{\rho}(h,y)\big)}{\sqrt{(2\pi)^{d+1}\det(\Lambda_{t^{*}})}}\exp\Big(-\frac{(y-\mu_{t^{*}})^{2}}{2}\Big)\cdot\exp\bigg(-\frac{h^{\prime}\bar{G}_{t^{*}}h}{2}\bigg).

Theorem
1
is proved in Section
9
. Roughly speaking, the approximation
\bar{\rho}(t,y)
is derived by local expansion of each of the three terms in (
16
) – the expectation of the Hessian determinant, the gradient density
f_{\nabla Y_{t}}(0)
and the height density
f_{Y_{t}}(y)
– about
t=t^{*},y=\bar{u}_{t^{*}}
, followed by a careful analysis of the relative magnitude of all terms in the resulting expansions. Several remarks are in order.

Remark 1
.

The terms in (
19
) are labeled “first-order” because they are each
\widetilde{O}(\delta_{n})
, whereas the approximation is accurate up to second-order relative error, meaning
4
4

4

A more careful analysis removes the
\log(\lambda_{n})
factor, see Theorem
3
.

\sup_{h\in B_{d}(0,\varepsilon_{n}),y\in\bar{u}_{t^{*}}\pm\Delta_{n}}\mathrm{Err}_{\rho}(h,y)=\widetilde{O}\Big(\delta_{n}^{2}+(\bar{u}_{t^{*}}-\mu_{t^{*}})\delta_{n}^{2}\Big).

More precisely, under negligible or moderate selection pressure (meaning
u\ll\mu_{t^{*}}
or
u=\mu_{t^{*}}+O(1)
respectively) the error is
\widetilde{O}(\delta_{n}^{2})
and the approximation is second-order accurate; under strong selection pressure (
u\gg\mu_{t^{*}}
but
u/\mu_{t^{*}}\to 1
) the error is
\widetilde{O}((\bar{u}_{t^{*}}-\mu_{t^{*}})\delta_{n}^{2})
and the approximation is between first- and second-order accurate; and under very strong selection pressure (
u/\mu_{t^{*}}\to C>1
) the error is in fact also
\widetilde{O}(\delta_{n})
. In a slight abuse of terminology we will sometimes simply summarize these different cases by saying that
\bar{\rho}(t,y)
is “second-order accurate.”

Remark 2
.

There exist many second-order accurate approximations to the distribution of a maximum likelihood estimator, either unconditionally or conditional on an ancillary statistic (see
(
5
;
14
;
6
;
37
;
38
)
among many others). To a certain extent
\bar{\rho}(t,y)
resembles some of these approximations, though it is not a direct consequence of any of them. Some distinctive features of
\bar{\rho}(t,y)
are: (i) it is correct even when
Y
is not a well-specified likelihood; (ii) it approximates the intensity of local maxima of height at least
u
, and thus accounts for selection and the possibility of multiple peaks; (iii) it is accurate up to second-order relative error, and valid even when
u=C\mu_{t^{*}}
for
C>1
which corresponds to a large-deviations event, (iv) it is derived via expansion of a Kac-Rice formula. As far as we know, the Kac-Rice formula has not seen much use in higher-order parametric statistical theory, with the notable exception of
(
38
)
who rederives the Kac-Rice formula and elucidates a connection to Barndorff-Nielsen’s
p^{*}
-formula
(
6
)
. Indeed we believe that many classical results in higher-order parametric statistics could be recovered from the Kac-Rice perspective, but this is out of the scope of the present work.

Remark 3
.

Inspection of the proof of Theorem
1
shows that the leading-order asymptotic behavior of
\rho(t,y)
is entirely determined by the density term
f_{\nabla Y_{t}}(0)\cdot f_{Y_{t}}(y)
in (
16
). This is because under high-curvature asymptotics the observed Hessian
-\nabla^{2}Y_{t}
is close to deterministic, and the “determinant term”
\mathbb{E}[(\det(-\nabla^{2}Y_{t}))\cdot\mathbf{1}(\nabla^{2}Y_{t}\prec 0)|Y_{t}=y,\nabla Y_{t}=0]
is close to
\det(\bar{H}_{t^{*}})
. The fluctuations of the Hessian about its deterministic limit contribute to
\bar{\rho}(t,y)
only through
T_{01}^{\rho}(y)=T_{01}^{\det}(y)
, which is the first-order term in a Taylor expansion of the determinant term about
\det(\bar{H}_{t^{*}})
.

3.4
Distributional effect of selection: insights and connections

Looking ahead, later in Section
5
we will show that up to a constant of proportionality
\bar{\rho}(t,y)
gives the asymptotic post-selection
density
of the height and location of a peak nearby
t^{*}
. This conditional density is defined on the event that (i) there is a unique
\hat{t}\in\widehat{T}
within distance
\varepsilon_{n}
of
t^{*}
, (ii) the peak
\hat{t}
is discovered, meaning
\widehat{Y}
survives the threshold
u
, and (iii)
\widehat{Y}\in[\bar{u}_{t^{*}}-\Delta_{n},\bar{u}_{t^{*}}+\Delta_{n}]
. It is instructive to compare this to the density conditional only (i); indeed with probability tending to one there is a unique
\hat{t}\in\widehat{T}
within distance
\varepsilon_{n}
of
t^{*}
with, so that it is only very slightly incorrect to think of this as the asymptotic
marginal density
of
(\hat{t},\widehat{Y})
. We now make a detailed comparison between conditional and marginal densities, revealing various effects of selection on the distribution of
(\hat{t},\widehat{Y})
, and drawing connections where possible to related previous work on RFT and conditional inference.

Leading-order effect of selection: truncation and Goldilocks precision.

By ignoring the first-order terms in (
20
), we obtain a simpler approximation to the intensity that is only first-order accurate, but nonetheless asymptotically valid, and which, when truncated to
y\in(u,\infty)
reads

\rho(t^{*}+h,y)\mathbf{1}(y>u)\propto(1+\widetilde{O}(\delta_{n}))\cdot\mathbf{1}(y>u)\exp\Big(-\frac{(y-\mu_{t^{*}})^{2}}{2}\Big)\cdot\exp\bigg(-\frac{h^{\prime}G_{t^{*}|\bar{u}_{t^{*}}}h}{2}\bigg).

The aforementioned marginal density is obtained by setting
u=-\infty
in the above expression, while taking
u>-\infty
gives the density that conditions on selection. We draw the following conclusions.

•

To a leading order
\widehat{Y}
and
\hat{t}
are independent, both marginally and conditionally on selection.

•

The leading-order effect of selection on the distribution of
\widehat{Y}
is to truncate its limiting Gaussian distribution to
(u,\infty)
. This is intuitive and will be familiar to readers with a background in post-selection inference, as it is exactly analogous to the selection effect in the “file drawer” problem of
20
.

•

The leading-order effect of selection on the distribution of the location
\hat{t}
is different: conditioning on selection preserves asymptotic Normality and unbiasedness of
\hat{t}
but changes its asymptotic precision, from

(-\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}})\Lambda_{t^{*}}^{-1}(-\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}})\quad\textrm{to}\quad\bar{G}_{t^{*}}=G_{t^{*}|\bar{u}_{t^{*}}}=(-\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}})\Lambda_{t^{*}}^{-1}(-\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}})+(\bar{u}_{t^{*}}-\mu_{t^{*}})(-\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}}).

In particular we can see that conditioning on selection has the (surprising and welcome) effect of
increasing
the precision with which
\hat{t}
estimates
t^{*}
. Intuitively, this is because selection biases the observed Hessian
\widehat{H}
upwards, to be nearer to
\bar{H}_{t^{*}}=\mathbb{E}[-\nabla^{2}Y_{t^{*}}|Y_{t^{*}}=\bar{u}_{t^{*}}]
than to its marginal limit
-\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}}
, and this upwards bias in curvature increases asymptotic precision.

Less happily, the bias in
\widehat{H}
also poses challenges for inference. When selection is guaranteed, the asymptotic precision of the location is
(-\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}})\Lambda_{t^{*}}^{-1}(-\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}})
(as shown originally in
4
;
15
), and moreover this is consistently estimated by the sandwich estimator
\widehat{H}\Lambda_{\hat{t}}^{-1}\widehat{H}
. However, under selection pressure this sandwich estimator has the wrong asymptotic limit:
\bar{H}_{t^{*}}\Lambda_{t^{*}}^{-1}\bar{H}_{t^{*}}
rather than
\bar{G}_{t^{*}}
. We refer to the correct asymptotic precision
\bar{G}_{t^{*}}
as the “Goldilocks” precision, since routine algebra establishes

\bar{G}_{t^{*}}=\bar{H}_{t^{*}}\Lambda_{t^{*}}^{-1}(-\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}}),

revealing that
\bar{G}_{t^{*}}
is a perfect compromise between the two incorrect sandwich forms of precision, i.e.

(-\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}})\Lambda_{t^{*}}^{-1}(-\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}})\preceq\bar{G}_{t^{*}}\preceq\bar{H}_{t^{*}}\Lambda_{t^{*}}^{-1}\bar{H}_{t^{*}}.

Remark 4
.

Conditional distributions where conditioning preserves asymptotic Normality and unbiasedness, but changes the limiting precision, are unusual in the study of either post-selection conditional inference or RFT. However, there are close parallels between these phenomena and those seen in a distinct conditional inference problem: maximum likelihood inference in curved exponential families
(
18
)
. In this latter context, a classical line of work
(
19
;
17
)
recommends conditioning on an (approximately) ancillary statistic, and shows that while the MLE is conditionally asymptotically Normal and unbiased, the expected Fisher information is not the right measure of conditional precision. We condition on
\widehat{Y}>u
for an entirely different purpose, but the effect is similar: after conditioning, the expected Hessian
-\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}}
alone does not determine the precision of
\hat{t}
.

First-order effect of selection: mean-shift and conditional Goldilocks.

We now turn to interpreting the first-order terms in (
19
).

•

Both
T_{10}^{\rho}(h)
and
T_{30}^{\rho}(h)
are first-order corrections to the distribution of
\hat{t}
. While these terms must be accounted for to produce a second-order accurate approximation to the distribution of
\hat{t}
, a fortunate symmetry means these terms have no effect on the asymptotic distribution of the Wald pivot
\widehat{W}_{t^{*}}(\hat{t})
, and thus, no effect on the accuracy of our asymptotic inferences.

•

The term
T_{10}^{\rho}(y)=T_{10}^{\det}(y)
is a first-order correction to the distribution of
\widehat{Y}
, that originates from the determinant term in (
16
), and which roughly speaking reflects upwards bias due to evaluating at a local maximum. This kind of bias will be familiar to readers familiar with RFT, since it is present even under the null
\mu\equiv 0
. This bias manifests itself in a mean shift of size
\mathrm{tr}(\bar{H}_{t^{*}}^{-1}\Lambda_{t^{*}})
under both null and alternative (with
\bar{H}_{t^{*}}=v\Lambda_{t^{*}}
under the null when thresholding at
v
).

•

Finally, the term
T_{21}^{\rho}(h,y)=T_{21}^{Y}(h,y)
term is a first-order correction to the distribution of both
\hat{t}
and
\widehat{Y}
that originates from the height density
f_{Y_{t}}(y)
in (
16
). Roughly speaking, it reflects downwards bias in
\widehat{Y}
due to evaluating
Y
at a point
\hat{t}\neq t^{*}
where
\mu_{\hat{t}}<\mu_{t^{*}}
. This kind of bias is not present under the null
\mu\equiv 0
, and manifests as an additional shift in the mean of
Y_{\hat{t}}
near points
t^{*}\in T^{*}
. It also reflects a change in the conditional variance of
\hat{t}|\widehat{Y}
, which can be positive or negative depending on whether
\widehat{Y}>\bar{u}_{t^{*}}
or
\widehat{Y}<\bar{u}_{t^{*}}
.

In obtaining local expansions to the post-selection densities
p(y)
and
p(t|y)
we show precisely that
at
t^{*}\in T^{*}
the total effect of these first-order terms is to shift the (pre-selection) mean of
\widehat{Y}
from

\mu_{t^{*}}\quad\textrm{to}\quad\mu_{t^{*}}+\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}(\bar{H}_{t^{*}}^{-1}\Lambda_{t^{*}})

and to change the conditional precision of
\hat{t}|\widehat{Y}=y
from

\bar{G}_{t^{*}}\quad\textrm{to}\quad G_{t^{*}|y}=\bar{G}_{t^{*}}+(y-\bar{u}_{t^{*}})(-\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}}).

The change in the conditional precision of
\hat{t}|\widehat{Y}=y
reflects the fact that
\hat{t}
and
\widehat{Y}
are not second-order independent, due to the
T_{21}^{\rho}(h,y)
term.

4
Peak detection and estimation

In this section, we combine the results of Section
3
with a more classical RFT analysis under the null
H_{0,t}
to show that the peak detection part of our method – i.e application of the TG test to peaks selected by pre-thresholding – controls
\varepsilon_{n}
-PCER. We copy the definition of
\varepsilon
-PCER here for convenience:

{\rm PCER}(\varepsilon,u,v)=\frac{\mathbb{E}[N(t\in\widehat{T}_{u}:{\rm dist}(t,T^{*})>\varepsilon)]}{\mathbb{E}[N(\widehat{T}_{v})]}.

To prove control of
\varepsilon_{n}
-PCER, we first show that the null distribution of
Y_{t}|t\in\widehat{Y}_{v}
is asymptotically Truncated Gaussian, and so the TG test controls the expected number of null discoveries. On the other hand, the local expansion
\bar{\rho}(t,y)
of Section
3
can be used to derive a lower bound on the expected number of
\varepsilon_{n}
-consistent discoveries. Finally, under the following assumptions the expected number of all other discoveries – peaks that are neither null nor
\varepsilon_{n}
-consistent – is negligible in comparison.

(A7)

Define the
null region

\mathcal{T}_{0}=\{t:\exists r>0\;\textrm{such that}\;\mu_{s}=0\;\forall s\in B_{d}(t,r)\}
, and let

g_{n}(\varepsilon):=\inf_{t\in\mathcal{T}:d(t,T^{*})>\varepsilon,\;d(t,\mathcal{T}_{0})>0}\|\nabla\mu_{t}\|.

Assume that for any fixed
\varepsilon>0
,
g_{n}(\varepsilon)\to\infty
,
\log\lambda_{n}/\{g_{n}(\varepsilon)\}^{2}\to 0
, and
\log|\mathcal{T}_{n}|/\{g_{n}(\varepsilon)\}^{2}\to 0
.

(A8)

There exists a constant
C_{4}
such that
\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T}}\lambda_{\max}(-\nabla^{2}\mu_{t})\leq C_{4}\lambda_{n}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

(A9)

The global maximum of
\mu
is achieved at an interior critical point:
\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T}}\mu_{t}=\max_{t^{*}\in T^{*}}\mu_{t^{*}}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

Theorem 2
.

Fix
\alpha\in(0,1)
, and suppose
v_{n}\to\infty
. Under Assumptions
1
-
9
, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
sufficiently large,

{\rm PCER}(\varepsilon_{n},u_{{\rm TG}}(\alpha,v_{n}),v_{n})\leq\alpha+C\Big(\frac{1}{v_{n}^{2}}+\delta_{n}^{2}+\sup_{t^{*}\in T^{*}}(\bar{u}_{t^{*}}-\mu_{t^{*}})\delta_{n}^{2}\Big).

To make the argument outlined above rigorous, we partition
\mathcal{T}
into three regions:

1.

the
null region

\mathcal{T}_{0}
defined in Assumption
7
,

2.

the
\varepsilon
-consistent discovery region

\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon_{n}}^{*}=\bigcup_{t^{*}\in T^{*}}B(t^{*},\varepsilon_{n})
, and

3.

the
high-gradient region

\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon_{n}}=\mathcal{T}\setminus(\mathcal{T}_{0}\cup\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon_{n}}^{*})
,
5
5

5

We call
\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon_{n}}
the high-gradient region because under Assumptions
3
and
7
,
g_{n}(\varepsilon_{n})\to\infty
.

and write
\varepsilon_{n}
-PCER in terms of the expected number of thresholded peaks, and discoveries, in each region:

{\rm PCER}(\varepsilon_{n},u,v):=\frac{\mathbb{E}[N_{u}(\mathcal{T})]-\mathbb{E}[N_{u}(\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon_{n}}^{*})]}{\mathbb{E}[N_{v}(\mathcal{T})]}=\frac{\mathbb{E}[N_{u}(\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon_{n}})]+\mathbb{E}[N_{u}(\mathcal{T}_{0})]}{\mathbb{E}[N_{v}(\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon_{n}}^{*})]+\mathbb{E}[N_{v}(\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon_{n}})]+\mathbb{E}[N_{v}(\mathcal{T}_{0})]}.

In the subsequent sections we derive bounds on each expectation in the expression above, finally establishing Theorem
2
in Section
4.4
. We comment more on Assumptions
7
-
9
in Section
4.3
.

4.1
Null per-comparison error rate

Recall that
{\rm PCER}_{0}(u,v)
is the expected number of null discoveries over the expected number of peaks selected by pre-thresholding. Peak detection via the TG test asymptotically controls the null-PCER as the pre-threshold
v_{n}\to\infty
.

Proposition 1
.

Fix
\alpha\in(0,1)
, and suppose
v_{n}\to\infty
. For all
n\in\mathbb{N}
sufficiently large,

{\rm PCER}_{0}(u_{{\rm TG}}(\alpha,v_{n}),v_{n})\leq\alpha+\frac{C}{v_{n}^{2}}.

Proposition
1
is proved in Sections
10.1
-
10.6
. The proof begins from the exact distribution of
Y_{t}|t\in\widehat{T}_{v}
; formally speaking this is a
Palm distribution
with density
(
1
;
10
)

p_{v}(y|t)=\frac{\mathbb{E}[\det(-\nabla^{2}Y_{t})\cdot\mathbf{1}(\nabla^{2}Y_{t}\prec 0)|\nabla Y_{t}=0,Y_{t}=y]}{\mathbb{E}[\det(-\nabla^{2}Y_{t})\cdot\mathbf{1}(Y_{t}>v,\nabla^{2}Y_{t}\prec 0)|\nabla Y_{t}=0]}\cdot f_{Y_{t}}(y)\cdot\mathbf{1}(y>v).

(21)

Careful analysis of the determinant term above shows that the survival function of this Palm distribution agrees with the mean-shifted TG survival function
\mathbb{S}_{v}(u,d/v)
up to second-order relative error, at which point integrating over
t\in\mathcal{T}_{0}
yields the claim of Proposition
1
.

Previous work
(
35
;
43
;
10
)
has used either the exact distribution (
21
), or exponentially accurate approximations, to calibrate tests for
H_{0,t}
or the global null
H_{0}:\mu\equiv 0
. Indeed our mean-shifted TG approximation can be obtained by extracting the first few terms from the asymptotic approximation of
9
. The accuracy of the resulting approximation is a middle ground between
9
, and the classic
\mathrm{Exp}(1)
approximation
(
3
)
of the normalized overshoot distribution of
Y_{t}
. The insight here is that second-order accuracy can be obtained by a simple mean shift in the reference distribution. One reason this insight is valuable is that it also applies – locally, near high-curvature peaks – under the alternative, where using the exact distribution (
21
) for inference appears more challenging.

4.2
\varepsilon_{n}
-consistent discoveries, and power

The results of Section
3
can be used to lower bound
\mathbb{E}[N_{u}(\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon_{n}}^{*})]
, which is the total expected number of
\varepsilon_{n}
-consistent discoveries. In fact, we prove a slightly stronger result. Integrating
\bar{\rho}(t,y)
over
t\in\mathcal{B}_{t^{*}}:=B_{d}(t^{*},\varepsilon_{n})
and
y\in\mathcal{I}_{t^{*}}:=(\bar{u}_{t^{*}}\pm\Delta_{n})\cap(u,\infty)
gives a second-order accurate asymptotic approximation of
\mathbb{E}[N_{\mathcal{I}_{t^{*}}}(\mathcal{B}_{t^{*}})]
, which is the expected number of
\varepsilon_{n}
-consistent discoveries of
t^{*}
that also have height falling within
\Delta_{n}
of
\bar{u}_{t^{*}}
. Of course
\mathbb{E}[N_{u}(\mathcal{B}_{t^{*}})]\geq\mathbb{E}[N_{\mathcal{I}_{t^{*}}}(\mathcal{B}_{t^{*}})]
, and some additional work shows that in fact the difference between the two is asymptotically negligible. To lighten notation, we let
\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}}:=\mathcal{B}_{t^{*}}\times\mathcal{I}_{t^{*}}
,
N(\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}}):=N_{\mathcal{I}_{t^{*}}}(\mathcal{B}_{t^{*}})
, and

\displaystyle\mathrm{Err}_{\rho}(\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}})

\displaystyle:=C\Big(|\bar{u}_{t^{*}}-\mu_{t^{*}}|\delta_{n}^{2}+\delta_{n}^{2}\Big).

\displaystyle\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{{\rm Trunc}}_{\rho}(\mathcal{I}_{t^{*}})

\displaystyle:=C\delta_{n}^{2}.

Proposition 2
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
1
, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
sufficiently large:

\displaystyle\frac{|\mathbb{E}[N(\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}})]-\bar{\mathbb{E}}[N(\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}})]|}{\bar{\mathbb{E}}[N(\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}})]}

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{Err}_{\rho}(\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}})+\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{{\rm Trunc}}_{\rho}(\mathcal{I}_{t^{*}}):=\mathrm{Err}_{\mathbb{E}[N(\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}})]},\quad\textrm{and}

(22)

\displaystyle\frac{|\mathbb{E}[N_{u}(\mathcal{B}_{t^{*}})-\mathbb{E}[N(\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}})]|}{\bar{\mathbb{E}}[N(\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}})]}

\displaystyle\leq\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{{\rm Trunc}}_{\rho}(\mathcal{I}_{t^{*}}),

where

\bar{\mathbb{E}}[N(\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}})]:=\sqrt{\frac{\det(\bar{H}_{t^{*}})}{\det(-\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}})}}\exp\Big(-\frac{1}{2}(\bar{u}_{t^{*}}-\mu_{t^{*}})\mathrm{tr}(\bar{H}_{t^{*}}^{-1}\Lambda_{t^{*}})\Big)\Psi\Big(u-\mu_{t^{*}}-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}(\bar{H}_{t^{*}}^{-1}\Lambda_{t^{*}})\Big).

(23)

Thus,
\mathbb{E}[N_{u}(\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon_{n}}^{*})]\geq(1-C\delta_{n}^{2}-C|\sup_{t^{*}}\bar{u}_{t^{*}}-\mu_{t^{*}}|\delta_{n}^{2})\cdot\sum_{t^{*}\in T^{*}}\bar{\mathbb{E}}[N(\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}})]
.

The proof of Proposition
2
is given in Section
10.7
.

Remark 5
.

As mentioned previously, under the high-curvature asymptotic assumptions of Theorem
1
, with probability tending to one there will be a unique
\hat{t}\in\widehat{T}
that
\varepsilon_{n}
-consistently estimates a given
t^{*}
. Equation (
23
) can thus be interpreted as the asymptotic probability that the TG test, applied at
\hat{t}
, correctly rejects the null
H_{0,t}
; in other words, as an asymptotic approximation to power. Some routine calculations show that if
(\bar{u}_{t^{*}}-\mu_{t^{*}})\delta_{n}\to 0
then

\bar{\mathbb{E}}[N(\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}})]=\big(1+O(|\bar{u}_{t^{*}}-\mu_{t^{*}}|^{2}\delta_{n}^{2})\big)\cdot\Psi\Big(u-\mu_{t^{*}}-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}(\bar{H}_{t^{*}}^{-1}\Lambda_{t^{*}}\big)\Big).

(24)

Once again, we see that an up to second-order accurate approximation – this time to asymptotic power – is given by applying a first-order mean-shift to a reference distribution, this time to
\Psi(u-\mu_{t^{*}})=\mathbb{P}(Y_{t^{*}}>u)
.

4.3
High-gradient discoveries

Finally, the expected number of high-gradient discoveries is small relative to the expected number of
\varepsilon_{n}
-discoveries, under the regularity conditions of Section
3.1
and Assumptions
7
-
9
.

Proposition 3
.

Under Assumptions
1
-
9
, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
sufficiently large:

\frac{\mathbb{E}[N_{u}(\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon_{n}})]}{\sum_{t^{*}\in T^{*}}\bar{\mathbb{E}}[N(\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}})]}\leq C\delta_{n}^{2}.

(25)

Assumption
7
resembles a typical condition made in the analysis of M-estimators (see e.g.
48
Theorem 5.9), but is weaker in that it allows for the possibility of multiple peaks and a non-empty null region
\mathcal{T}_{0}
. Assumption
8
prevents the signal from being dramatically curvier away from a true peak, implying a uniform upper bound on the determinant term in the Kac-Rice formula.
Assumption
9
ensures that the global maximum of
\mu_{t}
is achieved by an interior local maximum and not a boundary point with large gradient. Notice that these assumptions do not rule out the global null hypothesis
\mu\equiv 0
; they simply assert that any peaks that are present are asymptotically well-separated from the null.

4.4
Proof of Theorem
2

Propositions
1
-
3
immediately imply an upper bound on the
\varepsilon_{n}
-PCER: letting
u_{{\rm TG}}\equiv u_{{\rm TG}}(\alpha,v_{n})
,

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbb{E}[N_{u_{{\rm TG}}}(\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon_{n}})]+\mathbb{E}[N_{u_{{\rm TG}}}(\mathcal{T}_{0})]}{\mathbb{E}[N_{u_{{\rm TG}}}(\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon_{n}}^{*})]+\mathbb{E}[N_{v_{n}}(\mathcal{T}_{0})]}

\displaystyle\leq C\frac{\sum_{t^{*}\in T^{*}}\bar{\mathbb{E}}[N(\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}})]\delta_{n}^{2}+(1+C/v_{n}^{2})\alpha\cdot\mathbb{E}[N_{v_{n}}(\mathcal{T}_{0})]}{(1-C\delta_{n}^{2}-C(\sup_{t^{*}\in T^{*}}\bar{u}_{t^{*}}-\mu_{t^{*}})\delta_{n}^{2})\cdot\sum_{t^{*}\in T^{*}}\bar{\mathbb{E}}[N(\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}})]+\mathbb{E}[N_{v_{n}}(\mathcal{T}_{0})]}

(26)

\displaystyle\leq\alpha+C\Big(\frac{1}{v_{n}^{2}}+\delta_{n}^{2}+\sup_{t^{*}\in T^{*}}(\bar{u}_{t^{*}}-\mu_{t^{*}})\delta_{n}^{2}\Big),

for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
large enough that
C(\delta_{n}^{2}+\sup_{t^{*}\in T^{*}}(\bar{u}_{t^{*}}-\mu_{t^{*}})\delta_{n})\leq 1/2
. This is the claim of Theorem
2
.

5
Post-selection inference with
\varepsilon_{n}
-consistent discoveries

We now turn to localizing peaks via post-selection inference; more explicitly, inference that is valid conditional on
\hat{t}\in\widehat{T}_{u}
being the unique
\varepsilon_{n}
-consistent discovery of a true peak
t^{*}\in T^{*}
, with height
\widehat{Y}
within
\pm\Delta_{n}
of
\bar{u}_{t^{*}}
. The conditional distribution of such a peak is given by

\mathbb{Q}^{\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}}}(\mathcal{A}):=\mathbb{P}\Big((\hat{t},\widehat{Y})\in\mathcal{A}\big|N(\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}})=1\Big)=\frac{\mathbb{P}(N(\mathcal{A})=1,N(\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}})=1)}{\mathbb{P}(N(\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}})=1)},\quad\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}}.

(27)

The smoothness assumptions of Section
2.1
suffice to guarantee that
(\hat{t},\widehat{Y})
are continuous under
\mathbb{Q}^{\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}}}
. The joint density of
(\hat{t},\widehat{Y})
, marginal density of
\widehat{Y}
, and conditional density of
\hat{t}|\widehat{Y}=y
are respectively (writing
\nu_{d}
for the Lebesgue measure of
B_{d}(0,1)
)

p(t,y):=\lim_{r\to 0}\frac{1}{2|r|^{d+1}\nu_{d}}\mathbb{Q}^{\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}}}\Big(B_{d}(t,r)\times[y-r,y+r]\Big),\quad p(y)=\int_{\mathcal{B}_{t^{*}}}p(t,y)\,dt,\quad p(t|y)=\frac{p(t,y)}{p(y)},\quad(t,y)\in\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}}

(28)

Theorem 3
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
1
, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
sufficiently large the following statements hold at all
t^{*}\in T^{*},h\in B_{d}(0,\varepsilon_{n}),y\in\bar{u}_{t^{*}}\pm\Delta_{n}
:

•

The joint density of
(\hat{t},\widehat{Y})
given
N(\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}})=1
satisfies

\displaystyle\frac{\Big|p(t^{*}+h,y)-\bar{p}(t^{*}+h,y)\Big|}{\bar{p}(t^{*}+h,y)}

\displaystyle\leq C\Big(\mathrm{Err}_{\rho}(h,y)+\mathrm{Err}_{\mathbb{E}[N(\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}})]}\Big):=\mathrm{Err}_{p}(h,y),

(29)

where

\displaystyle\bar{p}(t^{*}+h,y)

\displaystyle:=\frac{\bar{\rho}(t^{*}+h,y)}{\bar{\mathbb{E}}[N(\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}})]}.

(30)

•

The marginal density of
\widehat{Y}
given
N(\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}})=1
satisfies

\displaystyle\frac{|p(y)-\bar{p}(y)|}{p(y)}

\displaystyle\leq C\Big(\mathrm{Err}_{\rho}(y)+\mathrm{Err}_{\mathbb{E}[N(\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}})]}\Big):=\mathrm{Err}_{p}(y),

(31)

where
\mathrm{Err}_{\rho}(y)
is defined in (
82
), and

\displaystyle\bar{p}(y)

\displaystyle:=\mathbf{1}(y>u)\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\frac{\exp\big(-\frac{1}{2}(y-\mu_{t^{*}}-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}(\bar{H}_{t^{*}}^{-1}\Lambda_{t^{*}}))^{2}\big)}{\Psi\big(u-\mu_{t^{*}}-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}(\bar{H}_{t^{*}}^{-1}\Lambda_{t^{*}})\big)}.

(32)

•

The conditional density of
\hat{t}
given
N(\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}})=1
and
\widehat{Y}=y
satisfies

\frac{\Big|p(t^{*}+h|y)-\bar{p}(t^{*}+h|y)\Big|}{\bar{p}(t^{*}+h|y)}\leq C\Big(\mathrm{Err}_{\rho}(h,y)+\mathrm{Err}_{\rho}(y)\Big):=\mathrm{Err}_{p}(h|y),

(33)

where

\displaystyle\bar{p}(t^{*}+h|y)

\displaystyle:=\Big(1+T_{10}^{\rho}(h)-\frac{1}{2}T_{30}^{\rho}(h)\Big)\cdot\frac{\sqrt{\det(G_{t^{*}|y})}}{\sqrt{(2\pi)^{d}}}\cdot\exp\Big(-\frac{1}{2}h^{\prime}G_{t^{*}|y}h\Big).

(34)

The proof of Theorem
3
is given in Section
11.1
. Intuitively, in the high-curvature limit
\delta_{n}\to 0
, we are very likely to observe exactly one peak in
\mathcal{B}_{t^{*}}
(see Proposition
4
). Consequently, the post-selection density
p(t,y)
of this unique peak is exponentially well approximated by
\rho(t,y)/\mathbb{E}[N(\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}})]
, which is the intensity of the expected number of such peaks, normalized to integrate to one. Thus the local expansions of Theorem
3
follow from Theorem
1
and Proposition
2
. We refer back to Section
3.4
for more explanation and interpretation of these asymptotic formulae.

Remark 6
.

Calculations similar to those used to prove Theorem
3
also lead to a second-order accurate local expansion of the post-selection density of
\hat{t}
that does not condition on
\widehat{Y}
:

\bar{p}(t^{*}+h):=\Big(1+T_{10}^{\rho}(h)-\frac{1}{2}T_{30}^{\rho}(h)\Big)\cdot\frac{\sqrt{\det(\bar{G}_{t^{*}})}}{\sqrt{(2\pi)^{d}}}\cdot\exp\Big(-\frac{1}{2}h^{\prime}\bar{G}_{t^{*}}h\Big).

Comparing this to (
34
) shows that the effect of conditioning on
\widehat{Y}=y
is to change the post-selection precision from
\bar{G}_{t^{*}}=G_{t^{*}|\bar{u}_{t^{*}}}
to
G_{t^{*}|y}=G_{t^{*}|\bar{u}_{t^{*}}}+(y-\bar{u}_{t^{*}})(-\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}})
, which is a first-order change in the precision. However this will not affect our approach to subsequent inference: both
\bar{G}_{t^{*}}
and
G_{t^{*}|y}
depend on nuisance parameters, so for inference we must plug in an estimate of precision, and for either
\bar{G}_{t^{*}}
or
G_{t^{*}|y}
we will use the estimated precision
\widehat{G}=\widehat{H}\Lambda_{\hat{t}}^{-1}\widehat{H}
. Henceforth we will deal only with the conditional local expansion
\bar{p}(t^{*}+h|y)
.

5.1
Pivotal quantities for height and location

Theorem
3
implies that under
\mathbb{Q}^{\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}}}
,

\bar{\mathbb{S}}_{\mu_{t^{*}}}(\widehat{Y}):=\frac{\Psi\big(\widehat{Y}-\mu_{t^{*}}-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}(\bar{H}_{t^{*}}^{-1}\Lambda_{t^{*}})\big)}{\Psi\big(u-\mu_{t^{*}}-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}(\bar{H}_{t^{*}}^{-1}\Lambda_{t^{*}})\big)}\overset{d}{\to}\mathrm{Unif}(0,1),

while additionally conditional on
\widehat{Y}=y
,

\bar{W}_{t^{*}}(y,\hat{t}):=(\hat{t}-t^{*})^{\prime}G_{t^{*}|y}(\hat{t}-t^{*})\overset{d}{\to}\chi_{d}^{2},

In each case the rate of convergence is up to second-order. However, neither
\bar{\mathbb{S}}_{\mu_{t^{*}}}
nor
\bar{W}_{t^{*}}
can be directly used for inference as they depend on unknown nuisance parameters. To conduct inference for the height, we will plug in the estimates
\widehat{H}=-\nabla^{2}Y_{\hat{t}}
for
\bar{H}_{t^{*}}
and
\Lambda_{\hat{t}}
for
\Lambda_{t^{*}}
, resulting in the asymptotic
Truncated Gaussian (TG)
pivot

\widehat{\mathbb{S}}_{\mu_{t^{*}}}(\widehat{Y})=\widehat{\mathbb{S}}_{\mu_{t^{*}}}(\widehat{Y},\hat{t},\widehat{H}):=\frac{\Psi(\widehat{Y}-\mu_{t^{*}}-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}(\widehat{H}^{-1}\Lambda_{\hat{t}}))}{\Psi(u-\mu_{t^{*}}-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}(\widehat{H}^{-1}\Lambda_{\hat{t}}))}.

To conduct inference for the location we will plug in
\widehat{H}
for both
\bar{H}_{t^{*}}
and
-\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}}
, resulting in the asymptotic
Wald
pivot

\widehat{W}_{t^{*}}(\hat{t})={W}_{t^{*}}(\hat{t},\widehat{H}):=(\hat{t}-t^{*})^{\prime}\widehat{G}(\hat{t}-t^{*}),\quad\widehat{G}:=\widehat{H}\Lambda_{\hat{t}}^{-1}\widehat{H}.

Theorem 4
.

Fix
\alpha\in(0,1)
. Under the assumptions of Theorem
3
, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
sufficiently large:

\Big|\mathbb{Q}^{\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}}}\big(\widehat{\mathbb{S}}_{\mu_{t^{*}}}(\widehat{Y})\leq\alpha\big)-\alpha\Big|\leq C\big(|\bar{u}_{t^{*}}-\mu_{t^{*}}|+\Delta_{n}+1\big)\varepsilon_{n}\delta_{n}:=\mathrm{Err}_{\mathbb{S}}.

(35)

If additionally
(\bar{u}_{t^{*}}-\mu_{t^{*}})\delta_{n}\to 0
, then

\displaystyle\Big|\mathbb{Q}^{\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}}}\Big(\widehat{W}_{t^{*}}(\hat{t})\leq q_{\chi_{d}^{2}(\alpha)}|\widehat{Y}=y\Big)-\alpha+A\cdot\frac{(y-\bar{u}_{t^{*}})+(\bar{u}_{t^{*}}-\mu_{t^{*}})}{2}\mathrm{tr}(\bar{H}_{t^{*}}^{-1}\Lambda_{t^{*}})\Big|

(36)

\displaystyle\leq C\Big(\delta_{n}^{2}(1+|y-\bar{u}_{t^{*}}|^{2}+|\bar{u}_{t^{*}}-\mu_{t^{*}}|^{2})\Big):=\mathrm{Err}_{W}(y),

where
q_{\chi_{d}^{2}}(\alpha)
is the
\alpha
th quantile of
\chi_{d}^{2}
distribution, and
A:=\alpha-\frac{1}{d}\mathbb{E}[\|Z\|^{2}\cdot\mathbf{1}\{\|Z\|\leq q_{\chi_{d}^{2}}(\alpha)\}],Z\sim N(0,1)
is a positive constant.

The proof of Theorem
4
is given in Sections
11.3
-
11.10
. Theorem
4
implies that
\widehat{\mathbb{S}}_{\mu_{t^{*}}}(\widehat{Y})
is up to nearly
second-order pivotal
for
\mu_{t^{*}}
. On the other hand, the Wald-pivot
\widehat{W}_{t^{*}}(\hat{t})
is at most first-order pivotal for
t^{*}
, due to the term

A\cdot\frac{(y-\bar{u}_{t^{*}})+(\bar{u}_{t^{*}}-\mu_{t^{*}})}{2}\mathrm{tr}(\bar{H}_{t^{*}}^{-1}\Lambda_{t^{*}})=\widetilde{O}(|\bar{u}-\mu_{t^{*}}|\delta_{n}).

The failure of
\widehat{W}_{t^{*}}(\hat{t})
to be second-order pivotal is caused by plugging in a biased estimate
\widehat{H}
for the nuisance parameter
-\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}}
in estimating the precision
G_{t^{*}|y}
.

5.2
Conditional coverage

Confidence intervals for peak height, and confidence ellipsoids for peak location, are constructed at each discovery
\hat{t}\in\widehat{T}_{u}
by inverting tests based on the TG and Wald pivots:

I_{\hat{t}}:=\Big\{\mu\in\mathbb{R}:\frac{\alpha}{2}\leq\widehat{\mathbb{S}}_{\mu}(\widehat{Y})\leq 1-\frac{\alpha}{2}\Big\},\quad C_{\hat{t}}:=\Big\{t\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\widehat{W}_{t}(\hat{t})\leq q_{\chi_{d}^{2}}(1-\alpha)\Big\}.

(37)

These confidence regions have asymptotic
(1-\alpha)
coverage under the conditions of Theorem
4
.

Corollary 1
.

Under the conditions of Theorem
4
,

\displaystyle\big|\mathbb{Q}^{\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}}}(\mu_{t^{*}}\in I_{\hat{t}})-(1-\alpha)\big|

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{Err}_{\mathbb{S}},\quad\textrm{and}\quad

\displaystyle\Big|\mathbb{Q}^{\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}}}(t^{*}\in C_{\hat{t}})-(1-\alpha)\Big|

\displaystyle\leq A\cdot\frac{(\mathbb{E}^{\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}}}[\widehat{Y}]-\mu_{t^{*}})}{2}\mathrm{tr}(\bar{H}_{t^{*}}^{-1}\Lambda_{t^{*}})+C\delta_{n}^{2}\big(1+(\bar{u}_{t^{*}}-\mu_{t^{*}})^{2}\big),

where
\mathbb{E}^{\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}}}[\widehat{Y}]
denotes the expectation of
\widehat{Y}
under
\mathbb{Q}^{\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}}}
.

Corollary
1
implies that
I_{\hat{t}}
has up to nearly second-order accurate coverage, as
\mathrm{Err}_{\mathbb{S}}=\widetilde{O}(\delta_{n}^{2}+(\bar{u}_{t^{*}}-\mu_{t^{*}})\delta_{n}^{2})
. Under negligible selection pressure (
\mu_{t^{*}}\gg u
) coverage of the location
t^{*}
is also nearly second-order accurate: in this case
\mathbb{E}^{\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}}}[\widehat{Y}]-\mu_{t^{*}}=O(\delta_{n})
and Corollary
1
implies
\mathbb{Q}^{\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}}}(t^{*}\in C_{\hat{t}})-(1-\alpha)=\widetilde{O}(\delta_{n}^{2})
. However if selection pressure is not negligible, then coverage of the location is at best nearly first-order accurate; and under very strong selection pressure,
C_{\hat{t}}
does not achieve nominal coverage, even asymptotically.

Clearly, an important and challenging part of conducting valid inference for peaks involves dealing with nuisance parameters, particularly
-\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}}
. In more traditional post-selection inference problems, a common strategy
(
20
;
27
)
is to condition on a sufficient statistic for the nuisance parameters, so that the resulting conditional distributions do not depend on the nuisance. This approach is effective when the data generating process belongs to a well-specified exponential family. However in our setting this strategy fails: the minimal sufficient statistic for the nuisance
-\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}}
is the entire field
Y
itself, and any inference conducted conditional on
Y
is trivial. Instead, in Section
6
we pursue an approach based on auxiliary randomization, which leaves behind enough information after selection to accurately estimate
-\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}}
.

5.3
Marginal coverage

We measure marginal (mis)coverage of the overall method using per-comparison miscoverage rate (PCMR), defined in (
11
) and copied here for convenience: recalling that
t^{*}(t):=\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{t^{*}\in T^{*}}\|t^{*}-t\|
denotes the true peak closest to a point
t
,

{\rm PCMR}_{T^{*}}(u,v):=\frac{\mathbb{E}[N(t\in\widehat{T}_{u}:t^{*}(t)\not\in C_{t})]}{\mathbb{E}[N(\widehat{T}_{v})]},\quad\textrm{and}\quad{\rm PCMR}_{\mu_{T^{*}}}(u,v):=\frac{\mathbb{E}[N(t\in\widehat{T}_{u}:\mu_{t^{*}(t)}\not\in I_{t})]}{\mathbb{E}[N(\widehat{T}_{v})]}.

In other words, PCMR measures the expected number of confidence regions that fail to cover the location or height of the nearest peak, compared to the expected number of peaks at which inference is conducted.

The conditional coverage guarantees of Corollary
1
hold for any threshold
u
satisfying the conditions of Theorem
3
, and do not specifically require that
u=u_{{\rm TG}}
be chosen to calibrate the TG test for significance. Taking
u=u_{{\rm TG}}
results in asymptotic marginal coverage: Theorem
2
implies that at most
100\alpha\%
of selected peaks are falsely declared significant without being unique
\varepsilon_{n}
-consistent estimates of some
t^{*}\in T^{*}
, while Corollary
1
implies that at most
100\alpha\%
-percent of
\varepsilon_{n}
-consistent estimate have corresponding confidence regions that fail to cover the truth. Together, these imply asymptotic control of PCMR at level
