\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{{\rm PCMR}}(\alpha,v_{n}):=\frac{\sum_{t^{*}\in T^{*}}\mathbb{P}(N(\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}})=1)\cdot\alpha+\alpha\cdot\mathbb{E}[N_{v_{n}}(\mathcal{T}_{0})]}{\sum_{t^{*}\in T^{*}}\mathbb{P}(N_{v_{n}}(\mathcal{B}_{t^{*}})=1)+\mathbb{E}[N_{v_{n}}(\mathcal{T}_{0})]}\leq\alpha.

Theorem 5
.

Fix
\alpha\in(0,1)
, and suppose
v_{n}\to\infty
. Under Assumptions
1
-
9
, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
sufficiently large:

{\rm PCMR}_{\mu_{T^{*}}}\leq\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{{\rm PCMR}}(\alpha,v_{n})+C\Big(\frac{1}{v_{n}^{2}}+\big(\sup_{t^{*}\in T^{*}}(\bar{u}_{t^{*}}-\mu_{t^{*}})+\Delta_{n}+1\big)\varepsilon_{n}\delta_{n}\Big).

(38)

If additionally
\sup_{t^{*}\in T^{*}}(\bar{u}_{t^{*}}-\mu_{t^{*}})\delta_{n}\to 0
, then

{\rm PCMR}_{T^{*}}(\alpha,v_{n})\leq\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{{\rm PCMR}}(\alpha,v_{n})+C\Big(\frac{1}{v_{n}^{2}}+\delta_{n}^{2}+A\cdot\sup_{t^{*}\in T^{*}}(\mathbb{E}^{\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}}}[\widehat{Y}]-\mu_{t^{*}})\delta_{n}+\sup_{t^{*}\in T^{*}}(\bar{u}_{t^{*}}-\mu_{t^{*}})^{2}\delta_{n}^{2}\Big).

(39)

For the proof of Theorem
5
see Section
11.12
. When there is asymptotically negligible selection pressure – i.e.
\min_{t^{*}\in T^{*}}\mu_{t^{*}}-u_{{\rm TG}}(\alpha,v_{n})\to\infty
– then
\inf_{t^{*}\in T^{*}}\mathbb{P}(N(\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}})=1)/\mathbb{P}(N_{v}(\mathcal{B}_{t^{*}})=1)\to 1
and
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{{\rm PCMR}}(\alpha,v_{n})\to\alpha
. This corresponds to a limiting regime where there is exactly one selected peak
\hat{t}\in\widehat{T}_{v}
that consistently estimates each
t^{*}\in T^{*}
, and that is declared significant with probability tending to one; in this case limiting miscoverage will be exactly the nominal level
\alpha
. Otherwise
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{{\rm PCMR}}(\alpha,v_{n})<\alpha
and the overall procedure will be conservative, since some consistent selections
\hat{t}\in\widehat{T}_{v}
may not be declared significant by the TG test.

6
Peak inference with a randomized field

There are at least two issues with our non-randomized method for selective peak inference. First, the confidence intervals for the height are based on a Truncated Gaussian distribution, and may therefore be quite wide, particularly if there is strong selection pressure
(
24
)
. Second, the confidence regions for location may not have nominal coverage, even asymptotically. Both problems become more dramatic as the significance threshold
u
increases. One way of understanding this is through the idea of left over (Fisher) information
(
20
)
. Intuitively, as
u
increases and selection pressure grows, there is less information left over for
\mu_{t^{*}}
, and consequently less information for the Hessian
\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}}
as well. Less information for
\mu_{t^{*}}
leads to wider intervals for the height; less information for
\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}}
leads to less accurate estimates of nuisance parameters, a less pivotal Wald statistic, and ultimately confidence regions with less accurate coverage.

A conceptually simple way of achieving valid inferences after selection is
data splitting

(
13
;
49
;
26
;
25
)
, which always sets aside some information for inference. However, in our generic problem setup there is only replicate which cannot further be split. A separate issue is that in post-model-selection inference it has been observed that data splitting can be ineffecient
(
20
)
. We propose an alternative method for randomized peak inference that addresses both issues. Roughly speaking the method works as follows: first, synthetic randomization is injected in a way that is designed to mimic the effects of data splitting, similar to
46
;
33
;
28
. The peak detection portion of Algorithm
1
– i.e. pre-thresholding followed by significance testing using the TG test – is then applied to this randomized field. Finally, post-selection inference for height and location is conducted using the resulting discoveries, in a way that is designed to use all of the information left over for inference, along the lines of
data carving

(
20
;
46
)
.

As in the non-randomized setting, our method for peak inference after randomized selection will be based on asymptotic expansions of the density of (randomized) peaks. We derive these expansions heuristically in Section
12
, but do not provide explicit upper bounds on the relative error, nor theoretical guarantees on coverage. Instead we confirm experimentally in Section
7
that the method achieves close to nominal coverage, both conditionally and marginally.

6.1
Randomized peak detection

Our randomized method for peak detection works as follows. First we sample
\omega\sim N(0,C)
, independently of
Y
. We then use
\omega
to “split” the field
Y
into two parts,

Y_{t}^{{\rm sel}}:=Y_{t}+\sqrt{\gamma}\cdot\omega_{t},\quad Y_{t}^{\inf}:=Y_{t}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\gamma}}\cdot\omega_{t},

where
\gamma\in(0,\infty)
is a user-determined parameter controlling the degree of randomization. Notice that by construction, the fields
Y_{t}^{{\rm sel}}
and
Y_{t}^{\inf}
are independent. We produce a set of candidate peaks for inference by first pre-thresholding the peaks of
Y_{t}^{{\rm sel}}
,

\wc{T}_{v}:=\{t\in\mathcal{T}:Y_{t}^{{\rm sel}}>v,\nabla Y_{t}^{{\rm sel}}=0,\nabla^{2}Y_{t}^{{\rm sel}}\prec 0\},

and then applying the TG test for peak detection to thresholded peaks
\check{t}\in\wc{T}_{v}
, resulting in discoveries

\wc{T}_{u}=\{t\in\mathcal{T}:Y_{t}^{{\rm sel}}>u,\nabla Y_{t}^{{\rm sel}}=0,\nabla^{2}Y_{t}^{{\rm sel}}\prec 0\}.

Just as in the non-randomized setting, the randomized peaks detected by the TG test tend to consistently discover true peaks. Concretely, the results of Section
4
imply that applying the TG test to each
\check{t}\in\wc{T}_{v}
, with significance threshold
u_{{\rm TG}}(\alpha,v)\cdot\sigma_{\gamma}
asymptotically controls the
\varepsilon_{n}^{\gamma}
-PCER, where
\sigma_{\gamma}:=\sqrt{1+\gamma}
,
\varepsilon_{n}^{\gamma}:=\delta_{n}^{\gamma}\sqrt{6\sigma_{1}^{2}\log(1/\delta_{n}^{\gamma})}
and
\delta_{n}^{\gamma}=\delta_{n}\cdot\sigma_{\gamma}
.

Randomization guarantees that at least
1/\mathrm{Var}[Y_{t}^{\inf}]=\gamma/(1+\gamma):=\pi
fraction of the total information is left over for inference, regardless of the degree of selection pressure. But there is a tradeoff: more randomization increases the information left over for inference, but can also result in less power for discovering true peaks (at the same nominal level
\alpha
).

6.2
Post-selection peak inference via data carving

Our high-level goal for inference after randomized peak detection remains the same as in the non-randomized setting: we would like to construct confidence regions for the location and height of some
t^{*}\in T^{*}
that is nearby a discovery
\check{t}\in\wc{T}_{u}
, given that such a true peak happens to exist. One way of achieving this, in the spirit of data splitting, is to only use
Y^{\inf}
to form the regions. Since
Y^{\inf}
and
Y^{{\rm sel}}
are independent, the distribution of peaks of
Y^{\inf}
is unaffected by selection, and (second-order accurate) confidence regions can be derived from the marginal distribution of peaks of
Y^{\inf}
. We give a concrete method along these lines in Section
12.1
, which we term selective peak inference via
data splitting
(despite the fact that in our setup there is only one replicate
Y
.)

We now propose an alternative that is more similar in spirit to
data carving

(
20
;
46
)
. As in data splitting, in data carving only
Y^{{\rm sel}}
is used for peak detection. However, data carving forms confidence regions using the full data
Y
, incorporating information from both
Y^{{\rm sel}}
and
Y^{\inf}
. Intuitively, by using all the information left after selection for conditional inference, data carving leads to more powerful inferences and tighter confidence regions than data splitting, while maintaining the same asympotic coverage guarantees. We demonstrate this experimentally in Section
7
.

Conditional distribution after randomized selection.

To construct confidence regions we examine the post-randomized selection distribution of a full-data peak
\hat{t}\in\widehat{T}
about a true peak
t^{*}\in T^{*}
. Roughly speaking, this is the conditional distribution given that there is a unique randomized peak
\check{t}\in\wc{T}
that
\varepsilon_{n}^{\gamma}
-consistently discovers
t^{*}
.
6
6

6

More precisely, the distribution conditions on the event that (i) there is a unique randomized peak
\check{t}\in\wc{T}
that
\varepsilon_{n}^{\gamma}
-consistently discovers
t^{*}
; (ii) there is a unique full-data peak
\hat{t}\in\widehat{T}
that
\varepsilon_{n}
-consistently discoveres
t^{*}
; (iii)
Y_{\check{t}}^{{\rm sel}}\in\bar{u}_{t^{*}}^{\gamma}\pm\Delta_{n}
, (iv)
\widehat{Y}\in\bar{u}_{t^{*}}^{\gamma}\pm\Delta_{n}
where
\bar{u}_{t^{*}}^{\gamma}=(1-\pi)\bar{u}_{t^{*}}+\pi\mu_{t^{*}}
. However (ii) - (iv) occur with high probability as
\delta_{n}\to 0
and thus do not have a large effect on the conditional distribution. See Section
12.2
for more details.
Heuristic calculations carried out in Section
12
suggest that a second-order accurate approximation to the post-randomized selection density of
\widehat{Y}
is

\bar{p}^{{\rm carve}}(y):\propto\Psi\Big(\frac{u-y-\frac{\gamma}{2}\mathrm{tr}(\bar{H}_{t^{*}}^{-1}\Lambda_{t^{*}})}{\sqrt{\gamma}}\Big)\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\exp\Big(-\frac{1}{2}(y-\mu_{t^{*}}-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}(\bar{H}_{t^{*}}^{-1}\Lambda_{t^{*}}))^{2}\Big),

(40)

while a second-order accurate approximaton to density of
\hat{t}|\widehat{Y}=y
is

\bar{p}^{{\rm carve}}(t^{*}+h|y):=(1+T_{10}^{{\rm carve}}(h)+T_{30}^{{\rm carve}}(h))\cdot\frac{\sqrt{\det(G_{t^{*}|y})}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\exp\Big(-\frac{1}{2}h^{\prime}G_{t^{*}|y}h\Big).

(41)

(The first-order terms
T_{10}^{{\rm carve}}(h),T_{30}^{{\rm carve}}(h)
are defined in Section
12
.) We call these calculations heuristic as we do not provide formal upper bounds on the error; we expect that they could be made fully rigorous using similar techniques to those used to prove the results of Sections
3
-
5
, but leave this to future work.

Examining these asymptotic densities reveals the effect of randomization on the post-selection distribution of height and location. Comparing (
40
) to (
32
) shows that the effect of randomization on the post-selection density of
\widehat{Y}
is to replace the indicator
\mathbf{1}(y>u)
by the ‘‘soft-truncation’’ Gaussian survival function.
7
7

7

Here we are borrowing terminology from
31
, who refer to likelihoods with a similar functional form to (
40
) as “soft-truncated” likelihoods.
This is a familiar consequence of randomization in post-selection inference problems
(
46
;
31
)
. We call the asymptotic post-randomized selection distribution of
\widehat{Y}
a
soft Truncated Gaussian
(soft TG) distribution. To a leading order, the soft TG asymptotic limit matches the distribution of
Y_{t^{*}}|Y_{t^{*}}^{{\rm sel}}>u
, as might be expected. Our analysis suggests that once again second-order accuracy is achieved through applying a first-order mean-shift to this reference distribution.

On the other hand (
41
) suggests that the post-randomized selection distribution of
\hat{t}|\widehat{Y}=y
has the same conditional Goldilocks precision matrix as under non-randomized selection. However under randomized selection
\widehat{Y}
has a different deterministic limit –
\bar{u}_{t^{*}}^{\gamma}=\pi\bar{u}_{t^{*}}+(1-\pi)\mu_{t^{*}}
rather than
\bar{u}_{t^{*}}
– and thus under randomized selection the conditional Goldilocks matrix is closer to

G_{t^{*}|\bar{u}_{t^{*}}^{\gamma}}=(-\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}})\Lambda_{t^{*}}(-\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}})+(1-\pi)(\bar{u}_{t^{*}}-\mu_{t^{*}})\Lambda_{t^{*}},

than to
\bar{G}_{t^{*}}
. Since
(-\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}})\Lambda_{t^{*}}(-\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}})\prec G_{t^{*}|\bar{u}_{t^{*}}^{\gamma}}\prec\bar{G}_{t^{*}}
, we see that randomized selection increases precision for the location, but by a smaller amount than non-randomized selection. On the other hand, after randomized selection we can use
Y^{\inf}
to construct an estimate of precision that is asymptotically unbiased even under strong selection pressure.

Pivots and confidence regions.

To construct approximate pivots, we use the limiting distributions suggested by (
40
) and (
41
), and then plug in estimates for nuisance parameters. For the height, this leads to the
soft TG
pivot:

\widehat{{\rm STG}}_{\mu_{t^{*}}}(y):=\frac{\int_{-\infty}^{y}\Psi\Big(\frac{u-z-\frac{\gamma}{2}\mathrm{tr}(\widehat{H}^{-1}\Lambda_{\hat{t}})}{\sqrt{\gamma}}\Big)\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\exp\Big(-\frac{1}{2}(z-\mu_{t^{*}}-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}(\widehat{H}^{-1}\Lambda_{\hat{t}}))^{2}\Big)\,dz}{\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\Psi\Big(\frac{u-z-\frac{\gamma}{2}\mathrm{tr}(\widehat{H}^{-1}\Lambda_{\hat{t}})}{\sqrt{\gamma}}\Big)\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\exp\Big(-\frac{1}{2}(z-\mu_{t^{*}}-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}(\widehat{H}^{-1}\Lambda_{\hat{t}}))^{2}\Big)\,dz}.

(42)

(This is not available in closed-form but can be calculated numerically.) For the location, we again use a
Wald
-type pivot; but now plug in the estimate
\widehat{H}^{\inf}:=-\nabla^{2}Y_{\hat{t}}^{\inf}
for the nuisance parameter
-\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}}
:

\widehat{W}_{t^{*}}^{{\rm carve}}(t):=(t-t^{*})^{\prime}\widehat{H}\Lambda_{\hat{t}}^{-1}\widehat{H}^{\inf}(t-t^{*}).

(43)

As in the non-randomized setting, we can construct confidence regions by inverting tests based on the soft TG and Wald pivots, resulting in

\displaystyle I_{\hat{t}}^{{\rm carve}}

\displaystyle:=\Big\{\mu:\frac{\alpha}{2}\leq\widehat{{\rm STG}}_{\mu}(\widehat{Y})\leq 1-\frac{\alpha}{2}\Big\},\quad\textrm{and}\quad C_{\hat{t}}^{{\rm carve}}:=\Big\{t:\widehat{W}_{t}^{{\rm carve}}(\hat{t})\leq q_{\chi_{d}^{2}}(1-\alpha)\Big\}.

(44)

For convenience, we summarize this method for peak inference via data carving in Algorithm
2
.

We expect that randomization should improve post-selection inference for both height and location, but in different ways. For the height, we expect that
I_{\hat{t}}^{{\rm carve}}
should have asymptotic up to second-order accurate coverage, which is the same order of accuracy achieved by the non-randomized
I_{\hat{t}}
. However we expect the post randomized-selection intervals to be significantly shorter under strong selection pressure, as is the case in more traditional post-selection inference problems
(
46
;
31
;
33
)
. For the location, the distribution of
\widehat{H}^{\inf}
is only weakly affected by selection – since
\widehat{H}^{\inf}
depends on
Y^{{\rm sel}}
only through the location
\hat{t}
at which it is evaluated – and in particular
\widehat{H}^{\inf}
should be an asymptotically unbiased estimate of
-\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}}
, with relative error converging to
0
in probability. As a result we expect that
C_{\hat{t}}^{{\rm carve}}
should have asymptotically up to second-order accurate coverage, even under non-negligible selection pressure, which improves on the coverage of the non-randomized
C_{\hat{t}}
. In Section
7
we verify both of these improvements experimentally.

Algorithm 2

Selective peak inference via data carving

0:

Field
Y
, gradient covariance
\mathrm{Cov}[\nabla\epsilon_{t}]
, pre-threshold
v\in\mathbb{R}
, significance level
\alpha\in(0,1)
, randomization level
\gamma>0
.

1:

Select peaks
\wc{T}_{v}
by pre-thresholding
Y^{{\rm sel}}
.

2:

At each peak
t\in\wc{T}_{v}
, test the null hypothesis
\mu_{t}=0
by applying the TG test to
Y^{{\rm sel}}
with significance threshold
\sigma_{\gamma}\cdot u_{{\rm TG}}(\alpha,v)
, making discoveries
\wc{T}_{u_{{\rm TG}}(\alpha,v)}
.

3:

For each discovery
\check{t}\in\wc{T}_{u_{{\rm TG}}(\alpha,v)}
, let
\hat{t}:=\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{t\in\widehat{T}}\|t-\check{t}\|
be the nearest peak in
Y
. Compute a confidence region
I_{\hat{t}}^{{\rm carve}}
for the height, and a confidence ellipsoid
C_{\hat{t}}^{{\rm carve}}
for the location, using (
42
)-(
44
).

7
Experiments

We conduct some experiments on simulated data to first empirically evaluate the finite-curvature accuracy of our asymptotic theory, and then to compare the performance of our various proposals for selective peak inference.

7.1
Validation of asymptotic theory

To evaluate the finite-curvature accuracy of the asymptotic theory of Section
5
, we draw
50000
independent replicates of a two-dimensional random field
Y\sim N(\mu,C)
defined over domain
\mathcal{T}=[-1,1]^{2}
, with signal and covariance kernel

\mu_{t}=\mu_{0}\cdot K(0,t),\quad K(s,t)=\exp\Big(-\frac{\|s-t\|^{2}}{2\cdot 0.15^{2}}\Big),

so that
T^{*}=\{0\}
and
\mu_{t^{*}}=\mu_{0}
. We retain only those replicates where there is a single peak
\hat{t}\in\widehat{T}_{u}
within distance
\varepsilon_{n}
of
t^{*}=0
. This is repeated for
\mu_{0}\in\{3,4,5,\cdots,11\}
to study the effect of stronger signal/higher curvature, and thresholds
u\in\{\mu_{0}-2,\mu_{0},\mu_{0}+2\}
, to compare results under weak, moderate, and strong selection pressure. The results are displayed in Figure
2
.

For the location, we compare how often the quantities

\displaystyle\chi_{d}^{2}\big((\hat{t}-t^{*})^{\prime}\bar{G}_{t^{*}}(\hat{t}-t^{*})\big),\quad\chi_{d}^{2}\big((\hat{t}-t^{*})^{\prime}\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}}\Lambda_{t^{*}}^{-1}\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}}(\hat{t}-t^{*})\big),\quad\textrm{and}\quad\chi_{d}^{2}\big((\hat{t}-t^{*})^{\prime}\bar{H}_{t^{*}}\Lambda_{t^{*}}^{-1}\bar{H}_{t^{*}}(\hat{t}-t^{*})\big)

fall below the particular choice
\alpha=0.2
, to evaluate how close empirical precision is to the asymptotically correct
\bar{G}_{t^{*}}
, as opposed to the sandwich precision with either the marginal Hessian
-\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}}
or the conditional Hessian
\bar{H}_{t^{*}}
. As predicted by the asymptotic theory, the precision is increasingly close to
\bar{G}_{t^{*}}
as
\mu_{0}
is increased, across different choices of
u
. In contrast, neither of the sandwich precision matrices are correct under moderate or strong selection pressure.

For the height, we compare the distribution of

\displaystyle\bar{\mathbb{S}}_{\mu_{t^{*}}}(\widehat{Y})=\frac{\Psi(\widehat{Y}-\mu_{t^{*}}-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}(\bar{H}_{t^{*}}^{-1}\Lambda_{t^{*}}))}{\Psi(u-\mu_{t^{*}}-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}(\bar{H}_{t^{*}}^{-1}\Lambda_{t^{*}}))},\quad\frac{\Psi(\widehat{Y}-\mu_{t^{*}})}{\Psi(u-\mu_{t^{*}})},\quad\textrm{and}\quad\Psi(\widehat{Y}-\mu_{t^{*}}).

The asymptotically correct
\bar{\mathbb{S}}_{\mu_{t^{*}}}(\widehat{Y})
is well calibrated for all
\mu_{0}
, while the naive choice
\Psi(\widehat{Y}-\mu_{t^{*}})
, which does not account for selection, is not calibrated under moderate or strong selection pressure. Additionally, at lower signal strength and under weak and moderate selection pressure, the first-order mean-shift correction
\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}(\bar{H}_{t^{*}}^{-1}\Lambda_{t^{*}})
noticeably improves calibration.

Additional experiments presented in Section
14
demonstrate that these conclusions are robust to the choice of
\alpha
, and are qualitatively similar in a one-dimensional setting.

Figure 2
:
Distribution of candidate quantities for location (top row) and height (bottom row). Different columns corresponds to different thresholds
u
. Details of experimental setup and takeaways are in the main text.

7.2
Conditional coverage

Our second experiment compares the performance of our three proposals for post-selection peak inference: the non-randomized method outlined in Algorithm
1
, and the two randomized methods, carve (Algorithm
2
) and split (Algorithm
3
). For the latter two methods the randomization tuning parameter is set to be
\gamma=1
, which would correspond to a 50/50 split if
Y
were constructed by averaging independent replicates. The distribution of
Y
and choices of threshold are the same as in our first experiment, but we now examine coverage and size of confidence regions conditional on selection. (Notice that the conditional distributions underlying each method differ in what precisely is conditioned on; see (
27
) and (
135
) respectively.) The results are displayed in Figure
3
.

For the location: all methods achieve nominal coverage when the signal strength is large and selection pressure is weak. Split inference is noticeably worst at the smallest signal strengths: this is due to the fact that, after standardization, the curvture of the field
Y^{\inf}
that split uses for inference is smaller than the curvature of the full data field
Y
. Under moderate or strong selection pressure, the non-randomized method also has inflated type I error, even when the signal is strong, as predicted by our theory.

For the height: all methods achieve very close to nominal coverage across different signal strengths and sample sizes, except for split and carve at the very smallest signal strengths. However, the split intervals are always wider than the carved intervals, and the standard intervals are much wider than either under strong selection pressure.

Additional experiments presented in Section
14.2
demonstrate that these conclusions are qualitatively similar in a one-dimensional setting. They also evaluate performance in a setup where the signal is wider than the covariance kernel. In this last setting, the relative performance of the non-randomized, carve, and split methods is similar, but all methods perform worse at low signal strengths. This makes sense as the wider signal has much smaller curvature, making the problem more challenging.

Figure 3
:
Comparing miscoverage and width of non-randomized, carve, and split methods for peak inference. Top two rows correspond to inference for location, bottom two rows to inference for height. “Width” of a confidence ellipse is the length of its largest semi-axis. Details of experimental setup and takeaways are in the main text.

7.3
Multiple peaks and overall performance

Our third and final experiment compares the performance of the non-randomized, carve, and split methods in a more realistic experiment with multiple peaks. In this experiment the signal is a superposition of nine compactly supported unimodal functions of various peak heights. Additionally, in this experiment we do not set the threshold
u
ad-hoc, but instead set
u=u_{{\rm TG}}(\alpha,v)
based on theory, to control null-PCER at
\alpha=0.1
. Figure
4
shows both conditional coverage on a per-peak basis and overall miscoverage, for both height and location.

For the location: both non-randomized and split methods have inflated miscoverage on a per-peak, conditional basis, while carved is closer to nominal. Marginally, however, all methods have nominal coverage, with split and carve even being conservative. This conservatism is expected because this is a setting where not all true peaks are discovered with high probability; see the discussion after Theorem
5
.

For the height: all methods achieve very close to nominal coverage on a conditional, per-peak basis and are marginally conservative. However the carved intervals are smallest, particularly at the true peaks which have the lowest heights.

Figure 4
:
Comparing power, miscoverage and width of non-randomized, carve, and split methods for peak inference, in the multipeak experiment described in Section
7.3
. Peak heights are evenly spaced between
\mu_{t^{*}}=3
(peak-1) and
\mu_{t^{*}}=6
(peak-9). Top left: example field along with all peaks declared significant by TG test.

8
Summary and discussion

This article lays out several procedures for peak detection followed by localization via formal statistical inference. These procedures account for the fact that peaks were subjected to thresholding and significance testing prior to inference. As a result, they are valid, both marginally and in a post-selective, conditional sense, even in regimes where some peaks will be falsely selected and discovery of true peaks is not guaranteed.

We conclude by mentioning a few interesting directions in which our theory and methodology might be extended.
First, our procedures assume that the field
Y
is standardized, and assume knowledge of
\Lambda_{t}
in constructing confidence regions. If the true covariance kernel
K
is unknown, then the field must be standardized using an estimate of variance, and an estimate of
\Lambda
must be used; it is not clear to us the effect this will have on subsequent inferences. Second, the TG peak detection procedure is calibrated to control PCER rather than a more stringent criterion like FWER or FDR. We believe that a larger choice of significance threshold
u
, based on a Bonferroni/BH-like procedure, should result in control of either FWER or FDR. A likely downstream consequence of this would be that our overall method for inference – with this larger choice of
u
– would control asymptotic false coverage rate rather than the focus of this paper, which is the per-comparison miscoverage rate. Finally, it would be interesting to consider post-selection peak inference using methods for identifying signal regions that are more sophisticated than simply thresholding peaks.

9
Proofs for Section
3

In this section we build to the proof of Theorem
1
. Sections
9.1
and
9.2
contain some preliminary estimates on the asymptotic behavior of the signal and covariance. In Section
9.3
we show that conditional on there being a discovery
\hat{t}\in\widehat{T}_{u}
that consistently estimates
t^{*}
, the relative error between the Hessian
-\nabla^{2}Y_{t}
and
\bar{H}_{t^{*}}
converges to
0
uniformly over
t
in a neighborhood of
t^{*}
. In Section
9.4
we state two Lemmas, Lemma
1
and Lemma
2
, that give local expansions of the determinant and density terms in the Kac-Rice formula for
\rho(t,y)
. Lemma
1
is proved in Section
9.5
and Lemma
2
is proved in Section
9.6
; both proofs rely on the results of Sections
9.1
-
9.3
. Combined these local expansions imply Theorem
1
, as shown in Section
9.7
.

Throughout, we will assume that
n\in\mathbb{N}
is large enough that each of the following is true: (
14
) is satisfied,
\mathcal{B}_{t^{*}}\subseteq B(t^{*},c_{0})
, and
C\sqrt{\log(\lambda_{n})}\leq\lambda_{n}
where
C
is some constant to be determined later. Notice that these are all true if
n\geq C
. In the future, we will not be as explicit about what lower bounds
n
must satisfy, and will often simply revert to stating that
n
is “sufficiently large.”

9.1
Asymptotics of signal

We consider Taylor expansion of
\mu_{t},\nabla\mu_{t},\nabla^{2}\mu_{t}
around
t=t^{*}
for some
t^{*}\in T^{*}
. By Assumption
3
, the remainder terms in these expansions are bounded as follows:

\displaystyle\sup_{h\in B(0,c_{2})}\Big|\mu_{t^{*}+h}-\mu_{t^{*}}-\frac{1}{2}h^{\prime}\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}}h\Big|

\displaystyle\leq C\|h\|^{3}\lambda_{t^{*}},

(45)

\displaystyle\sup_{h\in B(0,c_{2})}\|\nabla\mu_{t^{*}+h}-\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}}h-\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{3}\mu_{t^{*}}(h,h)\|

\displaystyle\leq C\|h\|^{3}\lambda_{t^{*}},

\displaystyle\sup_{h\in B(0,c_{2})}\|\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}+h}-\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}}-\nabla^{3}\mu_{t^{*}}(h)\|

\displaystyle\leq C\|h\|^{2}\lambda_{t^{*}}.

We will use the following consequences of (
45
) and Assumption
3
: there exists a constant
c>0
such that for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
sufficiently large,

\displaystyle\sup_{h\in B(0,c)}\|\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}+h}\|

\displaystyle\leq C\lambda_{t^{*}},

(46)

\displaystyle\sup_{h\in B(0,c)}\Big\{\frac{\|\nabla\mu_{t^{*}+h}\|}{\|h\|}\Big\}

\displaystyle\leq C\lambda_{t^{*}},

\displaystyle\sup_{h\in B(0,c)}\Big\{\frac{|\mu_{t^{*}+h}-\mu_{t^{*}}|}{\|h\|^{2}}\Big\}

\displaystyle\leq C\lambda_{t^{*}}.

9.2
Asymptotics of covariance

We begin by recalling some notation from the main text, and introducing some new notation as well. Recall that
\Lambda_{t}=\mathrm{Cov}[\nabla\epsilon_{t}]\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
and that we write
K_{ij}(t,s):=\mathrm{Cov}[\nabla^{i}\epsilon_{t},\nabla^{j}\epsilon_{s}]
; from now on we abbreviate
K_{ij}(t):=K_{ij}(t,t)
. Let
\Gamma_{t}:=\mathrm{Cov}[\nabla^{2}\epsilon_{t},\nabla\epsilon_{t}]\Lambda_{t}^{-1}=K_{21}(t)\Lambda_{t}^{-1}
; this is a
d\times d\times d
array with entries
(\Gamma_{t})_{ijk}=\sum_{m=1}^{d}[K_{21}(t)]_{ijm}[\Lambda_{t}^{-1}]_{mk}
.

Assumption
2
(and in the case of
\Gamma_{t}
, Equation (
2
)) imply that
\Lambda_{t},\Gamma_{t}
and the first and second derivatives of these array-valued mappings are bounded above by constants:

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\|\Lambda_{t}\|<C,\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\|\dot{\Lambda}_{t}\|<C,\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\|\ddot{\Lambda}_{t}\|<C,

(47)

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\|\Gamma_{t}\|<C,\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\|\dot{\Gamma}_{t}\|<C,\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\|\ddot{\Gamma}_{t}\|<C.

We also note that for any points
t,t^{\prime}
,

\displaystyle\|K_{20}(t,t^{\prime})-\Lambda_{t}\|\leq C\sup_{\tilde{t}\in B(t,\|t^{\prime}-t\|)}\|K_{21}(t,\tilde{t})\|\cdot\|t^{\prime}-t\|\leq C\|t^{\prime}-t\|,

\displaystyle\|K_{21}(t,t^{\prime})-K_{21}(t)\|\leq C\sup_{\tilde{t}\in B(t,\|t^{\prime}-t\|)}\|K_{22}(t,\tilde{t})\|\cdot\|t^{\prime}-t\|\leq C\|t^{\prime}-t\|.

9.3
Asymptotics of Hessian

In the main text we have observed that conditional on the selection event
N(\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}})=1
, the observed Hessian
\widehat{H}
is a biased estimate for
-\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}}
and instead has deterministic limit
\bar{H}_{t^{*}}=\mathbb{E}[-\nabla^{2}Y_{t^{*}}|Y_{t^{*}}=\bar{u}_{t^{*}}]
. In this section, we prove the related claim: conditional on
\{Y_{t}=y,\nabla Y_{t}=0\}
at some
t\in B_{d}(t^{*},\varepsilon_{n}),y\in\bar{u}_{t^{*}}\pm\Delta_{n}
, the relative error between
-\nabla^{2}Y_{s}
and
\bar{H}_{t^{*}}
converges to
0
in
\mathbb{P}
-probability, uniformly over
s\in B_{d}(t^{*},\varepsilon_{n})
. In fact we derive explicit bounds on the rate of convergence, see (
53
). Later in Section
11.3
we show that this implies
\widehat{H}
has deterministic limit
\bar{H}_{t^{*}}=\mathbb{E}[-\nabla^{2}Y_{t^{*}}|Y_{t^{*}}=\bar{u}_{t^{*}}]
under
\mathbb{Q}^{\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}}}
.

Projection/residual decomposition.

To describe the asymptotic behavior of the process
-\nabla^{2}Y_{s}
nearby a selected peak
t\in\widehat{T}_{u}
, we make use of the following decomposition of the negative Hessian of the noise:

\displaystyle-\nabla^{2}\epsilon_{s}=-K_{20}(s,t)\epsilon_{t}-K_{21}(s,t)(\Lambda_{t}^{-1}\nabla\epsilon_{t})+R_{s}^{t}.

By construction the residual
R_{s}^{t}
is a symmetric, mean-zero Gaussian random matrix that is independent of
(\epsilon_{t},\nabla\epsilon_{t})
. The covariance between
R_{s}^{t},R_{s^{\prime}}^{t}
– either marginally or conditionally on
(\epsilon_{t},\nabla\epsilon_{t})
– is an array
K_{22}^{t}(s,s^{\prime})\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d\times d\times d}
, having elements

[K_{22}^{t}(s,s^{\prime})]_{ijkl}:=\mathrm{Cov}\big([R_{s}^{t}]_{ij},[R_{s^{\prime}}^{t}]_{kl}\big)=[K_{22}(s,s^{\prime})]_{ijkl}-[K_{20}(s,t)]_{ij}[K_{20}(s^{\prime},t)]_{kl}-\sum_{m,o}[K_{21}(s,t)]_{klm}[\Lambda_{t}^{-1}]_{mo}[K_{12}(t,s^{\prime})]_{oij}.

(48)

Conditional on
\{\epsilon_{t}=y-\mu_{t},\nabla\epsilon_{t}=-\nabla\mu_{t}\}
, we have that

-\nabla^{2}\epsilon_{s}\overset{d}{=}-K_{20}(s,t)(y-\mu_{t})+K_{21}(s,t)(\Lambda_{t}^{-1}\nabla\mu_{t})+R_{s}^{t},

and therefore conditional
\{Y_{t}=y,\nabla Y_{t}=0\}
we have that

-\nabla^{2}Y_{s}\overset{d}{=}H_{s}^{t}(y)+R_{s}^{t},\quad H_{s}^{t}(y):=-\nabla^{2}\mu_{s}-K_{20}(s,t)(y-\mu_{t})+K_{21}(s,t)(\Lambda_{t}^{-1}\nabla\mu_{t}),\quad R_{s}^{t}\sim N_{d\times d}(0,K_{22}^{t}(s,s)).

(49)

We note that the pinned mean
H_{t}^{t}(y)
is equal to the deterministic Hessian
H_{t|y}
defined in (
7
). Notationally, hereafter when evaluating the pinned mean
H_{s}^{t}(y)
and residual
R_{s}^{t}
at
t=s
, we will drop the superscript, writing
H_{t|y}
for
H_{t}^{t}(y)
and
R_{t}
for
R_{t}^{t}
.

Asymptotic deterministic equivalent: signal region.

We now consider the Hessian
-\nabla^{2}Y_{s}
in the neighborhood of a true peak
t^{*}
. Recall the notation from the main text
\mathcal{B}_{t^{*}}:=B_{d}(t^{*},\varepsilon_{n})
and
\mathcal{I}_{t^{*}}:=u_{t^{*}}\pm\Delta_{n}\cap(u,\infty)
. We will show that conditional on
\{Y_{t}=y,\nabla Y_{t}=0\}
for any
t\in\mathcal{B}_{t^{*}},y\in\mathcal{I}_{t^{*}}
, with high probability the process
-\nabla^{2}Y_{s}
is uniformly close to
\bar{H}_{t^{*}}
over all
s\in\mathcal{B}_{t^{*}}
. To show this we proceed from the projection/residual decomposition in (
49
). We will show first that the relative error between the pinned mean
H_{s}^{t}(y)
and the deterministic Hessian
\bar{H}_{t^{*}}
is uniformly small. Then we will show that with high probability, uniformly over
s\in\mathcal{B}_{t^{*}}
, the residual Hessian
R_{s}^{t}\in\mathcal{R}_{t^{*}}:=B_{d\times d}(0,\xi_{n})\cap\mathbb{R}_{sym}^{d\times d}
, where
B_{d\times d}(0,r)
denotes an
r
-ball in the set of symmetric matrices
\mathbb{R}_{sym}^{d\times d}=\{A\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}:A=A^{\prime}\}
, the radius

\xi_{n}:=\sigma_{22}\cdot\Delta_{n}\sqrt{d},

and the variance term is

\sigma_{22}^{2}:=\sup_{s,t\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\sup_{x\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\mathrm{Var}[x^{\prime}R_{s}^{t}x]<\infty.

(50)

We begin with the pinned mean. Observe that
H_{s}^{t}(y)
is Lipschitz in its lower argument; to bound its Lipschitz constant, we combine the following:

\displaystyle\sup_{s,t\in\mathcal{B}_{t^{*}}}\|\nabla^{2}\mu_{s}-\nabla^{2}\mu_{t}\|

\displaystyle\leq C\lambda_{t^{*}}\|s-t\|

\displaystyle\sup_{s,t\in\mathcal{T}}\|K_{20}(s,t)-K_{20}(t,t)\|

\displaystyle\leq C\|s-t\|

\displaystyle\sup_{s,t\in\mathcal{T}}\|K_{21}(s,t)-K_{21}(t,t)\|

\displaystyle\leq C\|s-t\|,

to conclude via the triangle inequality that

\displaystyle\sup_{s,t\in\mathcal{B}_{t^{*}}}\|H_{s}^{t}(y)-H_{t|y}\|

\displaystyle=\sup_{s,t\in\mathcal{B}_{t^{*}}}\|\nabla^{2}\mu_{t}-\nabla^{2}\mu_{s}+(y-\mu_{t})(K_{20}(t,t)-K_{20}(s,t))+(K_{21}(s,t)-K_{21}(t,t))\Lambda_{t}^{-1}\nabla\mu_{t}\|

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{s,t\in\mathcal{B}_{t^{*}}}C\Big(\lambda_{t^{*}}\|s-t\|+|y-\mu_{t}|\cdot\|s-t\|+\|\nabla\mu_{t}\|\cdot\|s-t\|\Big)

\displaystyle\overset{(i)}{\leq}\sup_{s,t\in\mathcal{B}_{t^{*}}}C\Big(\lambda_{t^{*}}\|s-t\|+\big(|y-\bar{u}_{t^{*}}|+|\bar{u}_{t^{*}}-\mu_{t^{*}}|+\lambda_{t^{*}}\|t-t^{*}\|\big)\|s-t\|+\lambda_{t^{*}}\cdot\|t-t^{*}\|\cdot\|s-t\|\Big)

\displaystyle\overset{(ii)}{\leq}C\Big(\lambda_{t^{*}}\varepsilon_{n}+\Delta_{n}\varepsilon_{n}+|\bar{u}_{t^{*}}-\mu_{t^{*}}|\varepsilon_{n}\Big)

\displaystyle\overset{(iii)}{\leq}C\Delta_{n}.

Above
(i)
uses the upper bounds on
|\mu_{t}-\mu_{t^{*}}|
and
\|\nabla\mu_{t}\|
stated in (
46
), in
(ii)
and
(iii)
we have absorbed asymptotically negligible terms into the constant
C
, and in
(iii)
we observe that
\lambda_{t^{*}}\varepsilon_{n}\leq C\Delta_{n}
. Essentially the same analysis bounds the difference between
H_{t|y}
and
\bar{H}_{t^{*}}
uniformly over
t\in\mathcal{B}_{t^{*}},y\in\mathcal{I}_{t^{*}}
:

\sup_{t\in\mathcal{B}_{t^{*}},y\in\mathcal{I}_{t^{*}}}\|H_{t|y}-\bar{H}_{t^{*}}\|\leq\sup_{t\in\mathcal{B}_{t^{*}},y\in\mathcal{I}_{t^{*}}}C\Big(\lambda_{t^{*}}\|t-t^{*}\|+(|y-\bar{u}_{t^{*}}|+|\bar{u}_{t^{*}}-\mu_{t^{*}}|)\cdot\|t-t^{*}\|+\lambda_{t^{*}}\|t-t^{*}\|^{2}\Big)\leq C\Big(\lambda_{t^{*}}\varepsilon_{n}\Big)\leq C\Delta_{n}.

(51)

To show that the residual
R_{s}^{t}
is relatively small with high probability, we will apply the Borell-TIS inequality as stated in (
152
), which applies as
K_{22}^{t}(s,\cdot)\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for all
s\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
by Assumption
2
, and implies that for any constant
\eta>0
, there exists a constant
C(\eta)
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big(\sup_{s\in\mathcal{B}_{t^{*}}}\lambda_{\max}(R_{s}^{t})\geq\xi_{n}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\Big(\sup_{s\in B(t^{*},1)}\sup_{x\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}x^{\prime}R_{s}^{t}x\geq\xi_{n}\Big)

(52)

\displaystyle\leq C(\eta)\exp\Big(\eta\xi_{n}^{2}-\frac{\xi_{n}^{2}}{2\sigma_{22}^{2}}\Big).

Combining our analyses of the pinned mean and pinned residual, we conclude the following: conditional on
\{Y_{t}=y,\nabla Y_{t}=0\}
for any
t\in\mathcal{B}_{t^{*}},y\in\mathcal{I}_{t^{*}},\eta>0
,

\sup_{s\in\mathcal{B}_{t^{*}}}\frac{\|-\nabla^{2}Y_{s}-\bar{H}_{t^{*}}\|}{\|\bar{H}_{t^{*}}\|}\leq C\delta_{t^{*}}(\Delta_{n}+\xi_{n})\leq C\varepsilon_{n},

(53)

with probability at least
1-C(\eta)\exp(\eta\xi_{n}^{2}-\xi_{n}^{2}/(2\sigma_{22}^{2}))
.

First-order Taylor expansion of Hessian.

So far we have derived an upper bound on the difference between the observed Hessian
-\nabla^{2}Y_{t}
and the deterministic Hessian
\bar{H}_{t^{*}}
. To obtain second-order accurate approximations of the density
p(t,y)
, we will need to compute a first-order Taylor expansion of the observed Hessian about
\bar{H}_{t^{*}}
. Conditional on
\{Y_{t}=y,\nabla Y_{t}=0,R_{t}=R\}
, the observed Hessian is equal to

-\nabla^{2}Y_{t}=H_{t|y}+R.

The first-order Taylor expansion of the right hand side about
(t=t^{*},y=\bar{u}_{t^{*}},R=0)
is

\displaystyle\bar{H}_{t}(y,R)

\displaystyle:=\bar{H}_{t^{*}}+T_{10}^{H}(t-t^{*})+T_{01}^{H}(y-\bar{u}_{t^{*}})+R,

(54)

\displaystyle T_{10}^{H}(h)

\displaystyle=-\nabla^{3}\mu_{t^{*}}(h)+(\bar{u}_{t^{*}}-\mu_{t^{*}})\dot{\Lambda}_{t^{*}}(h)-\Gamma_{t^{*}}(\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}}(h)),\quad T_{01}^{H}(y-\bar{u}_{t^{*}})=(y-\bar{u}_{t^{*}})\Lambda_{t^{*}}.

To upper bound the error incurred by Taylor expansion, we combine the following:

\displaystyle\|\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}+h}-\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}}-\nabla^{3}\mu_{t^{*}}(h)\|

\displaystyle\leq C\lambda_{t^{*}}\|h\|^{2},

\displaystyle\|\Gamma_{t^{*}+h}(\nabla\mu_{t^{*}+h})-\Gamma_{t^{*}}(\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}}h)\|

\displaystyle\leq C\lambda_{t^{*}}\|h\|^{2},

\displaystyle\|(y-\mu_{t^{*}})\Lambda_{t^{*}}+(\bar{u}_{t^{*}}-\mu_{t^{*}})\dot{\Lambda}_{t^{*}}(h)-(y-\mu_{t^{*}+h})\Lambda_{t^{*}+h}\|

\displaystyle\leq C(|y-\bar{u}_{t^{*}}|\|h\|+|\bar{u}_{t^{*}}-\mu_{t^{*}}|\|h\|^{2}),

to conclude that the Taylor expansion remainder term is upper bounded by

C\Big(\lambda_{t^{*}}\|t-t^{*}\|^{2}+|y-\bar{u}_{t^{*}}|\|t-t^{*}\|+|\bar{u}_{t^{*}}-\mu_{t^{*}}|\|t-t^{*}\|^{2}\Big)\leq C\Big(\lambda_{n}\|t-t^{*}\|^{2}+|y-\bar{u}_{t^{*}}|\|t-t^{*}\|\Big).

(55)

The second inequality above follows from the upper bound
|\bar{u}_{t^{*}}-\mu_{t^{*}}|\leq C\lambda_{n}
assumed in Assumption
6
. Finally, notice that the first-order terms in the Taylor expansion have magnitude at most

\|T_{10}^{H}(h)\|\leq C\lambda_{n}\|h\|\leq C\sqrt{\log\lambda_{n}},\quad\|T_{01}^{H}(y)\|\leq|y-\bar{u}_{t^{*}}|\leq\Delta_{n}\leq C\sqrt{\log\lambda_{n}},\quad\|R\|\leq C\sqrt{\log\lambda_{n}},

(56)

and so these terms are asymptotically negligible compared to the leading order term
\bar{H}_{t^{*}}\asymp\lambda_{n}
. Thus, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
sufficiently large that
C\sqrt{\log\lambda_{n}}<\lambda_{n}/4
, it follows that
\|\bar{H}_{t}(y,R)-\bar{H}_{t^{*}}\|\leq\lambda_{n}/2
and so
\|\bar{H}_{t}(y,R)\|\geq\lambda_{n}/2
. We conclude that for all such
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the relative error in a first-order Taylor expansion of the observed Hessian is at most

\frac{\|H_{t|y}+R-\bar{H}_{t}(y,R)\|}{\|\bar{H}_{t}(y,R)\|}\leq C\big(\|h\|^{2}+\delta_{n}|y-\bar{u}_{t^{*}}|\|h\|\big):=\mathrm{Err}_{H}(h,y).

(57)

Local expansion of determinant.

Applying the results of the preceding paragraph leads to a local expansion of the determinant of the observed Hessian
\det(H_{t|y}+R)
about
(t=t^{*},y=\bar{u}_{t^{*}},R=0)
. Let
E_{t}(y)=H_{t|y}+R-\bar{H}_{t}(y,R)
, and suppose
n\in\mathbb{N}
is large enough that
C(\delta_{n}^{2}\log\lambda_{n})\leq 1/4
, where
C
is the constant from (
55
), and
C\sqrt{\log\lambda_{n}}\leq\lambda_{n}/4
. It follows that
\|E_{t}(y)\|/\|\bar{H}_{t}(y,R)\|\leq 1/2
. As a result we can apply (
154
) to conclude that

\displaystyle\Big|\det(H_{t|y}+R)-\det(\bar{H}_{t}(y,R))\Big|\leq C\det(\bar{H}_{t}(y,R))\cdot\frac{\|E_{t}(y)\|}{\lambda_{\min}(\bar{H}_{t}(y,R))}\leq C\det(\bar{H}_{t^{*}})\cdot\mathrm{Err}_{H}(h,y).

Now let’s express
\bar{H}_{t}(y,R)=\bar{H}_{t^{*}}+\bar{E}(h,y,R)
, where
\bar{E}(h,y,R)=T_{10}^{H}(h)+T_{01}^{H}(y)+R
. As stated earlier if
n
is large enough so that
C\sqrt{\log\lambda_{n}}\leq\lambda_{n}/4
, then
\|\bar{E}(h,y,R)\|\leq\|H_{t^{*}}\|/2
. We may therefore apply (
154
) again, concluding that

\displaystyle\Big|\det(\bar{H}_{t}(y,R))-\det\big(\bar{H}_{t^{*}})(1+\mathrm{tr}(\bar{H}_{t^{*}}^{-1}\bar{E}(h,y,R)\big)\Big|

\displaystyle\leq C\det(\bar{H}_{t^{*}})\cdot\frac{\|\bar{E}(h,y,R)\|^{2}}{\lambda_{\min}(\bar{H}_{t^{*}})^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C\det(\bar{H}_{t^{*}})(\|h\|^{2}+|y-\bar{u}_{t^{*}}|^{2}\delta_{n}^{2}+\|R\|^{2}\delta_{n}^{2}).

In the latter inequality we have inserted the upper bounds in (
56
). Additionally, recognize that
\mathrm{tr}(\bar{H}_{t^{*}}^{-1}\bar{E}(h,y,R))=T_{10}^{\det}(h)+T_{01}^{\det}(y)+\mathrm{tr}(\bar{H}_{t^{*}}^{-1}R)
. In summary, we have shown that

\displaystyle\Big|\det(H_{t^{*}+h|y}+R)-\det(\bar{H}_{t^{*}})\big(1+T_{10}^{\det}(h)+T_{01}^{\det}(y)+\mathrm{tr}(\bar{H}_{t^{*}}^{-1}R)\big)\Big|

(58)

\displaystyle\leq C\det(\bar{H}_{t^{*}})\Big(\|h\|^{2}+|y-\bar{u}_{t^{*}}|^{2}\delta_{n}^{2}+\|R\|^{2}\delta_{n}^{2}\Big):=\mathrm{Err}_{\det}(h,y,R).

We will use this local expansion in the proof of Lemma
1
.

9.4
Proof of Theorem
1
: Expansions of determinant and density terms

We are now ready to develop the local expansions of the determinant and density terms in the Kac-Rice formula for
\rho(t,y)
, which we copy here for convenience:

\rho(t,y)=\mathbb{E}\Big[\det(-\nabla^{2}Y_{t})\cdot\mathbf{1}(\nabla^{2}Y_{t}\prec 0)|Y_{t}=y,\nabla Y_{t}=0\Big]\cdot f_{Y_{t},\nabla Y_{t}}(y,0)

Determinant term.

The expectation of the determinant of a Gaussian random matrix, multiplied by the indicator that the matrix is negative definite, is in general difficult to compute. However, in our high-curvature asymptotic setup, at points
t
nearby true peaks
t^{*}
the probability that
\nabla^{2}Y_{t}
is negative definite is exponentially close to
1
. We are thus left with the conditional expectation of a determinant of a Gaussian matrix which can be effectively approximated by local expansion. To concisely write the first-order terms in this expansion, we recall notation from the main text:

T_{10}^{\det}(h)=\mathrm{tr}\big(\bar{H}_{t^{*}}^{-1}\dot{H}_{t^{*}|\bar{u}_{t^{*}}}(h)\big),\quad T_{01}^{\det}(y)=(y-\bar{u}_{t^{*}})\mathrm{tr}\big(\bar{H}_{t^{*}}^{-1}\Lambda_{t^{*}}),

where
\dot{H}_{t^{*}|\bar{u}_{t^{*}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d\times d}
is the derivative of the deterministic Hessian,

\dot{H}_{t^{*}|\bar{u}_{t^{*}}}(h)=-\nabla^{3}\mu_{t^{*}}(h)+(\bar{u}_{t^{*}}-\mu_{t^{*}})\cdot\dot{\Lambda}_{t^{*}}(h)+\Gamma_{t^{*}}(\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}}h).

(59)

Lemma 1
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
1
, at points
t\in\mathcal{B}_{t^{*}},y\in\mathcal{I}_{t^{*}}
, writing
h=t-t^{*}
:

\displaystyle

\displaystyle\frac{\Big|\mathbb{E}[\det(-\nabla^{2}Y_{t})\cdot\mathbf{1}(\nabla^{2}Y_{t}\prec 0)|Y_{t}=y,\nabla Y_{t}=0]-\det(\bar{H}_{t^{*}})\big(1+T_{10}^{\det}(h)+T_{01}^{\det}(y)\big)\Big|}{\det(\bar{H}_{t^{*}})}\leq\mathrm{Err}_{\det}(h,y),

(60)

where

\mathrm{Err}_{\det}(h,y):=C\Big(\big(|y-\bar{u}_{t^{*}}|^{2}+1\big)\delta_{n}^{2}+\|h\|^{2}\Big).

The proof of Lemma
1
is given in Section
9.5
. The linear terms and error term have respective magnitude

\sup_{t\in\mathcal{B}_{t^{*}}}T_{10}^{\det}(h)\leq C\varepsilon_{n},\quad\sup_{y\in\mathcal{I}_{t^{*}}}T_{01}^{\det}(y)\leq C\varepsilon_{n},\quad\sup_{t\in\mathcal{B}_{t^{*}},y\in\mathcal{I}_{t^{*}}}\mathrm{Err}_{\det}(h,y)\leq C\varepsilon_{n}^{2}.

(61)

This is why we refer to
T_{10}^{\det}(h),T_{01}^{\det}(y)
as first-order terms, while the error is only second-order.

Density term.

Standard asymptotic analysis (carried out in Section
9.6
) shows that under Assumption
3
, at points
t\in\mathcal{B}_{t^{*}},y\in\mathcal{I}_{t^{*}}
, the density of both the height and gradient are locally well-approximated by Taylor expansion around
t=t^{*},y=\bar{u}_{t^{*}}
. We again recall the notation introduced in the main text for the first-order terms in this expansion:

\displaystyle T_{21}^{Y}(h,y)

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2}(y-\bar{u}_{t^{*}})h^{\prime}\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}}h

\displaystyle T_{10}^{\nabla Y}(h)

\displaystyle:=-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}\big(\Lambda_{t^{*}}^{-1}\dot{\Lambda}_{t^{*}}(h)\big),\quad

\displaystyle T_{30}^{\nabla Y}(h)

\displaystyle:=h^{\prime}\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}}\Lambda_{t^{*}}^{-1}\{\nabla^{3}\mu_{t^{*}}(h,h)\}-h^{\prime}\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}}\Lambda_{t^{*}}^{-1}\{\dot{\Lambda}_{t^{*}}(h)\}\Lambda_{t^{*}}^{-1}h

Lemma 2
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
1
, at points
t\in\mathcal{B}_{t^{*}},y\in\mathcal{I}_{t^{*}}
, writing
h=t-t^{*}
:

\displaystyle\frac{\Big|f_{Y_{t}}(y)-\bar{f}_{Y_{t}}(y)\Big|}{\bar{f}_{Y_{t}}(y)}

\displaystyle\leq C\Big(\big(|y-\bar{u}_{t^{*}}|+|\bar{u}_{t^{*}}-\mu_{t^{*}}|\big)\lambda_{n}\|h\|^{3}+\lambda_{n}^{2}\|h\|^{4}+|y-\bar{u}_{t^{*}}|^{2}\lambda_{n}^{2}\|h\|^{4}\Big):=\mathrm{Err}_{Y}(h,y),

(62)

and

\displaystyle\frac{\Big|f_{\nabla Y_{t}}(0)-\bar{f}_{\nabla Y_{t}}(0)\Big|}{\bar{f}_{\nabla Y_{t}}(0)}

\displaystyle\leq C\Big(\|h\|^{2}+\|h\|^{4}\lambda_{n}^{2}\Big):=\mathrm{Err}_{f_{\nabla Y}}(h),

(63)

where

\bar{f}_{Y_{t}}(y):=\frac{(1+T_{21}^{Y}(h,y))}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\exp\Big(-\frac{1}{2}\Big\{(y-\mu_{t^{*}})^{2}-(\bar{u}_{t^{*}}-\mu_{t^{*}})h^{\prime}\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}}h\Big\}\Big),

(64)

and

\bar{f}_{\nabla Y_{t}}(0):=\Big(1-\frac{T_{30}^{\nabla Y}(h)}{2}+T_{10}^{\nabla Y}(h)\Big)\frac{1}{\sqrt{(2\pi)^{d}\det(\Lambda_{t^{*}})}}\exp\bigg(-\frac{1}{2}h^{\prime}\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}}\Lambda_{t^{*}}^{-1}\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}}h\bigg),

(65)

The proof of Lemma
2
is given in Section
9.6
. Theorem
1
is essentially an immediate consequence of Lemmas
1
and
2
; for completeness, we include a proof in Section
9.4
.

9.5
Proof of Lemma
1

We begin by rewriting the determinant term using the decomposition in (
49
):

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\det(-\nabla^{2}Y_{t})\cdot\mathbf{1}(\nabla^{2}Y_{t}\prec 0)|Y_{t}=y,\nabla Y_{t}=0\Big]

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{d\times d}^{sym}}\det(H_{t|y}+R)\cdot\mathbf{1}(H_{t|y}+R\succ 0)\cdot f_{R_{t}}(R)\,dR,

(66)

where
f_{R_{t}}(R)
is the Gaussian density of the pinned residual
R_{t}
defined in (
48
). We derive a local expansion of this integral via an approach that we will use repeatedly in the proofs to come: truncate the integral to a range at which a local expansion of the integrand is valid, apply this local expansion, then “undo” the truncation. This yields

\displaystyle\int\det(H_{t|y}+R)\cdot\mathbf{1}(H_{t|y}+R\succ 0)\cdot f_{R_{t}}(R)\,dR

\displaystyle=\det(\bar{H}_{t^{*}})(1+T_{10}^{\det}(h)+T_{01}^{\det}(y))

\displaystyle-\int_{\mathcal{R}_{t^{*}}^{c}}\det(\bar{H}_{t^{*}})(1+T_{10}^{\det}(h)+T_{01}^{\det}(y))\cdot f_{R_{t}}(R)\,dR

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathcal{R}_{t^{*}}}\Big(\det(H_{t|y}+R)\mathbf{1}(H_{t|y}+R\succ 0)-\det(\bar{H}_{t^{*}})\big(1+T_{10}^{\det}(h)+T_{01}^{\det}(y)\big)\Big)\cdot f_{R_{t}}(R)\,dR

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathcal{R}_{t^{*}}^{c}}\det(H_{t|y}+R)\cdot\mathbf{1}(H_{t|y}+R\succ 0)\cdot f_{R_{t}}(R)\,dR.

The first term on the right hand side of the equality above is the claimed local expansion, and so what remains is to bound the various sources of error, due to local expansion, truncation, and undoing the truncation.

Error due to local expansion.

For all
n
large enough such that
C\sqrt{\log\lambda_{n}}<\lambda_{n}
, the positive definite requirement
H_{t|y}+R\succ 0
is automatically satisfied for all
t\in\mathcal{B}_{t^{*}},y\in\mathcal{I}_{t^{*}},R\in\mathcal{R}_{t^{*}}
. (Recall that
\mathcal{R}_{t^{*}}=B_{d\times d}(0,\xi_{n})\cap\mathbb{R}_{sym}^{d\times d}
). Moreover,
\mathcal{R}_{t^{*}}
is symmetric about
0
and
\mathrm{tr}(\bar{H}_{t^{*}}^{-1}R)
is an odd function of
R
, so
\int_{\mathcal{R}_{t^{*}}}\mathrm{tr}(\bar{H}_{t^{*}}^{-1}R)f_{R_{t}}(R)\,dR=0
. Thus the error due to local expansion is

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{R}_{t^{*}}}\Big(\det(H_{t|y}+R)\mathbf{1}(H_{t|y}+R\succ 0)-\det(\bar{H}_{t^{*}})(1+T_{10}^{\det}(h)+T_{01}^{\det}(y)\big)\Big)\cdot f_{R_{t}}(R)\,dR

(67)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{R}_{t^{*}}}\Big(\det(H_{t|y}+R)-\det(\bar{H}_{t^{*}})\big(1+T_{10}^{\det}(h)+T_{01}^{\det}(y)+\mathrm{tr}(\bar{H}_{t^{*}}^{-1}R)\big)\Big)\cdot f_{R_{t}}(R)\,dR

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathcal{R}_{t^{*}}}\mathrm{Err}_{\det}(t,y,R)f_{R_{t}}(R)\,dR

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{R}_{t^{*}}}C\det(\bar{H}_{t^{*}})\Big(\|h\|^{2}+|y-\bar{u}_{t^{*}}|^{2}\delta_{n}^{2}+\delta_{n}^{2}\|R\|^{2}\Big)f_{R_{t}}(R)\,dR

\displaystyle\leq C\det(\bar{H}_{t^{*}})\Big(\|h\|^{2}+|y-\bar{u}_{t^{*}}|^{2}\delta_{n}^{2}+\delta_{n}^{2}\Big):=\mathrm{Err}_{\det}(h,y,\mathcal{R}_{t^{*}}).

The final inequality follows since
f_{R_{t}}(R)
is a probability density of a mean-zero random matrix, with variance
\mathrm{Var}([R_{t}]_{ij})\leq\sigma_{22}^{2}
for all
i,j\in\{1,\ldots,d\}
.

Truncation error.

The error due to truncation and the error due to undoing the truncation can both be bounded using the moderate-deviation inequality (
52
). The error due to undoing the truncation is at most

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{R}_{t^{*}}^{c}}\det(\bar{H}_{t^{*}})(1+T_{10}^{\det}(h)+T_{01}^{\det}(y))\cdot f_{R_{t}}(R)\,dR

\displaystyle\quad=\det(\bar{H}_{t^{*}})(1+T_{10}^{\det}(h)+T_{01}^{\det}(y))\cdot\mathbb{P}\Big(\|R_{t}\|\geq\xi_{n}\Big)

\displaystyle\quad\overset{(i)}{\leq}C\det(\bar{H}_{t^{*}})\cdot\mathbb{P}\Big(\|R_{t}\|\geq\xi_{n}\Big)

\displaystyle\quad\leq C\det(\bar{H}_{t^{*}})\cdot\mathbb{P}\Big(\lambda_{\max}(R_{t})\geq\frac{1}{d}\xi_{n}\Big)

\displaystyle\quad\overset{(ii)}{\leq}C\det(\bar{H}_{t^{*}})\cdot\exp\Big(-\frac{\xi_{n}^{2}}{3d\sigma_{22}^{2}}\Big)

\displaystyle\quad=C\det(\bar{H}_{t^{*}})\delta_{n}^{2}:=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{{\rm Trunc}}_{\det},

where
(i)
follows from the bounds on
T_{10}^{\det}(h),T_{01}^{\det}(y)
set out in (
61
), and
(ii)
invokes (
52
) with a proper choice of constant
\eta
. A similar analysis takes care of the first truncation error term, with the slight complication that we need to properly account for the (polynomial) growth of the determinant term:

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{R}_{t^{*}}^{c}}\det(H_{t|y}+R)f_{R_{t}}(R)\,dR

\displaystyle\overset{(i)}{\leq}\Big(\int_{\mathcal{R}_{t^{*}}^{c}}|\det(H_{t|y}+R)|^{q_{1}}f_{R_{t}}(R)\,dR\Big)^{1/q_{1}}\cdot\Big(\mathbb{P}(\|R_{t}\|\geq\xi_{n})\Big)^{1/q_{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}_{sym}^{d\times d}}|\det(H_{t|y}+R)|^{q_{1}}f_{R_{t}}(R)\,dR\Big)^{1/q_{1}}\cdot\Big(\mathbb{P}(\|R_{t}\|\geq\xi_{n})\Big)^{1/q_{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}_{sym}^{d\times d}}(\|H_{t|y}\|^{d}+\|R\|^{d})^{q_{1}}f_{R_{t}}(R)\,dR\Big)^{1/q_{1}}\cdot\Big(\mathbb{P}(\|R_{t}\|\geq\xi_{n})\Big)^{1/q_{2}}

\displaystyle\overset{(ii)}{\leq}C\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}_{sym}^{d\times d}}(\lambda_{n}^{dq_{1}}+\|R\|^{dq_{1}})f_{R_{t}}(R)\,dR\Big)^{1/q_{1}}\cdot\Big(\mathbb{P}(\|R_{t}\|\geq\xi_{n})\Big)^{1/q_{2}}

\displaystyle\overset{(iii)}{\leq}C\Big(\lambda_{n}^{dq_{1}}+1\Big)^{1/q_{1}}\cdot\Big(\mathbb{P}(\|R_{t}\|\geq\xi_{n})\Big)^{1/q_{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C\det(\bar{H}_{t^{*}})\cdot\Big(\mathbb{P}(\|R_{t}\|\geq\xi_{n})\Big)^{1/q_{2}}

\displaystyle\overset{(iv)}{\leq}C\det(\bar{H}_{t^{*}})\delta_{n}^{2}:={\rm Trunc}_{\det}.

Above
(i)
is Hölder’s inequality, and holds for any conjugate exponents
1/q_{1}+1/q_{2}=1
;
(ii)
relies on (
51
) to upper bound
\|H_{t|y}\|^{p}\leq C\|\bar{H}_{t^{*}}\|^{p}
for all
n
sufficiently large and
(t,y)\in\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}}
;
(iii)
follows because
f_{R_{t}}(R)
is the probability density of a mean-zero Gaussian random matrix with variance
\mathrm{Var}([R_{t}]_{ij})\leq\sigma_{22}^{2}
for all
i,j\in\{1,\ldots,d\}
; and
(iv)
follows from a sufficiently small (but constant) choice of conjugate exponent
q_{2}
.

Summing up the three sources of error gives
\mathrm{Err}_{\det}(h,y)
, and completes the proof of Lemma
1
.

9.6
Proof of Lemma
2

We separately derive (
64
) and (
65
).

Density of height.

The density of the height is

f_{Y_{t}}(y)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\cdot\exp\Big(-\frac{1}{2}(y-\mu_{t})^{2}\Big).

(68)

We substitute the second-order Taylor expansion of
\mu_{t}
about
t=t^{*}
into the quadratic, incurring error

\Big|\big(y-\mu_{t}\big)^{2}-(y-\mu_{t^{*}}+\frac{1}{2}h^{\prime}\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}}h)^{2}\Big|\leq C\Big(\lambda_{n}^{2}\|h\|^{5}+|y-\mu_{t^{*}}|\lambda_{n}\|h\|^{3}\Big).

Expanding the square gives

\Big|(y-\mu_{t^{*}}-\frac{1}{2}h^{\prime}\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}}h)^{2}-(y-\mu_{t^{*}})^{2}-(y-\mu_{t^{*}})h^{\prime}\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}}h\Big|\leq C\Big(\lambda_{n}^{2}\|h\|^{4}\Big),

so that by the triangle inequality

\Big|\big(y-\mu_{t}\big)^{2}-(y-\mu_{t^{*}})^{2}-(y-\mu_{t^{*}})h^{\prime}\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}}h\Big|\leq C\Big(\lambda_{n}^{2}\|h\|^{4}+|y-\mu_{t^{*}}|\lambda_{n}\|h\|^{3}\Big).

The right hand side of the above equation is at most
C\delta_{n}^{2}(\log\lambda_{n})^{2}
, which is less than
1/2
for all
n
sufficiently large. In that case, exponentiating both sides of the above inequality and invoking the bound
|\exp(x)-1|\leq\exp(1/2)|x|
for all
|x|\leq 1/2
yields

\frac{f_{Y_{t}}(y)-\tilde{f}_{Y_{t}}(y)}{\tilde{f}_{Y_{t}}(y)}\leq C(\lambda_{n}^{2}\|h\|^{4}+|y-\mu_{t^{*}}|\lambda_{n}\|h\|^{3}),

where

\displaystyle\tilde{f}_{Y_{t}}(y)

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\exp\Big(-\frac{1}{2}(y-\mu_{t^{*}})^{2}+\frac{1}{2}(y-\mu_{t^{*}})h^{\prime}\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}}h\Big)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\exp\Big(-\frac{1}{2}(y-\mu_{t^{*}})^{2}+\frac{1}{2}(\bar{u}_{t^{*}}-\mu_{t^{*}})h^{\prime}\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}}h\Big)\exp\Big(\frac{1}{2}(y-\bar{u}_{t^{*}})h^{\prime}\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}}h\Big).

Finally, invoking the bound

\Big|\exp\Big(\frac{1}{2}(y-\bar{u}_{t^{*}})h^{\prime}\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}}h\Big)-\big(1+\frac{1}{2}(y-\bar{u}_{t^{*}})h^{\prime}\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}}h\big)\Big|\leq C|y-\bar{u}_{t^{*}}|^{2}\lambda_{n}^{2}\|h\|^{4},

again valid for all
n
sufficiently large, gives the desired approximation
\bar{f}_{Y_{t}}(y)
.

Density of gradient.

The density of the gradient
\nabla Y_{t}
evaluated at
0
is

f_{\nabla Y_{t}}(0)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{(2\pi)^{d}\det(\Lambda_{t})}}\exp\Big(-\frac{1}{2}\nabla\mu_{t}^{\prime}\Lambda_{t}^{-1}\nabla\mu_{t}\Big).

(69)

A first-order Taylor expansion of
\Lambda_{t}^{-1}
about
t=t^{*}
implies

\|\Lambda_{t^{*}+h}^{-1}-\Lambda_{t^{*}}^{-1}+\Lambda_{t^{*}}^{-1}\{\dot{\Lambda}_{t^{*}}(h)\}\Lambda_{t^{*}}^{-1}\|\leq C\|h\|^{2},

where the bound on the remainder term holds as (
2
) implies that
\|\Lambda_{t}^{-1}\|
is uniformly bounded away from
0
. Inserting this along with a second-order Taylor expansion of
\nabla\mu_{t}
about
t=t^{*}
into the quadratic, we obtain

\Big|\nabla\mu_{t}^{\prime}\Lambda_{t}^{-1}\nabla\mu_{t}-\big(\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}}h+\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{3}\mu(h,h)\big)^{\prime}\big(\Lambda_{t^{*}}^{-1}-\Lambda_{t^{*}}^{-1}\{\dot{\Lambda}_{t^{*}}(h)\}\Lambda_{t^{*}}^{-1}\big)\big(\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}}h+\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{3}\mu(h,h)\big)\Big|\leq C\big(\lambda_{n}^{2}\|h\|^{4}\big).

Expanding the resulting quadratic gives

\Big|(\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}}h+\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{3}\mu(h,h))^{\prime}(\Lambda_{t^{*}}^{-1}-\Lambda_{t^{*}}^{-1}\{\dot{\Lambda}_{t^{*}}(h)\}\Lambda_{t^{*}}^{-1})(\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}}h+\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{3}\mu(h,h))-\Big(h^{\prime}\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}}\Lambda_{t^{*}}^{-1}\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}}h+T_{30}^{\nabla Y}(h)\Big)\Big|\leq C\Big(\lambda_{n}^{2}\|h\|^{4}\Big),

and so by the triangle inequality

\Big|\nabla\mu_{t}^{\prime}\Lambda_{t}^{-1}\nabla\mu_{t}-\Big(h^{\prime}\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}}\Lambda_{t^{*}}^{-1}\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}}h+T_{30}^{\nabla Y}(h)\Big)\Big|\leq C\Big(\lambda_{n}^{2}\|h\|^{4}\Big).

On the other hand, a first-order Taylor expansion of
\{\det(\Lambda_{t})\}^{-1/2}
about
t=t^{*}
implies

\Big|\frac{1}{\sqrt{\det(\Lambda_{t})}}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\det(\Lambda_{t^{*}})}}(1-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}(\Lambda_{t^{*}}^{-1}\dot{\Lambda}_{t^{*}}(h)))\Big|\leq C\|h\|^{2},

where (
2
) implies that
\det(\Lambda_{t})
is uniformly bounded away from
0
. We have thus established an approximation to the log of
f_{\nabla Y_{t}}(0)
; from here, analysis similar to that used in tackling the density of the height yields the claimed result (
65
).

9.7
Finishing off the proof of Theorem
1

After applying Lemmas
1
and
2
, some routine calculations yield the upper bound

\frac{\rho(t,y)-\bar{\rho}(t,y)}{\bar{\rho}(t,y)}\leq C\Big(\{T_{01}^{\rho}(y)\}^{2}+\{T_{10}^{\rho}(h)\}^{2}+\{T_{30}^{\rho}(h)\}^{2}+\{T_{21}^{\rho}(h,y)\}^{2}+\mathrm{Err}_{\det}(h,y)+\mathrm{Err}_{Y}(h,y)+\mathrm{Err}_{{\nabla Y}}(h)\Big).

As one would anticipate, each of the squared first-order terms above is second-order:

\displaystyle\{T_{01}^{\rho}(y)\}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C|y-\bar{u}_{t^{*}}|^{2}\delta_{n}^{2}

\displaystyle\{T_{10}^{\rho}(h)\}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\|h\|^{2}

\displaystyle\{T_{30}^{\rho}(h)\}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\lambda_{n}^{4}\|h\|^{6}

\displaystyle\{T_{21}^{\rho}(h,y)\}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C|y-\bar{u}_{t^{*}}|^{2}\lambda_{n}^{2}\|h\|^{4},

all of which are at most a constant times
\mathrm{Err}_{\det}(h,y)+\mathrm{Err}_{\nabla Y}(h)
. Thus we have shown that
\frac{\rho(t,y)-\bar{\rho}(t,y)}{\bar{\rho}(t,y)}
is upper bounded by
\mathrm{Err}_{\rho}(h,y)
, and the corresponding lower bound follows similarly.

10
Proofs for Section
4

10.1
Proof of Proposition
1

We begin by deriving a second-order accurate Truncated Gaussian approximation to the Palm density defined in (
21
). The approximation is derived by local expansion, and will be valid at points
y\in v\pm\sqrt{3\log v}
for all
v\in\mathbb{R}
sufficiently large. (Here and henceforth, we use the phrase “for all
v
sufficiently large” to mean “for all
v\geq C
”, where as always
C
is a constant that depends only on the covariance kernel
K
, dimension
d
and level
\alpha
.)

Lemma 3
.

For all
v
sufficiently large, for any
t\in\mathcal{T}_{0}
and
y\in v\pm\sqrt{3\log v}
,

\frac{|\bar{p}_{v}(y|t)-p_{v}(y|t)|}{\bar{p}_{v}(y|t)}\leq C\Big(\frac{(y-v)^{2}}{v^{2}}+\frac{1}{v^{2}}\Big):=\mathrm{Err}_{p}(y|t)

(70)

where

\bar{p}_{v}(y|t)=\frac{1}{\Psi(v-d/v)}\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\exp\Big(-\frac{1}{2}(y-d/v)^{2}\Big).

(71)

The proof of Lemma
3
is given in Section
10.2
.
The survival function corresponding to this density is
\mathbb{P}(Y_{t}>u|t\in\widehat{T}_{v})=\int_{u}^{\infty}p_{v}(y|t)\,dy
. Using Lemma
3
and a truncation argument it can be shown that this survival function is close to a Truncated Gaussian survival function with mean parameter
d/v
.

Lemma 4
.

For any constant
B>0
, the following statement holds: for all
v
sufficiently large, for any
v\leq u\leq v+B/v
,

\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T}_{0}}\frac{\Big|\mathbb{P}(Y_{t}>u|t\in\widehat{T}_{v})-\mathbb{S}_{v}(u,d/v)\Big|}{\mathbb{S}_{v}(u,d/v)}\leq\frac{C}{v^{2}},

where
C
depends on
B
.

The proof of Lemma
4
is given in Section
10.3
. We assume an upper bound on
u
in Lemma
4
in order to get an upper bound on the relative, rather than absolute, error. It is likely that the result can be strengthened to hold for a wider range of
u
– thus allowing
\alpha\to 0
in Proposition
1
– but we will not pursue this further.

The final step in our analysis is to use Lemma
4
to show that the null false positive rate is close to
\alpha
. This is straightforward. The expected number of null discoveries made by the TG test is

\mathbb{E}[N_{u_{{\rm TG}}(\alpha,v)}(\mathcal{T}_{0})]=\int_{\mathcal{T}_{0}}\int_{u_{{\rm TG}}(\alpha,v)}^{\infty}\rho_{v}(t,y)\,dy\,dt=\int_{\mathcal{T}_{0}}\Big\{\int_{u_{{\rm TG}}(\alpha,v)}^{\infty}p_{v}(y|t)\,dy\Big\}\cdot\int_{v}^{\infty}\rho_{v}(t,y)\,dy\,dt.

(72)

For any fixed
\alpha\in(0,1)
, let
B_{\alpha}
be the twice the
(1-\alpha)
th quantile of the
\mathrm{Exp}(1)
distribution. For all
n\in\mathbb{N}
sufficiently large
u_{{\rm TG}}(\alpha,v_{n})\leq v_{n}+\frac{B_{\alpha}}{v_{n}}
. We can therefore apply Lemma
4
to conclude that

\Big|\int_{u_{{\rm TG}}(\alpha,v_{n})}^{\infty}p_{v}(y|t)\,dy-\int_{u_{{\rm TG}}(\alpha,v_{n})}^{\infty}\bar{p}_{v}(y|t)\,dy\Big|\leq C\frac{\mathbb{S}_{v_{n}}(u_{{\rm TG}}(\alpha,v_{n}),d/v_{n})}{v_{n}^{2}}\leq\frac{C}{v_{n}^{2}}.

Replacing
p_{v}(y|t)
by
\bar{p}_{v}(y|t)
in (
72
), and recognizing that
\int_{u_{{\rm TG}}(\alpha,v)}^{\infty}\bar{p}_{v}(y|t)\,dy=\mathbb{S}_{v}(u_{{\rm TG}}(\alpha,v),d/v)=\alpha
and
\int_{\mathcal{T}_{0}}\int_{v}^{\infty}\rho_{v}(t,y)\,dy\,dt=\mathbb{E}[N_{v}(\mathcal{T}_{0})]
, we deduce that

\Big|\mathbb{E}[N_{u_{{\rm TG}}(\alpha,v_{n})}(\mathcal{T}_{0})]-\alpha\cdot\mathbb{E}[N_{v_{n}}(\mathcal{T}_{0})]\Big|\leq\frac{C}{v_{n}^{2}}\mathbb{E}[N_{v_{n}}(\mathcal{T}_{0})].

After dividing both sides of the previous inequality by
\mathbb{E}[N_{v_{n}}(\mathcal{T}_{0})]
, and recognizing that
{\rm PCER}_{0}(u,v)=\mathbb{E}[N_{u}(\mathcal{T}_{0})]/N_{v}(\mathcal{T})]\leq\mathbb{E}[N_{u}(\mathcal{T}_{0})]/N_{v}(\mathcal{T}_{0})]
, we arrive at the claim of Proposition
1
.

10.2
Proof of Lemma
3

Recall the Palm density defined in (
21
),

p_{v}(y|t)=\frac{\mathbb{E}[\det(-\nabla^{2}Y_{t})\cdot\mathbf{1}(\nabla^{2}Y_{t}\prec 0)|\nabla Y_{t}=0,Y_{t}=y]}{\mathbb{E}[\det(-\nabla^{2}Y_{t})\cdot\mathbf{1}(Y_{t}>v,\nabla^{2}Y_{t}\prec 0)|\nabla Y_{t}=0]}\cdot f_{Y_{t}}(y)\cdot\mathbf{1}(y>v)=\frac{\rho_{v}(t,y)}{\int_{v}^{\infty}\rho_{v}(t,y)\,dy}.

To prove Lemma
3
we give a local expansion of
\rho_{v}(t,y)
about
y=v
that is accurate at points
t\in\mathcal{T}_{0}
.

Lemma 5
.

For all
v
sufficiently large, for any
t\in\mathcal{T}_{0}
and
y\in v\pm\sqrt{3\log v}
,

\frac{\Big|\rho_{v}(t,y)-\bar{\rho}(t,y)\Big|}{\bar{\rho}(t,y)}\leq C\Big(\frac{(y-v)^{2}}{v^{2}}+\frac{1}{v^{2}}\Big).

(73)

where

\displaystyle\bar{\rho}(t,y)

\displaystyle:=\frac{v^{d}\sqrt{\det(\Lambda_{t})}\exp(-d)}{(2\pi)^{(d+1)/2}}\exp\Big(-\frac{1}{2}(y-d/v)^{2}\Big).

(74)

The proof of Lemma
5
is given in Section
10.4
. It follows similar steps to the proof of Theorem
1
, but is simpler because we only compute a local expansion in the second argument of
\rho_{v}(t,y)
.

To compute the normalizing constant
\rho_{v}(t)=\int_{v}^{\infty}\rho_{v}(t,y)\,dy
– which can be thought of as the marginal intensity function of the counting process
N_{v}
– we combine Lemma
5
with a truncation argument.

Lemma 6
.

For all
v
sufficiently large, for any
t\in\mathcal{T}_{0}
,

\frac{|\rho_{v}(t)-\bar{\rho}_{v}(t)|}{\bar{\rho}_{v}(t)}\leq\frac{C}{v^{2}}.

where

\bar{\rho}_{v}(t)=\frac{v^{d}\sqrt{\det(\Lambda_{t})}\exp(-d)}{(2\pi)^{d/2}}\cdot\Psi(v-d/v).

The proof of Lemma
6
is given in Section
10.5
. Lemma
3
follows immediately from Lemmas
5
and
6
.

10.3
Proof of Lemma
4

The survival function of
Y_{t}|t\in\widehat{T}_{v}
can be written in terms of the marginal intensity
\rho_{v}(t)
:

\mathbb{P}(Y_{t}>u|t\in\widehat{T}_{v})=\frac{\rho_{u}(t)}{\rho_{v}(t)}.

Lemma
6
upper bounds the relative error incurred by substituting
\bar{\rho}_{v}(t)
for
\rho_{v}(t)
. Exactly the same analysis used to prove Lemma
6
, but substituting
u
for
v
everywhere in the proof of that lemma, also implies that

\frac{|\bar{\rho}_{u}(t)-\rho_{u}(t)|}{\bar{\rho}_{u}(t)}\leq\frac{C}{u^{2}},

(75)

where to be perfectly explicit

\ \bar{\rho}_{u}(t)=\frac{u^{d}\sqrt{\det(\Lambda_{t})}\exp(-d)}{(2\pi)^{d/2}}\cdot\Psi(u-d/u).

It remains to upper bound the relative error between
\bar{\rho}_{u}(t)
and

\tilde{\rho}_{u}(t)=\frac{v^{d}\sqrt{\det(\Lambda_{t})}\exp(-d)}{(2\pi)^{d/2}}\cdot\Psi(u-d/v),

and it is here we use the condition that
v\leq u\leq v+\frac{B}{v}
. Specifically, this condition along with Taylor expansion implies that for all
v\geq C\sqrt{\log v}
sufficiently large:

\big|u^{d}-v^{d}\big|\leq Cv^{d}\frac{(u-v)}{v}\leq\frac{Cv^{d}}{v^{2}},

and

\Big|\Psi\Big(u-\frac{d}{u}\Big)-\Psi\Big(u-\frac{d}{v}\Big)\Big|\leq\Psi\Big(u-\frac{d}{v}\Big)\cdot\frac{(u-v)}{v}\leq C\Psi\Big(u-\frac{d}{v}\Big)\frac{1}{v^{2}},

from which we deduce that

\frac{|\tilde{\rho}_{u}(t)-\bar{\rho}_{u}(t)|}{\tilde{\rho}_{u}(t)}\leq\frac{C}{v^{2}}.

(76)

Together (
75
), (
76
) and Lemma
6
imply the result of Lemma
4
.

10.4
Proof of Lemma
5

At null points
t\in\mathcal{T}_{0}
, the Kac-Rice formula of the intensity
\rho(t,y)
simplifies:

\displaystyle\rho(t,y)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Big[\det(-\nabla^{2}Y_{t})\cdot\mathbf{1}(\nabla^{2}Y_{t}\prec 0)|\nabla Y_{t}=0,Y_{t}=y\Big]\cdot f_{Y_{t}}(y)\cdot f_{\nabla Y_{t}}(0)

(77)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Big[\det(-\nabla^{2}Y_{t})\cdot\mathbf{1}(\nabla^{2}Y_{t}\prec 0)|\nabla Y_{t}=0,Y_{t}=y\Big]\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\exp\Big(-\frac{1}{2}y^{2}\Big)\cdot\frac{1}{(2\pi\det(\Lambda_{t}))^{d/2}}.

To prove Lemma
5
, we compute a local expansion of the determinant term in the above expression.

Lemma 7
.

For all
v
sufficiently large, for any
t\in\mathcal{T}_{0}
and
y\in v\pm\sqrt{3\log v}
,

\frac{\Bigg|\mathbb{E}\Big[\det(-\nabla^{2}Y_{t})\cdot\mathbf{1}(\nabla^{2}Y_{t}\prec 0)|\nabla Y_{t}=0,Y_{t}=y\Big]-\Big(1+(y-v)\frac{d}{v}\Big)\det(v\cdot\Lambda_{t})\Bigg|}{\det(v\cdot\Lambda_{t})}\leq C\Big(\frac{(y-v)^{2}}{v^{2}}+\frac{1}{v^{2}}\Big).

(78)

The proof of Lemma
7
is given in Section
10.6
. Now we show how the first-order expansion of the determinant term translates into a mean shift in the Gaussian density in (
77
). For all
v
large enough so that
d\sqrt{3\log v}/v\leq 1
, we have that for all
y\in v\pm\sqrt{3\log v}
,

\Big|1+(y-v)\frac{d}{v}-\exp\Big((y-v)\frac{d}{v}\Big)\Big|\leq C\frac{(y-v)^{2}}{v^{2}}.

(79)

On the other hand,

\Big|\frac{\exp(-\frac{1}{2}y^{2})}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\cdot\exp\Big((y-v)\frac{d}{v}\Big)-\frac{\exp(-d)}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\exp\Big(-\frac{1}{2}(y-d/v)^{2}\Big)\Big|\leq\frac{C}{v^{2}}\exp\Big(-\frac{1}{2}(y-d/v)^{2}\Big).

(80)

Combining (
78
)-(
80
) gives the claim of Lemma
5
.

10.5
Proof of Lemma
6

We expand the marginal intensity
\rho_{v}(t)
of the counting process
N_{v}
as follows:

\rho_{v}(t)=\int_{v}^{\infty}\rho_{v}(t,y)\,dy=\int_{v}^{\infty}\bar{\rho}(t,y)\,dy+\int_{v+\sqrt{3\log v}}^{\infty}\bar{\rho}(t,y)\,dy+\int_{v}^{v+\sqrt{3\log v}}\rho_{v}(t,y)-\bar{\rho}(t,y)\,dy+\int_{v+\sqrt{3\log v}}^{\infty}\rho_{v}(t,y)\,dy.

Above
\bar{\rho}(t,y)
is the approximate intensity defined in (
74
). The first term on the right hand side of the previous equation is the main term:

\int_{v}^{\infty}\bar{\rho}(t,y)\,dy=\det(H_{t|v})\exp(-d)\Psi(v-d/v)f_{\nabla Y_{t}}(0)=\bar{\rho}_{v}(t).

(81)

The remaining terms represent error due to truncating the integral to
[v,v+\sqrt{3\log v}]
, replacing
\rho_{v}(t,y)
by the local expansion
\bar{\rho}(t,y)
, and undoing the truncation. The error due to truncation is at most

\displaystyle\int_{v+\sqrt{3\log v}}^{\infty}\rho_{v}(t,y)\,dy

\displaystyle=\int_{v+\sqrt{3\log v}}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}[\det(-\nabla^{2}Y_{t})\cdot\mathbf{1}(\nabla^{2}Y_{t}\prec 0)|Y_{t}=y,\nabla Y_{t}=0]\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\exp\Big(-\frac{1}{2}y^{2}\Big)f_{\nabla Y_{t}}(0)\,dy

\displaystyle\overset{(i)}{\leq}C\int_{v+\sqrt{3\log v}}^{\infty}y^{d}\cdot\exp\big(-\frac{1}{2}y^{2}\big)\,dy

\displaystyle\overset{(ii)}{\leq}Cv^{d}\Psi(v+\sqrt{3\log v})

\displaystyle\overset{(iii)}{\leq}Cv^{d}\Psi(v)\cdot\exp(-\frac{1}{2}v)

\displaystyle\leq C\bar{\rho}_{v}(t)\cdot\exp(-\frac{1}{2}v).

Above,
(i)
uses the fact that
f_{\nabla Y_{t}}(0)\leq C/\sqrt{\det(\Lambda_{t})}\leq C
is upper bounded by a constant, while the determinant term grows polynomially in
y
, i.e. for all
v\geq 1
and
y>v+\sqrt{3\log v}
,

\mathbb{E}[\det(-\nabla^{2}Y_{t})|\nabla Y_{t}=0,Y_{t}=y]\leq C(y^{d}+1)\leq Cy^{d};

(ii)
follows from the bound on the moments of a Truncated Gaussian as given in (
146
); and
(iii)
applies the upper bound on Mills’ ratio as stated in (
144
). Very similar arguments imply that

\int_{v+\sqrt{3\log v}}^{\infty}\bar{\rho}(t,y)\,dy=C\det(v\cdot\Lambda_{t})\cdot\Psi(v+\sqrt{3\log v}-d/v)\leq C\bar{\rho}_{v}(t)\cdot\exp(-\frac{1}{2}v).

Finally, Lemma
5
upper bounds the relative error between
\bar{\rho}(t,y)
and
\rho_{v}(t,y)
. Integrating this yields the following upper bound:

\displaystyle\int_{v}^{v+\sqrt{3\log v}}|\rho_{v}(t,y)-\bar{\rho}(t,y)|\,dy

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{v}^{\infty}\bar{\rho}(t,y)\Big(\frac{(y-v)^{2}}{v^{2}}+\frac{1}{v^{2}}\Big)\,dy

\displaystyle\leq C\det(v\cdot\Lambda_{t})\cdot\frac{\Psi(v-d/v)}{v^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C\bar{\rho}_{v}(t)\cdot\frac{1}{v^{2}},

with the second and third inequalities following by (
146
) and (
144
) respectively. Combining these bounds yields the claim of Lemma
6
.

10.6
Proof of Lemma
7

The proof of Lemma
7
is similar to the proof of Lemma
1
. The determinant term is

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}}\det(H_{t|y}+R)\cdot\mathbf{1}(H_{t|y}+R\succ 0)\cdot f_{R_{t}}(R)\,dR,

where we recall that the density of the pinned residual
f_{R_{t}}(R)
is defined in (
48
). Let
\mathcal{R}_{0}:=B_{d\times d}(0,d\cdot\sqrt{3\log v})
. We expand the determinant term as follows:

\displaystyle\int\det(H_{t|y}+R)\cdot\mathbf{1}(H_{t|y}+R\succ 0)\cdot f_{R_{t}}(R)\,dR

\displaystyle=\det({H}_{t|v})\Big(1+\frac{d(y-v)}{v}\Big)

\displaystyle-\int_{\mathcal{R}_{0}^{c}}\det({H}_{t|v})\Big(1+\frac{d(y-v)}{v}\Big)\cdot f_{R_{t}}(R)\,dR

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathcal{R}_{0}}\det({H}_{t|v})\Big(\mathrm{tr}(\{H_{t|v}\}^{-1}R)\Big)\cdot f_{R_{t}}(R)\,dR

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathcal{R}_{0}}\Big(\det(H_{t|y}+R)\mathbf{1}(H_{t|y}+R\succ 0)-\det(H_{t|v})\Big(1+\frac{d(y-v)}{v}+\mathrm{tr}(\{H_{t|v}\}^{-1}R)\Big)\cdot f_{R_{t}}(R)\,dR

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathcal{R}_{0}^{c}}\det(H_{t|v}+R)\cdot\mathbf{1}(H_{t|v}+R\succ 0)\cdot f_{R_{t}}(R)\,dR.

The first term on the right hand side of the equality above is the claimed local expansion of the determinant term. The second term on the right hand side is the error due to “undoing” truncation to
\mathcal{R}_{0}
. The third term integrates an odd function over a set that is symmetric about the origin, and is thus zero. The fourth term is error due to local expansion, and the last term is error due to truncating to
\mathcal{R}_{0}
. Thus it remains only to bound the various sources of error, due to local expansion, truncation, and undoing the truncation.

In bounding the error due to local expansion, we apply a first-order Taylor expansion of
\det(H_{t|y}+R)
about
y=v
,
R=0
to conclude that for all
v>C\sqrt{\log v}
the following is true: for all
t\in\mathcal{T}_{0},y\in v\pm\sqrt{3\log v}
and
R\in B_{d\times d}(0,\sqrt{3\log v})

\Big|\det(H_{t|y}+R)-\det(H_{t|v})\cdot\Big(1+\frac{d(y-v)}{v}+\mathrm{tr}(\{H_{t|v}\}^{-1}R)\Big)\Big|\leq C\Big(\frac{(y-v)^{2}}{v^{2}}+\frac{\|R\|^{2}}{v^{2}}\Big),

and moreover
H_{t|y}+R\succ 0
. Thus error due to local expansion is at most

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{R}_{0}}\Big(\det(H_{t|y}+R)\mathbf{1}(H_{t|y}+R\succ 0)-\det(H_{t|v})\Big(1+\frac{d(y-v)}{v}+\mathrm{tr}(\{H_{t|v}\}^{-1}R)\Big)\cdot f_{R_{t}}(R)\,dR

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{\mathcal{R}_{0}}\Big(\frac{(y-v)^{2}}{v^{2}}+\frac{\|R\|^{2}}{v^{2}}\Big)\cdot f_{R_{t}}(R)\,dR

\displaystyle\leq C\Big(\frac{(y-v)^{2}}{v^{2}}+\frac{1}{v^{2}}\Big).

Arguments similar to those used in the proof of Lemma
1
show that the error due to truncation and undoing the truncation are both at most
C\det(H_{t|v})/v^{2}
. This completes the proof of Lemma
7
.

10.7
Proof of Proposition
2

We begin by deriving the upper bound on the relative error between
\bar{\mathbb{E}}[N(\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}})]
and
\mathbb{E}[N(\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}})]
, and then proceed to upper bound the error between
\mathbb{E}[N_{u}(\mathcal{B}_{t^{*}})]
and
\mathbb{E}[N(\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}})]
.

10.7.1
Error between
\bar{\mathbb{E}}[N(\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}})]
and
\mathbb{E}[N(\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}})]
.

Define
\rho(y):=\int_{\mathcal{B}_{t^{*}}}\rho(t,y)\,dt
. One can think of this as the intensity function of peaks of height
y
across
\mathcal{B}_{t^{*}}
. The following Lemma computes a local expansion
\bar{\rho}(y)
to
\rho(y)
by integrating
\bar{\rho}(t,y)
over
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. There are two types of error incurred: error due to replacing
\rho(t,y)
by
\bar{\rho}(t,y)
, and error due to integrating over
\mathbb{R}^{d}
rather than
\mathcal{B}_{t^{*}}
. These are bounded by the following:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Err}_{\rho}(\mathcal{B}_{t^{*}},y)

\displaystyle:=C\Big(|y-\bar{u}_{t^{*}}|^{2}\delta_{n}^{2}+|\bar{u}_{t^{*}}-\mu_{t^{*}}|\delta_{n}^{2}+\delta_{n}^{2}\Big)

\displaystyle{\rm Trunc}_{\rho}(\mathcal{B}_{t^{*}},y)

\displaystyle:=C\delta_{n}^{2}

Lemma 8
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
1
, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
sufficiently large, for any
y\in\mathcal{I}_{t^{*}}
,

\displaystyle\frac{\big|\rho(y)-\bar{\rho}(y)\big|}{\bar{\rho}(y)}\leq\mathrm{Err}_{\rho}(\mathcal{B}_{t^{*}},y)+{\rm Trunc}_{\rho}(\mathcal{B}_{t^{*}},y):=\mathrm{Err}_{\rho}(y),

(82)

where

\displaystyle\bar{\rho}(y)

\displaystyle:=\bar{\mathbb{E}}[N(\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}})]\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\frac{\exp\Big(-\frac{1}{2}\big(y-\mu_{t^{*}}-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}(\bar{H}_{t^{*}}^{-1}\Lambda_{t^{*}})\big)^{2}\Big)}{\Psi(u-\mu_{t^{*}}-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}(\bar{H}_{t^{*}}^{-1}\Lambda_{t^{*}}))}\cdot\mathbf{1}(y>u).

(83)

The proof of Lemma
8
is given in Section
10.8
. Now we want to use Lemma
8
to bound the difference between
\mathbb{E}[N(\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}})]=\int_{\mathcal{I}_{t^{*}}}\rho(y)\,dy
and
\bar{\mathbb{E}}[N(\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}})]
. To do so we substitute
\bar{\rho}(y)
for
\rho(y)
and undo the truncation in the integral defining
\mathbb{E}[N(\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}})]
, yielding the decomposition:

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{I}_{t^{*}}}{\rho}(y)\,dy

\displaystyle=\int_{u}^{\infty}\bar{\rho}(y)\,dy-\int_{(u,\infty)\setminus\mathcal{I}_{t^{*}}}\bar{\rho}(y)\,dy+\int_{\mathcal{I}_{t^{*}}}\rho(y)-\bar{\rho}(y)\,dy.

(84)

Direct calculation shows that the first term on the right hand side above is exactly
\int_{u}^{\infty}\bar{\rho}(y)\,dy=\bar{\mathbb{E}}[N(\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}})]
. The remaining terms represent error due to local expansion and undoing the truncation, and we now bound each.

Error due to local expansion.

The error due to approximation of
\rho(y)
by
\bar{\rho}(y)
is at most

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{I}_{t^{*}}}|\rho(y)-\bar{\rho}(y)|\,dy\leq\int_{\mathcal{I}_{t^{*}}}\mathrm{Err}_{\rho}(y)\cdot\bar{\rho}(y)\,dy\leq C\delta_{n}^{2}\Big((1+|\bar{u}_{t^{*}}-\mu_{t^{*}}|)\cdot\bar{\mathbb{E}}[N(\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}})]+\int_{\mathcal{I}_{t^{*}}}|y-\bar{u}_{t^{*}}|^{2}\bar{\rho}(y)\,dy\Big).

The remaining integral can be upper bounded using the Truncated Gaussian moment bounds in Section
13.1
: letting
m=\mu_{t^{*}}+\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}(\bar{H}_{t^{*}}^{-1}\Lambda_{t^{*}})
,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{I}_{t^{*}}}|y-\bar{u}_{t^{*}}|^{2}\bar{\rho}(y)\,dy

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\bar{\mathbb{E}}[N(\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}})]}{\Psi(\bar{u}_{t^{*}}-m)}\int_{u}^{\infty}\frac{|y-\bar{u}_{t^{*}}|^{2}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\cdot\exp\Big(-\frac{1}{2}\big(y-m\big)^{2}\Big)\,dy

\displaystyle=\frac{\bar{\mathbb{E}}[N(\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}})]}{\Psi(\bar{u}_{t^{*}}-m)}\int_{u-m}^{\infty}|z-(\bar{u}_{t^{*}}-m)|^{2}\phi(z)\,dz

\displaystyle\leq C\cdot\bar{\mathbb{E}}[N(\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}})].

Thus, the relative error due to local expansion is at most

\int_{\mathcal{I}_{t^{*}}}|\rho(y)-\bar{\rho}(y)|\,dy\leq C\cdot\bar{\mathbb{E}}[N(\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}})]\cdot\Big(|\bar{u}_{t^{*}}-\mu_{t^{*}}|\delta_{n}^{2}+\delta_{n}^{2}\Big).

Error due to undoing the truncation.

Now we bound the error due to integrating over
(u,\infty)
rather than over
\mathcal{I}_{t^{*}}
. In bounding this error we will use the fact that
\mathrm{tr}(\bar{H}_{t^{*}}^{-1}\Lambda_{t^{*}})\leq C\delta_{n}
, and we will assume that
n\in\mathbb{N}
is sufficiently large so that
C\delta_{n}<1
. We partition the complement of this interval,
\mathcal{I}_{t^{*}}^{c}=(u,\infty)\setminus\mathcal{I}_{t^{*}}
, into
\mathcal{I}_{-}^{c}:=[u,\max(\bar{u}_{t^{*}}-\Delta_{n},u))
and
\mathcal{I}_{+}^{c}:=(\bar{u}_{t^{*}}+\Delta_{n},\infty)
, so that the truncation error is the sum of lower and upper truncation error terms,

\int_{(u,\infty)\setminus\mathcal{I}_{t^{*}}}\bar{\rho}(y)\,dy=\int_{\mathcal{I}_{-}^{c}}\bar{\rho}(y)\,dy+\int_{\mathcal{I}_{+}^{c}}\bar{\rho}(y)\,dy.

The lower truncation error is zero if
u\geq\mu_{t^{*}}
, since in that case
u>\bar{u}_{t^{*}}-\Delta_{n}
. Otherwise
\mu_{t^{*}}>u
and
\bar{u}_{t^{*}}=\mu_{t^{*}}
, and so
\Psi(u-\mu_{t^{*}}-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}(\bar{H}_{t^{*}}^{-1}\Lambda_{n}))\geq\Psi(-1/2)>1/4
. In this case the lower truncation error is at most

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{I}_{-}^{c}}\bar{\rho}(y)\,dy

\displaystyle=\frac{\bar{\mathbb{E}}[N(\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}})]}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{\mathcal{I}_{-}^{c}}\frac{\exp\Big(-\frac{1}{2}\big(y-\mu_{t^{*}}-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}(\bar{H}_{t^{*}}^{-1}\Lambda_{t^{*}})\big)^{2}\Big)}{\Psi(\bar{u}_{t^{*}}-\mu_{t^{*}}-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}(\bar{H}_{t^{*}}^{-1}\Lambda_{t^{*}}))}\,dy

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\bar{\mathbb{E}}[N(\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}})]}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{\mathcal{I}_{-}^{c}}\frac{\exp\Big(-\frac{1}{2}(y-\mu_{t^{*}})^{2}\Big)}{\Psi(\bar{u}_{t^{*}}-\mu_{t^{*}}-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}(\bar{H}_{t^{*}}^{-1}\Lambda_{t^{*}}))}\,dy

\displaystyle\leq\bar{\mathbb{E}}[N(\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}})]\cdot\frac{\Phi(-\Delta_{n})}{\Psi(u-\mu_{t^{*}}-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}(\bar{H}_{t^{*}}^{-1}\Lambda_{t^{*}}))}

\displaystyle\leq 4\cdot\bar{\mathbb{E}}[N(\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}})]\Phi(-\Delta_{n})

\displaystyle\overset{(i)}{\leq}4\cdot\bar{\mathbb{E}}[N(\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}})]\exp\Big(-\frac{1}{2}\Delta_{n}^{2}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq 4\cdot\bar{\mathbb{E}}[N(\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}})]\cdot\delta_{n}^{3},

with
(i)
following from a standard bound on Mills’ ratio – recorded in (
144
) for convenience.

A corresponding bound on upper truncation error can again be deduced using standard bounds on Mills’ ratio:

\int_{\mathcal{I}_{+}^{c}}\bar{\rho}(y)\,dy=\frac{\bar{\mathbb{E}}[N(\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}})]}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\cdot\frac{\Psi(\bar{u}_{t^{*}}+\Delta_{n}-\mu_{t^{*}}-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}(\bar{H}_{t^{*}}^{-1}\Lambda_{t^{*}}))}{\Psi(\bar{u}_{t^{*}}-\mu_{t^{*}}-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}(\bar{H}_{t^{*}}^{-1}\Lambda_{t^{*}}))}\leq\bar{\mathbb{E}}[N(\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}})]\exp\Big(-\frac{1}{2}\Delta_{n}^{2}\Big)\leq\bar{\mathbb{E}}[N(\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}})]\delta_{n}^{3}.

We conclude that the error due to undoing truncation to
\mathcal{I}_{t^{*}}
is at most
C\bar{\mathbb{E}}[N(\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}})]\delta_{n}^{3}
, of a lower order than the error due to local expansion.

10.7.2
Upper bound on
\mathbb{E}[N_{u}(\mathcal{B}_{t^{*}})]-\mathbb{E}[N(\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}})]
.

It remains to upper bound the difference between the expected number of
\varepsilon_{n}
-consistent discoveries, and the expected number of
\varepsilon_{n}
-consistent discoveries that also have height
Y_{t}\in\bar{u}_{t^{*}}\pm\Delta_{n}
:

\mathbb{E}[N_{u}(\mathcal{B}_{t^{*}})]-\mathbb{E}[N(\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}})]=\int_{\mathcal{B}_{t^{*}}}\int_{\mathcal{I}_{t^{*}}^{c}}\rho(t,y)\,dy\,dt=\int_{\mathcal{B}_{t^{*}}}\int_{\mathcal{I}_{-}^{c}}\rho(t,y)\,dy\,dt+\int_{\mathcal{B}_{t^{*}}}\int_{\mathcal{I}_{+}^{c}}\rho(t,y)\,dy\,dt.

We will upper bound each of the two error terms above, the lower truncation error and the upper truncation error, separately. In obtaining these bounds, we cannot rely on Theorem
1
because the range of integration is over
y\not\in\bar{u}_{t^{*}}\pm\Delta_{n}
. Instead, we will bound each term in the Kac-Rice formula for
\rho(t,y)
, and integrate these bounds. For both lower and upper truncation error, the following bound on the determinant term in the Kac-Rice formula will be useful:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\det(-\nabla^{2}Y_{t})\cdot\mathbf{1}(\nabla^{2}Y_{t}\prec 0)|Y_{t}=y,\nabla Y_{t}=0]

\displaystyle=\int\det(H_{t|y}+R)\cdot\mathbf{1}(H_{t|y}+R\succ 0)f_{R_{t}}(R)\,dR

\displaystyle\leq C\Big(\det(H_{t|y})+1\Big)

\displaystyle\leq C\Big(\det(H_{t^{*}|y})+1\Big)

\displaystyle\leq C\Big(\det(\bar{H}_{t^{*}})+(y-\bar{u}_{t^{*}})_{+}^{d}+1\Big)

\displaystyle\leq C\Big(\det(\bar{H}_{t^{*}})+(y-\bar{u}_{t^{*}})_{+}^{d}\Big)

(85)

where in the last inequality we have assumed that
n\in\mathbb{N}
