the latter inequality following by (
150
) for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
sufficiently large. This upper bound, along with an application of Hölder’s inequality with conjugate exponents
q_{1}=5,q_{2}=1.25
, implies

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{R}_{t^{*}}^{c}}\|R\|^{2}\exp(-\frac{1}{2}{\rm vech}(R)^{\prime}\Theta_{t^{*}}^{-1}{\rm vech}(R))\,dR

(131)

\displaystyle\leq\Big(\int\|R\|^{10}\exp(-\frac{1}{2}{\rm vech}(R)^{\prime}\Theta_{t^{*}}^{-1}{\rm vech}(R))\,dR\Big)^{1/5}\cdot\Big(\int_{\mathcal{R}_{t^{*}}^{c}}\exp(-\frac{1}{2}{\rm vech}(R)^{\prime}\Theta_{t^{*}}^{-1}{\rm vech}(R))\,dR\Big)^{1/1.25}

\displaystyle\leq C\delta_{n}^{2}.

Finally the approximate density

\tilde{p}(z,R|y)\leq C(1+\delta_{n}\|R\|^{2})\exp(-\frac{1}{2}{\rm vech}(R)^{\prime}\Theta_{t^{*}}^{-1}{\rm vech}(R))\cdot\bar{p}(z|y).

Thus the error due to truncation is at most

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{R}_{t^{*}}^{c}}\tilde{p}(z,R|y)\,dR\leq\int_{\mathcal{R}_{t^{*}}^{c}}C(1+\delta_{n}\|R\|^{2})\exp(-\frac{1}{2}{\rm vech}(R)^{\prime}\Theta_{t^{*}}^{-1}{\rm vech}(R))\bar{p}(z|y)\,dR\leq C\delta_{n}^{2.5}\bar{p}(z|y):=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{{\rm Trunc}}_{p}(\mathcal{R}_{t^{*}})\cdot\bar{p}(z|y).

Error due to normalizing constant.

Equation (
103
) shows that
\mathbb{Q}^{\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}}}(\widehat{R}\in\mathcal{R}_{t^{*}})\geq 1-C\delta_{n}^{2}
. On the other hand, the arguments of the previous paragraph imply that
\int_{\mathcal{R}_{t^{*}}}p(z,R|y)\,dR\leq C\bar{p}(z|y)
. Therefore, the error due to using the wrong normalizing constant is at most

\Big(\frac{1}{\mathbb{Q}^{\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}}}(\widehat{R}\in\mathcal{R}_{t^{*}})}-1\Big)\int_{\mathcal{R}_{t^{*}}}p(z,R|y)\,dR\leq C\delta_{n}^{2}\cdot\bar{p}(z|y):={\rm Trunc}_{p}(\mathcal{R}_{t^{*}})\cdot\bar{p}(z|y),

for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
sufficiently large.

11.11
Proof of Corollary
1

Conditional coverage of the height
\mu_{t^{*}}
by
\mathcal{I}_{\hat{t}}
follows immediately from (
35
). The probability that
C_{\hat{t}}
covers the true location
t^{*}
is

\mathbb{Q}^{\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}}}(t^{*}\in C_{\hat{t}})=\int_{\mathcal{I}_{t^{*}}}\mathbb{Q}^{\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}}}(t^{*}\in C_{\hat{t}}|\widehat{Y}=y)p(y)\,dy.

Theorem
4
gives the approximation

\displaystyle\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{\mathbb{Q}}^{\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}}}(t^{*}\in C_{\hat{t}})

\displaystyle:=\int_{\mathcal{I}_{t^{*}}}\big(1-\alpha-A\cdot\frac{(y-\bar{u}_{t^{*}})+(\bar{u}_{t^{*}}-\mu_{t^{*}})}{2}\mathrm{tr}(\bar{H}_{t^{*}}^{-1}\Lambda_{t^{*}})\big)p(y)\,dy

\displaystyle=1-\alpha-A\cdot\frac{(\mathbb{E}^{\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}}}[\widehat{Y}]-\mu_{t^{*}})}{2}\mathrm{tr}(\bar{H}_{t^{*}}^{-1}\Lambda_{t^{*}}).

The difference between this approximation and the true coverage probability is at most

\displaystyle\Big|\mathbb{Q}^{\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}}}(t^{*}\in C_{\hat{t}})-\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{\mathbb{Q}}^{\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}}}(t^{*}\in C_{\hat{t}})\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathcal{I}_{t^{*}}}\mathrm{Err}_{W}(y)\cdot p(y)\,dy

\displaystyle\leq C\delta_{n}^{2}\int_{\mathcal{I}_{t^{*}}}\Big(1+|y-\bar{u}_{t^{*}}|^{2}+|\bar{u}_{t^{*}}-\mu_{t^{*}}|^{2})\Big)p(y)\,dy.

To bound the integral in the last line above, we first apply Theorem
3
which implies that

\Big|\int_{\mathcal{I}_{t^{*}}}|y-\bar{u}_{t^{*}}|^{2}p(y)\,dy-\int_{\mathcal{I}_{t^{*}}}|y-\bar{u}_{t^{*}}|^{2}\bar{p}(y)\,dy\Big|\leq\int_{\mathcal{I}_{t^{*}}}|y-\bar{u}_{t^{*}}|^{2}\mathrm{Err}_{p}(y)\bar{p}(y)\,dy\leq C(|\bar{u}_{t^{*}}-\mu_{t^{*}}|+1)\delta_{n}^{2},

with the latter inequality following from (
146
). On the other hand,

\int_{\mathcal{I}_{t^{*}}}|y-\bar{u}_{t^{*}}|^{2}\bar{p}(y)\,dy\leq C,

again by (
146
). Thus we conclude that

\Big|\mathbb{Q}^{\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}}}(t^{*}\in C_{\hat{t}})-\big(1-\alpha-A\cdot\frac{(\mathbb{E}^{\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}}}[\widehat{Y}]-\mu_{t^{*}})}{2}\mathrm{tr}(\bar{H}_{t^{*}}^{-1}\Lambda_{t^{*}})\big)\Big|\leq C\delta_{n}^{2}(1+|\bar{u}_{t^{*}}-\mu_{t^{*}}|^{2}),

which is the desired claim.

11.12
Proof of Theorem
5

We will explicitly derive the upper bound on
{\rm PCMR}_{T^{*}}
in terms of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{{\rm PCMR}}_{n}
as stated in (
39
). The upper bound on
{\rm PCMR}_{\mu_{T^{*}}}
in terms of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{{\rm PCMR}}_{n}
, given in (
38
), is derived in a very similar way and we omit the proof. Throughout this proof, we write
v\equiv v_{n}
and
u_{{\rm TG}}\equiv u_{{\rm TG}}(\alpha,v_{n})
.

The proof of (
39
) will proceed similarly to the proof of Theorem
2
: we partition the parameter space
\mathcal{T}
into regions and consider the expected number of regions produced that fail to cover in each region. We introduce notation to count the number of points at which a region is produced that fails to cover: for a set
\mathcal{A}=(\mathcal{B}\times\mathcal{I})\subset\mathcal{T}\times\mathbb{R}
,

V(\mathcal{A}):=N(t\in\widehat{T}\cap\mathcal{B}:Y_{t}\in\mathcal{I},t^{*}(t)\not\in C_{t}),

and abbreviate
V_{u}(\mathcal{B})=V(\mathcal{B}\times(u,\infty))
. The overall miscoverage rate of our method is

\frac{\mathbb{E}[V_{u_{{\rm TG}}}(\mathcal{T})]}{\mathbb{E}[N_{v}(\mathcal{T})]}=\frac{\mathbb{E}[V_{u_{{\rm TG}}}(\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon_{n}}^{*})+V_{u_{{\rm TG}}}(\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon_{n}})+V_{u_{{\rm TG}}}(\mathcal{T}_{0})]}{\mathbb{E}[N_{v}(\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon_{n}}^{*})+N_{v}(\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon_{n}})+N_{v}(\mathcal{T}_{0})]}.

As
V_{u}(\mathcal{S})\leq N_{u}(\mathcal{S})
, the miscoverage rate is at most

\frac{\mathbb{E}[V_{u_{{\rm TG}}}(\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon_{n}}^{*})+N_{u_{{\rm TG}}}(\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon_{n}})+N_{u_{{\rm TG}}}(\mathcal{T}_{0})]}{\mathbb{E}[N_{v}(\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon_{n}}^{*})+N_{v}(\mathcal{T}_{0})]}.

(132)

Proposition
1
upper bounds the expected number of null false positives
\mathbb{E}[N_{u_{{\rm TG}}(\alpha,v)}(\mathcal{T}_{0})]
in terms of
\mathbb{E}[N_{v}(\mathcal{T}_{0})]
:

\mathbb{E}[N_{u_{{\rm TG}}}(\mathcal{T}_{0})]\leq(1+C/v^{2})\cdot\alpha\cdot\mathbb{E}[N_{v}(\mathcal{T}_{0})].

Propositions
2
and
3
upper bound the expected number of high-gradient peaks
\mathbb{E}[N_{u_{{\rm TG}}}(\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon_{n}})]
in terms of the expected number of
\varepsilon_{n}
-consistent estimates
\mathbb{E}[N_{v}(\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon_{n}}^{*})]
:

\mathbb{E}[N_{u_{{\rm TG}}}(\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon_{n}})]\leq C\delta_{n}^{2}\mathbb{E}[N_{u_{{\rm TG}}}(\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon_{n}}^{*})]\leq C\delta_{n}^{2}\mathbb{E}[N_{v}(\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon_{n}}^{*})].

For all
n\in\mathbb{N}
sufficiently large,
\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon_{n}}^{*}=\cup_{t^{*}\in T^{*}}B(t^{*},\varepsilon_{n})
is a union of disjoint balls of radius
\varepsilon_{n}
. The expected number of
\varepsilon_{n}
-consistent estimates is therefore bounded below by

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[N_{v}(\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon_{n}}^{*})]=\sum_{t^{*}\in T^{*}}\mathbb{E}[N_{v}(\mathcal{B}_{t^{*}})]\geq\sum_{t^{*}\in T^{*}}\mathbb{P}(N_{v}(\mathcal{B}_{t^{*}})=1).

What is left is to upper bound the expected number of
\varepsilon_{n}
-consistent estimates with corresponding confidence regions that fail to cover the truth. Applying Proposition
2
and Proposition
4
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[V_{u_{{\rm TG}}}(\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon_{n}}^{*})]

\displaystyle=\sum_{t^{*}\in T^{*}}\mathbb{E}[V_{u_{{\rm TG}}}(\mathcal{B}_{t^{*}})]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{t^{*}\in T^{*}}\mathbb{E}[V(\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}})]+\mathbb{E}[N_{\mathcal{I}_{t^{*}}^{c}}(\mathcal{B}_{t^{*}})]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{t^{*}\in T^{*}}\mathbb{E}[V(\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}})]+C\Big((\bar{u}_{t^{*}}-\mu_{t^{*}})\delta_{n}^{2}+\delta_{n}^{2}\Big)\mathbb{E}[N(\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}})]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{t^{*}\in T^{*}}\mathbb{E}[V(\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}})]+C\Big((\bar{u}_{t^{*}}-\mu_{t^{*}})\delta_{n}^{2}+\delta_{n}^{2}\Big)\mathbb{P}(N(\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}})=1).

Finally, we apply Corollary
1
to upper bound the expected number of
\varepsilon_{n}
-consistent estimates of a particular
t^{*}\in T^{*}
with corresponding confidence regions that fail to cover
t^{*}
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[V(\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}})]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[V(\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}})\cdot\mathbf{1}(N(\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}})=1)]+\mathbb{E}[V(\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}})\cdot\mathbf{1}(N(\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}})\geq 1)]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{Q}^{\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}}}(t^{*}\not\in C_{\hat{t}})+\mathbb{E}[V(\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}})\cdot\mathbf{1}(N(\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}})\geq 1)]

\displaystyle\leq C\Big(\alpha+A\cdot\frac{(\mathbb{E}^{\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}}}[\widehat{Y}]-\mu_{t^{*}})}{2}\mathrm{tr}(\bar{H}_{t^{*}}^{-1}\Lambda_{t^{*}})+C\delta_{n}^{2}\big(1+(\bar{u}_{t^{*}}-\mu_{t^{*}})^{2}\big)+\mathbb{E}[N(\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}})\cdot\mathbf{1}(N(\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}})\geq 1)]\Big)

\displaystyle\leq C\Big(\alpha+A\cdot\frac{(\mathbb{E}^{\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}}}[\widehat{Y}]-\mu_{t^{*}})}{2}\mathrm{tr}(\bar{H}_{t^{*}}^{-1}\Lambda_{t^{*}})+C\delta_{n}^{2}\big(1+(\bar{u}_{t^{*}}-\mu_{t^{*}})^{2}\big)+C\exp(-c\lambda_{n}^{2})\Big),

where the last inequality is established in the proof of Proposition
4
. Combining all these bounds, basic algebra implies that

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbb{E}[V_{u_{{\rm TG}}}(\mathcal{T})]}{\mathbb{E}[N_{v}(\mathcal{T})]}

\displaystyle\leq\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{{\rm PCMR}}_{n}+C\Big(\frac{1}{v^{2}}+\delta_{n}^{2}+A\cdot\sup_{t^{*}\in T^{*}}(\mathbb{E}^{\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}}}[\widehat{Y}]-\mu_{t^{*}})\delta_{n}+\sup_{t^{*}\in T^{*}}(\bar{u}_{t^{*}}-\mu_{t^{*}})^{2}\delta_{n}^{2}\Big),

establishing (
39
).

12
Peak inference with randomized selection: details and derivations

In this section we explain in more detail the data splitting and data carving methods for peak inference after randomized selection discussed in Section
6
, and provide heuristic derivations of the local expansions (
40
) and (
41
). Recall that the peak detection portion of both methods is identical, and both methods will select the same randomized peaks
\wc{T}_{u}
. Thus, for both methods we will be interested in the distribution of peaks nearby
t^{*}\in T^{*}
, given that
t^{*}
is uniquely
\varepsilon_{n}^{\gamma}
-consistently estimated by some randomized peak
\check{t}\in\wc{T}_{u}
. (Recall that
\varepsilon_{n}^{\gamma}=\sigma_{\gamma}\cdot\varepsilon_{n}
.) Formally speaking, the event we will condition on is
N^{{\rm sel}}(\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}}^{{\rm sel}})=1
, where

N^{{\rm sel}}(\mathcal{A})=\Big\{(t^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in\mathcal{A}:Y_{t^{\prime}}^{{\rm sel}}=y^{\prime},Y_{t^{\prime}}^{{\rm sel}}>u,\nabla Y_{t^{\prime}}^{{\rm sel}}=0,\nabla^{2}Y_{t^{\prime}}^{{\rm sel}}\prec 0\Big\},

(133)

and

\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}}^{{\rm sel}}:=\Big\{(t^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in\mathbb{R}^{d+1}:t^{\prime}\in B_{d}(t^{*},\varepsilon_{n}^{\gamma}),y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}_{t^{*}}^{{\rm sel}}\},\quad\mathcal{I}_{t^{*}}^{{\rm sel}}:=(\bar{u}_{t^{*}}\pm\Delta_{n})\cap(u,\infty).

Section
12.1
discusses data splitting, and Sections
12.2
-
12.6
deal with data carving.

12.1
Peak inference by data splitting

As discussed in the main text, the general principle of data splitting is that the data used for inference be independent of the data used for selection. In our peak inference context, the data used for selection is
Y^{{\rm sel}}
. Thus, our data splitting method conducts inference using the location and height of peaks of the independent field
Y^{\inf}
,

\widetilde{T}=\{t\in\mathcal{T}:\nabla Y_{t}^{\inf}=0,\nabla^{2}Y_{t}^{\inf}\prec 0\}.

As always, we would like our confidence regions for the location and height of a true peak
t^{*}\in T^{*}
to be valid conditional on
t^{*}
being discovered; in the context of randomized selection, this means valid conditional on
N^{{\rm sel}}(\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}})=1
. To construct these confidence regions, we will be interested in the asymptotic distribution of the height and location of a peak
\tilde{t}\in\widetilde{T}
nearby
t^{*}
conditional on
N^{{\rm sel}}(\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}})=1
; but since
Y^{{\rm sel}}
and
Y^{\inf}
are independent, this is the same as the unconditional asymptotic distribution of
\tilde{t}
.

The asymptotic distribution of
\tilde{t}
and
\widetilde{Y}:=Y_{\tilde{t}}
can be obtained by applying the results of Sections
4
and
5
, with threshold
u=-\infty
, to the field
Y_{t}^{\inf}/\tau_{\gamma}
where
\tau_{\gamma}=\sqrt{1+1/\gamma}
. From this we deduce the following: (1) for each
t^{*}\in T^{*}
, there will be a unique
\tilde{t}\in\widetilde{T}
that
\tau_{\gamma}\cdot\varepsilon_{n}
-consistently estimates
t^{*}
, (2) letting
\widetilde{H}=-\nabla^{2}Y_{\tilde{t}}^{\inf}
and
\widetilde{G}=\widetilde{H}\Lambda_{\tilde{t}}^{-1}\widetilde{H}
,

\Psi\Big(\frac{\widetilde{Y}-\mu_{t^{*}}-\frac{\tau_{\gamma}^{2}}{2}\mathrm{tr}(\widetilde{H}^{-1}\Lambda_{\tilde{t}})}{\tau_{\gamma}}\Big)\overset{d}{\to}{\rm Unif}(0,1),\quad(\tilde{t}-t^{*})^{\prime}\widetilde{G}(\tilde{t}-t^{*})\overset{d}{\to}\chi_{d}^{2}.

In each case, the rate of convergence is
\widetilde{O}(\delta_{n}^{2})
, meaning these are asymptotic second-order pivotal.
Inverting tests based on these asymptotic pivots thus lead to confidence regions that have second-order accurate asymptotic coverage, both marginally and conditionally on
N^{{\rm sel}}(\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}}^{{\rm sel}})=1
. Our overall method for post-selection peak inference via data splitting is summarized in Algorithm
3
.

Algorithm 3

Selective peak inference via data splitting

0:

Field
Y
, gradient covariance
\mathrm{Cov}[\nabla\epsilon_{t}]
, pre-threshold
v\in\mathbb{R}
, significance level
\alpha\in(0,1)
, randomization level
\gamma>0
.

1:

Select peaks
\wc{T}_{v}
by pre-thresholding
Y^{{\rm sel}}
.

2:

At each peak
t\in\wc{T}_{v}
, test the null hypothesis
H_{0,t}
by applying the TG test to
Y^{{\rm sel}}
with significance threshold
\sigma_{\gamma}\cdot u_{{\rm TG}}(\alpha,v)
, making discoveries
\wc{T}_{u_{{\rm TG}}(\alpha,v)}
.

3:

For each discovery
\check{t}\in\wc{T}_{u_{{\rm TG}}(\alpha,v)}
, let
\tilde{t}:=\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{t\in\widetilde{T}}\|\check{t}-\tilde{t}\|
be the nearest peak in
Y^{\inf}
. Return confidence regions

\displaystyle I_{\hat{t}}^{{\rm split}}

\displaystyle:=\Big\{\mu:\frac{\alpha}{2}\leq\Psi\Big(\frac{\widetilde{Y}-\mu_{t^{*}}-\frac{\tau_{\gamma}^{2}}{2}\mathrm{tr}(\widetilde{H}^{-1}\Lambda_{\tilde{t}})}{\tau_{\gamma}}\Big)\leq 1-\frac{\alpha}{2}\Big\}

(134)

\displaystyle C_{\hat{t}}^{{\rm split}}

\displaystyle:=\Big\{t:(\tilde{t}-t)^{\prime}\widetilde{G}(\tilde{t}-t)\leq q_{\chi_{d}^{2}}(1-\alpha)\Big\}.

12.2
Peak inference by data carving

We calibrate post-selection peak inference via data carving using the conditional distribution

\mathbb{Q}^{{\rm carve}}(\mathcal{A}):=\mathbb{P}\Big((\hat{t},\widehat{Y})\in\mathcal{A}|N^{{\rm sel}}(\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}}^{{\rm sel}})=1,N(\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}}^{\gamma})=1\Big),

(135)

where

\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}}^{\gamma}:=\Big\{(t,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{d+1}:t\in B_{d}(t^{*},\varepsilon_{n}),y\in\mathcal{I}_{t^{*}}^{\gamma}\Big\},\quad\mathcal{I}_{t^{*}}^{\gamma}:=\bar{u}_{t^{*}}^{\gamma}\pm\Delta_{n},\bar{u}_{t^{*}}^{\gamma}:=(1-\pi)\bar{u}_{t^{*}}+\pi\mu_{t^{*}},

and we recall that
\pi=\gamma/(1+\gamma)
corresponds to the fraction of information “left behind” for inference. The event
N^{{\rm sel}}(\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}}^{{\rm sel}})=1
corresponds to the event that
t^{*}
is
\varepsilon_{n}^{\gamma}
-consistently discovered by a randomized peak
\check{t}
. The event
N(\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}}^{\gamma})=1
occurs with asymptotic probability tending to one, and ensures there is a unique full-data peak nearby
t^{*}
.

A formula for the
\mathbb{Q}^{{\rm carve}}
-distribution of
(\hat{t},\widehat{Y})
can be derived from the joint distribution of peaks
\widehat{T},\wc{T}
. Let
p^{{\rm pair}}(t,y,t^{\prime},y^{\prime})
be the probability density of
(\hat{t},\widehat{Y},\check{t},\wc{Y})
under the event
N^{{\rm sel}}(\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}}^{{\rm sel}})=1,N(\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}}^{\gamma})=1
: then

\mathbb{Q}^{{\rm carve}}(\mathcal{A})=\int_{\mathcal{A}}\int_{\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}}^{{\rm sel}}}p^{{\rm pair}}(t,y,t^{\prime},y^{\prime})\,dt^{\prime}\,dy^{\prime},\quad\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}}^{\gamma}

and thus the probability density of
(\hat{t},\widehat{Y})
under the same event is

p^{{\rm carve}}(t,y)=\int_{\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}}^{{\rm sel}}}p^{{\rm pair}}(t,y,t^{\prime},y^{\prime})\,dt^{\prime}\,dy^{\prime}.

The density of the height, and the conditional density of the location given the height, are correspondingly given by

p^{{\rm carve}}(y):=\int_{\mathcal{B}_{t^{*}}}p^{{\rm carve}}(t,y)\,dt,\quad p^{{\rm carve}}(t|y):=\frac{p^{{\rm carve}}(t,y)}{p^{{\rm carve}}(y)}.

Roadmap.

We now provide a high-level roadmap of the heuristic derivations of our approximations to
p^{{\rm carve}}(t,y),p^{{\rm carve}}(y)
and
p^{{\rm carve}}(t|y)
. As in our analysis of the non-randomized case, a central role is played by the intensity function of a point process that counts peaks. In this case the relevant point process counts
pairs
of peaks of
Y
and
Y^{{\rm sel}}
. We introduce the vector-valued random field
\Phi_{t,t^{\prime},y,y^{\prime}}=(Y_{t^{\prime}}^{{\rm sel}}-y^{\prime},Y_{t}-y,\nabla Y_{t^{\prime}}^{{\rm sel}},\nabla Y_{t})
, and notice that pairs of peaks correspond to zeros of
\Phi
:

N^{{\rm pair}}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{A}^{\prime}):=N\Big\{(t,y)\in\mathcal{A},(t^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime}:\Phi_{t,t^{\prime},y,y^{\prime}}=0,\nabla^{2}Y_{t}\prec 0,\nabla^{2}Y_{t^{\prime}}^{{\rm sel}}\prec 0\Big\}.

(136)

The Kac-Rice theorem yields a formula for the intensity
\rho^{{\rm pair}}(t,y,t^{\prime},y^{\prime})
of
N^{{\rm pair}}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{A^{\prime}})
.
In Section
12.3
, we provide a local expansion of
\rho^{{\rm pair}}(t,y,t^{\prime},y^{\prime})
around
t^{\prime}=t,y^{\prime}=\bar{u}_{t^{*}}
. Then in Sections
12.4
and
12.5
we marginalize this local expansion over
(t^{\prime},y^{\prime})
to obtain approximations
\bar{\rho}^{{\rm carve}}(t,y)
and
\bar{\rho}^{{\rm carve}}(y)
to the “marginal” intensity functions

\displaystyle\rho^{{\rm carve}}(t,y):=\int_{\mathcal{I}_{t^{*}}^{\gamma}}\int_{\mathcal{B}_{t^{*}}^{\gamma}}\rho^{{\rm pair}}(t,y,t^{\prime},y^{\prime})\,dt^{\prime}\,dy^{\prime},\quad\rho^{{\rm carve}}(y):=\int_{\mathcal{B}_{t^{*}}}\rho^{{\rm carve}}(t,y)\,dt.

Finally, our approximations to the conditional densities of interest will be

\bar{p}^{{\rm carve}}(t|y)=\frac{\bar{\rho}^{{\rm carve}}(t,y)}{\bar{\rho}^{{\rm carve}}(y)},\quad\bar{p}^{{\rm carve}}(y)\propto\bar{\rho}^{{\rm carve}}(y).

yielding the explicit local expansions stated in (
40
) and (
41
).

Our calculations will be heuristic in two senses. First, we will not provide explicit upper bounds on the relative error due to local expansion and truncation, though we expect that the error terms are ultimately second-order. Second, we are (implicitly) approximating the density
p^{{\rm carve}}
(up to a constant of
proportionality) by the intensity
\rho^{{\rm pair}}
, through

p^{{\rm carve}}(t,y,t^{\prime},y^{\prime})=\lim_{r\to 0}\frac{1}{\nu_{r}}\frac{\mathbb{P}(N^{{\rm pair}}(\mathcal{A}_{r},\mathcal{A}_{r}^{\prime})=1)}{\mathbb{P}(N^{{\rm pair}}(\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}}^{\gamma},\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}}^{{\rm sel}})=1)}\approx\lim_{r\to 0}\frac{\mathbb{E}[N^{{\rm pair}}(\mathcal{A}_{r})]}{\mathbb{E}[N^{{\rm pair}}(\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}}^{\gamma},\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}}^{{\rm sel}})]}=\frac{\rho^{{\rm pair}}(t,y,t^{\prime},y^{\prime})}{\mathbb{E}[N^{{\rm pair}}(\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}}^{\gamma},\mathcal{S}_{t^{*}}^{{\rm sel}})]},

where
\mathcal{A}_{r}=B(t,r)\times[y-r,y+r]
and
\mathcal{A}_{r}^{\prime}=B(t^{\prime},r)\times[y^{\prime}-r,y^{\prime}+r]
and
\nu_{r}
is the (Lebesgue) measure of
\mathcal{A}_{r}\times\mathcal{A}_{r}^{\prime}
. This approximation ignores the possibility that
N^{{\rm pair}}(\cdot,\cdot)>1
.
We expect that analysis similar to that used in the proof of Proposition
4
should confirm that the error thus incurred is at most
C\exp(-c\lambda_{n}^{2})
but we do not give an explicit bound to that effect.

12.3
Local expansion of
\rho^{{\rm pair}}

Under the smoothness assumptions of Section
2.1
, the counting process
N^{{\rm pair}}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{A}^{\prime})
satisfies all of the conditions of the Kac-Rice theorem, so long as
\gamma>0
to rule out degenerate cases. Thus we have a Kac-Rice formula for its intensity function,

\rho^{{\rm pair}}(t,y,t^{\prime},y^{\prime})=\mathbb{E}\Big[|\det(\nabla\Phi_{t,t^{\prime},y,y^{\prime}})|\cdot\mathbf{1}(\nabla^{2}Y_{t}\prec 0,\nabla^{2}Y_{t^{\prime}}^{{\rm sel}}\prec 0)|\Phi_{t,t^{\prime},y,y^{\prime}}=0\Big]\cdot f_{\Phi_{t,t^{\prime},y,y^{\prime}}}(0).

(137)

Above
\nabla\Phi_{t,t^{\prime},y,y^{\prime}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2(d+1)\times 2(d+1)}
is the Jacobian of
\Phi
, while
f_{\Phi_{t,t^{\prime},y,y^{\prime}}}(0)
is the (Gaussian) density of
\Phi_{t,t^{\prime},y,y^{\prime}}
evaluated at
0
. From this formula we derive a local expansion
\bar{\rho}^{{\rm pair}}(t,y,t+h,y^{\prime})
around
h=0,y^{\prime}=\bar{u}_{t^{*}}
, as usual by separately computing local expansions of the density and determinant terms.

12.3.1
Determinant term

The determinant term is

\mathbb{E}\Big[|\det(\nabla\Phi_{t,t^{\prime},y,y^{\prime}})|\cdot\mathbf{1}(\nabla^{2}Y_{t}\prec 0,\nabla^{2}Y_{t^{\prime}}^{{\rm sel}}\prec 0)|\Phi_{t,t^{\prime},y,y^{\prime}}=0\Big].

The Jacobian
\nabla\Phi_{t,t^{\prime},y,y^{\prime}}
is block diagonal and direct calculation shows that

\det(\nabla\Phi_{t,t^{\prime},y,y^{\prime}})=\det(\nabla^{2}Y_{t})\cdot\det(\nabla^{2}Y_{t^{\prime}}^{{\rm sel}})
,
and therefore the determinant term is

\mathbb{E}\Big[\det(\nabla^{2}Y_{t})\cdot\det(\nabla^{2}Y_{t^{\prime}}^{{\rm sel}})\cdot\mathbf{1}(\nabla^{2}Y_{t}\prec 0,\nabla^{2}Y_{t^{\prime}}^{{\rm sel}}\prec 0)|\Phi_{t,t^{\prime},y,y^{\prime}}=0\Big].

Ignoring the negative-definite indicators and swapping determinant and expectation gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\det(\nabla^{2}Y_{t})\cdot\det(\nabla^{2}Y_{t^{\prime}}^{{\rm sel}})\cdot\mathbf{1}(\nabla^{2}Y_{t}\prec 0,\nabla^{2}Y_{t^{\prime}}^{{\rm sel}}\prec 0)|\Phi_{t,t^{\prime},y,y^{\prime}}=0\Big]

(138)

\displaystyle\approx\det\Big(\mathbb{E}[\nabla^{2}Y_{t}|\Phi_{t,t^{\prime},y,y^{\prime}}=0]\Big)\cdot\det\Big(\mathbb{E}[\nabla^{2}Y_{t^{\prime}}^{{\rm sel}}|\Phi_{t,t^{\prime},y,y^{\prime}}=0]\Big).

Reasoning similar to that used in the proof of Lemma
1
should justify that the error incurred in (
138
) is second-order, but we do not prove this. Instead we move to computing a local expansion of (
138
) about
t^{\prime}=t,y^{\prime}=\bar{u}_{t^{*}}
.

Conditional expectation of Hessian.

The conditional expectation of
-\nabla^{2}Y^{{\rm sel}}_{t^{\prime}}
given
\Phi_{t,t^{\prime},y,y^{\prime}}=0
is

J_{t^{\prime}}^{t,t^{\prime},y,y^{\prime}}:=\Big(-\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{\prime}}+\mathrm{Cov}[\nabla^{2}Y_{t^{\prime}}^{{\rm sel}},\Phi_{t,t^{\prime},y,y^{\prime}}]\Big(\big\{\mathrm{Var}[\Phi_{t,t^{\prime},y,y^{\prime}}]\big\}^{-1}\mathbb{E}[\Phi_{t,t^{\prime},y,y^{\prime}}]\Big),

where the array
\mathrm{Cov}[\nabla^{2}Y_{t^{\prime}}^{{\rm sel}},\Phi_{t,t^{\prime},y,y^{\prime}}]
has entries
\mathrm{Cov}[\nabla^{2}Y_{t^{\prime}}^{{\rm sel}},\Phi_{t,t^{\prime},y,y^{\prime}}]_{ijk}=\mathrm{Cov}[\partial_{ij}Y_{t^{\prime}}^{{\rm sel}},(\Phi_{t,t^{\prime},y,y^{\prime}})_{k}]
. We will show that at points
t^{\prime}=t+h,h=\widetilde{O}(\delta_{n})
and
y^{\prime}=\bar{u}_{t^{*}}+\widetilde{O}(1)
,

J_{t+h}^{t,t+h,y,y^{\prime}}\approx\bar{H}_{t}+T_{10}^{J}(h)+T_{01}^{J}(y^{\prime}),\quad\textrm{where}\quad\bar{H}_{t}:=H_{t|\bar{u}_{t^{*}}}.

(The first-order terms
T_{10}^{J}(h)
and
T_{01}^{J}(y^{\prime})
are defined below.) To derive this approximation to
J_{t+h}^{t,t+h,y,y^{\prime}}
, we start by taking a first-order Taylor expansion of each term in the definition of
J_{t^{\prime}}^{t,t^{\prime},y,y^{\prime}}
, which gives the approximations:

\displaystyle\nabla^{2}\mu_{t+h}

\displaystyle\approx\nabla^{2}\mu_{t}+\nabla^{3}\mu_{t}(h)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Var}[\Phi_{t,t+h}(y,y^{\prime})]

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}1+\gamma&K(t+h,t)&0&K_{01}(t+h,t)\\
K(t,t+h)&1&K_{01}(t,t+h)&0\\
0&K_{10}(t+h,t)&(1+\gamma)\Lambda_{t+h}&K_{11}(t+h,t)\\
K_{10}(t,t+h)&0&K_{11}(t,t+h)&\Lambda_{t}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle\approx\begin{bmatrix}1+\gamma&1&0&0\\
1&1&0&0\\
0&0&(1+\gamma)\Lambda_{t}&\Lambda_{t}\\
0&0&\Lambda_{t}&\Lambda_{t}\end{bmatrix}+\begin{bmatrix}0&0&0&\Lambda_{t}h\\
0&0&-\Lambda_{t}h&0\\
0&-\Lambda_{t}h&(1+\gamma)\dot{\Lambda}_{t}(h)&K_{21}(t)(h)\\
\Lambda_{t}h&0&K_{21}(t)(h)&0\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle:=V_{t}^{\Phi}+A_{t}(h)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\Phi_{t,t+h,y,y^{\prime}}]

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}y^{\prime}-\mu_{t+h}\\
y-\mu_{t}\\
-\nabla\mu_{t+h}\\
-\nabla\mu_{t}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle\approx\begin{bmatrix}y^{\prime}-\mu_{t}\\
y-\mu_{t}\\
-\nabla\mu_{t}\\
-\nabla\mu_{t}\end{bmatrix}+\begin{bmatrix}-\nabla\mu_{t}^{\prime}h\\
0\\
-\nabla^{2}\mu_{t}h\\
0\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle:=\mathbb{E}[\Phi_{t,t,y,y^{\prime}}]+B_{t}(h).

Finally, we introduce some notation to compactly write the first-order Taylor expansion of
\mathrm{Cov}[\nabla^{2}Y_{t+h},\Phi_{t,t+h,y,y^{\prime}}]
. For an array
A
, we write
A=\begin{pmatrix}D_{1}\\
\vdots\\
D_{m}\end{pmatrix}
to mean that
A_{ijk}=D_{k}
. In this notation,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Cov}[\nabla^{2}Y^{{\rm sel}}_{t+h},\Phi_{t,t+h,y,y^{\prime}}]

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}-(1+\gamma)\cdot\Lambda_{t+h}\\
K_{20}(t+h,t)\\
(1+\gamma)\cdot K_{21}(t+h)\\
K_{21}(t+h,t)\\
\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\approx\begin{pmatrix}-(1+\gamma)\cdot\Lambda_{t}\\
-\Lambda_{t}\\
(1+\gamma)\cdot K_{21}(t)\\
K_{21}(t)\\
\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}(1+\gamma)\cdot\dot{\Lambda}_{t}(h)\\
K_{30}(t)(h)\\
(1+\gamma)\cdot K_{31}(t)(h)+(1+\gamma)\cdot K_{22}(t)(h)\\
K_{31}(t)(h)\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle:=C_{t}^{\nabla^{2}Y^{{\rm sel}},\Phi}+C_{t}(h).

Combining these approximations, we conclude that the following approximation of the conditional expectation of the Hessian is accurate up to (nearly) second-order relative error:

\displaystyle J_{t}^{t,t+h,y,y^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\approx-\nabla^{2}\mu_{t}+C_{t}^{\nabla^{2}Y^{{\rm sel}},\Phi}\{V_{t}^{\Phi}\}^{-1}\mathbb{E}[\Phi_{t,t,y,y^{\prime}}]

\displaystyle-\nabla^{3}\mu_{t}(h)+C_{t}(h)\{V_{t}^{\Phi}\}^{-1}\mathbb{E}[\Phi_{t,t,y,y^{\prime}}]-C_{t}^{\nabla^{2}Y^{{\rm sel}},\Phi}\{V_{t}^{\Phi}\}^{-1}A_{t}(h)\{V_{t}^{\Phi}\}^{-1}\mathbb{E}[\Phi_{t,t,y,y^{\prime}}]+C_{t}^{\nabla^{2}Y^{{\rm sel}},\Phi}\{V_{t}^{\Phi}\}^{-1}B_{t}(h).

This can be further simplified. In the first-order terms above
\mathbb{E}[\Phi_{t,t,y,y^{\prime}}]
can be replaced by
\mathbb{E}[\Phi_{t,t,y,\bar{u}_{t^{*}}}]
, incurring second-order error
\widetilde{O}(\delta_{n})
. Additionally, the leading-order term above simplifies to

\displaystyle-\nabla^{2}\mu_{t}+C_{t}^{\nabla^{2}Y^{{\rm sel}},\Phi}\{V_{t}^{\Phi}\}^{-1}\Phi_{t,t}(y,y^{\prime})

\displaystyle=-\nabla^{2}\mu_{t}+\begin{bmatrix}-(1+\gamma)\cdot\Lambda_{t}\\
-\Lambda_{t}\\
(1+\gamma)\cdot K_{21}(t)\\
K_{21}(t)\\
\end{bmatrix}\Bigg(\begin{bmatrix}1+\gamma&1&0&0\\
1&1&0&0\\
0&0&(1+\gamma)\Lambda_{t}&\Lambda_{t}\\
0&0&\Lambda_{t}&\Lambda_{t}\end{bmatrix}^{-1}\begin{bmatrix}y^{\prime}-\mu_{t}\\
y-\mu_{t}\\
-\nabla\mu_{t}\\
-\nabla\mu_{t}\end{bmatrix}\Bigg)

\displaystyle=-\nabla^{2}\mu_{t}+\frac{1}{\gamma}\begin{bmatrix}-(1+\gamma)\cdot\Lambda_{t}\\
-\Lambda_{t}\\
(1+\gamma)\cdot K_{21}(t)\\
K_{21}(t)\\
\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}(y^{\prime}-\mu_{t})-(y-\mu_{t})\\
-(y^{\prime}-\mu_{t})+(1+\gamma)(y-\mu_{t})\\
0\\
\gamma\Lambda_{t}^{-1}\nabla\mu_{t}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=-\nabla^{2}\mu_{t}+(y^{\prime}-\mu_{t})\Lambda_{t}+\Gamma_{t}(\nabla\mu_{t})

\displaystyle=\bar{H}_{t}+(y^{\prime}-\bar{u}_{t^{*}})\Lambda_{t}.

Combining these yields our approximation to the Hessian of the selection field:

\displaystyle J_{t}^{t,t+h,y,y^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\approx\bar{H}_{t}+T_{10}^{J}(h)+T_{01}^{J}(y^{\prime}),\quad\textrm{where}

\displaystyle T_{10}^{J}(h)

\displaystyle=-\nabla^{3}\mu_{t}(h)+C_{t}(h)\{V_{t}^{\Phi}\}^{-1}\mathbb{E}[\Phi_{t,t,y,\bar{u}_{t^{*}}}]-C_{t}^{\nabla^{2}Y^{{\rm sel}},\Phi}\{V_{t}^{\Phi}\}^{-1}A_{t}(h)\{V_{t}^{\Phi}\}^{-1}\mathbb{E}[\Phi_{t,t,y,\bar{u}_{t^{*}}}]+C_{t}^{\nabla^{2}Y^{{\rm sel}},\Phi}\{V_{t}^{\Phi}\}^{-1}B_{t}(h)

\displaystyle T_{01}^{J}(y^{\prime})

\displaystyle=(y^{\prime}-\bar{u}_{t^{*}})\Lambda_{t}.

Similar analysis implies that

\mathbb{E}[-\nabla^{2}Y_{t}|\Phi_{t,t+h,y,y^{\prime}}=0]:=H_{t}^{t,t+h}(y,y^{\prime})\approx H_{t|y}+T_{10}^{H}(h).

As
T_{10}^{H}(h)=\widetilde{O}(\delta_{n}),T_{10}^{J}(h)=\widetilde{O}(\delta_{n}),T_{01}^{J}(y^{\prime})=\widetilde{O}(\delta_{n})
, their product is second-order. Taylor expansion of the function
\det(A+E)
around
E=0
finally yields our first-order expansion of the overall determinant term:

\det(H_{t|y})\cdot\det(\bar{H}_{t})\cdot\Big(1+\mathrm{tr}\big(\{H_{t|y}\}^{-1}T_{10}^{H}(h)\big)+\mathrm{tr}\big(\{\bar{H}_{t}\}^{-1}T_{10}^{J}(h)\big)+\mathrm{tr}\big(\{\bar{H}_{t}\}^{-1}T_{01}^{J}(y^{\prime})\big)\Big).

(139)

12.3.2
Density term

The density term can be factorized as

f_{\Phi_{t,t^{\prime},y,y^{\prime}}}(0)=f_{Y_{t}}(y)\cdot f_{\nabla Y_{t}}(0)\cdot f_{Y_{t^{\prime}}^{{\rm sel}},\nabla Y_{t^{\prime}}^{{\rm sel}}|Y_{t},\nabla Y_{t}}(y^{\prime},0|y,0),

(140)

where
f_{Y_{t^{\prime}}^{{\rm sel}},\nabla Y_{t^{\prime}}^{{\rm sel}}|Y_{t},\nabla Y_{t}}
denotes the conditional density of
(Y_{t^{\prime}}^{{\rm sel}},\nabla Y_{t^{\prime}}^{{\rm sel}})
given
(Y_{t},\nabla Y_{t})
, and is given by

f_{Y_{t^{\prime}}^{{\rm sel}},\nabla Y_{t^{\prime}}^{{\rm sel}}|Y_{t},\nabla Y_{t}}(y^{\prime},0|y,0)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{(2\pi)^{d+1}\det(V_{t^{\prime}|t,y})}}\cdot\exp\Big(-\frac{1}{2}(\mu_{t^{\prime}|t,y}~\nabla\mu_{t^{\prime}|t,y})^{\prime}V_{t^{\prime}|t,y}^{-1}(\mu_{t^{\prime}|t,y}~\nabla\mu_{t^{\prime}|t,y})\Big).

Above

\displaystyle V_{t^{\prime}|t,y}:=\mathrm{Var}[(Y_{t^{\prime}}^{{\rm sel}},\nabla Y_{t^{\prime}}^{{\rm sel}})|Y_{t},\nabla Y_{t}],\;\mu_{t^{\prime}|t,y}:=\mathbb{E}[Y_{t^{\prime}}^{{\rm sel}}|Y_{t}=y,\nabla Y_{t}=0],\;\nabla\mu_{t^{\prime}|t,y}:=\mathbb{E}[\nabla Y_{t^{\prime}}^{{\rm sel}}|Y_{t}=y,\nabla Y_{t}=0].

We now give expansions of each of these terms, and then plug back into (
140
) to give an overall expansion of the density term.

Conditional expectation.

The conditional expectation of
Y_{t+h}^{{\rm sel}}|Y_{t}=y,\nabla Y_{t}=0
is

\mu_{t+h|t,y}=\mu_{t+h}+K(t+h,t)(y-\mu_{t})+K_{01}(t+h,t)\Lambda_{t}^{-1}(0-\nabla\mu_{t}).

Taking a second-order Taylor expansion of
\mu_{t+h|t,y}
around
h=0
and disregarding terms that are
\widetilde{O}(\delta_{n}^{2})
gives

\displaystyle\mu_{t+h|t,y}\approx\mu_{t}+\frac{1}{2}h^{\prime}\nabla^{2}\mu_{t}h+(y-\mu_{t})-\frac{1}{2}h^{\prime}\Lambda_{t}h(y-\mu_{t})-h^{\prime}\nabla\mu_{t}-K_{21}(t)(h,h)^{\prime}\Lambda_{t}^{-1}\nabla\mu_{t}=y-\frac{1}{2}h^{\prime}H_{t|y}h.

Similarly, the conditional expectation of
\nabla{Y}_{t+h}^{{\rm sel}}|\{Y_{t}=y,\nabla Y_{t}=0\}
is

\nabla\mu_{t+h|t,y}=\nabla\mu_{t+h}+K_{10}(t+h,t)(y-\mu_{t})+K_{11}(t+h,t)\Lambda_{t}^{-1}(0-\nabla\mu_{t}).

Taking a first-order Taylor expansion of
\nabla\mu_{t+h|t,y}
around
h=0
and disregarding terms that are
\widetilde{O}(\delta_{n}^{2})
gives

\displaystyle\nabla\mu_{t+h|t,y}\approx-H_{t|y}h+\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{3}\mu_{t}(h,h)+\frac{1}{2}(y-\mu_{t})K_{30}(t)(h,h).

Conditional variance.

The conditional variance is

\displaystyle V_{t+h|t,y}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}1+\gamma-\{K(t+h,t)\}^{2}-K_{01}(t+h,t)\Lambda_{t}^{-1}K_{10}(t,t+h)&-K(t+h,t)K_{01}(t,t+h)^{\prime}-K_{01}(t+h,t)^{\prime}\Lambda_{t}^{-1}K_{11}(t,t+h)\\
-K_{11}(t+h,t)\Lambda_{t}^{-1}K_{01}(t,t+h)-K(t+h,t)K_{01}(t,t+h)&(1+\gamma)\Lambda_{t}-K_{11}(t+h,t)\Lambda_{t}^{-1}K_{11}(t,t+h)\end{bmatrix}.

Taking a first-order Taylor expansion of
V_{t+h|t,y}
around
h=0
and disregarding terms that are
\widetilde{O}(\delta_{n}^{2})
yields

V_{t+h|t,y}\approx\begin{bmatrix}\gamma&0^{\prime}\\
0&\gamma\Lambda_{t}-\dot{\Lambda}_{t}(h)\end{bmatrix}.

Local expansion of density term.

Plugging in our approximations to the conditional mean and variance, applying Taylor expansion of the matrix inverse and determinant functions, and further disregarding all terms that are
\widetilde{O}(\delta_{n}^{2})
yields our local expansion of the density term:

\frac{1+T_{10}^{p_{|t,y}}(h)+T_{30}^{p_{|t,y}}(h)+T_{21}^{p_{t|y}}(h,y^{\prime})+T_{11}^{p_{t|y}}(h,y^{\prime})}{\sqrt{(2\pi)^{d+1}\gamma^{d+1}\det(\Lambda_{t})}}\exp\Big(-\frac{1}{2\gamma}(y^{\prime}-y)^{2}-\frac{1}{2\gamma}h^{\prime}\bar{H}_{t}\Lambda_{t}^{-1}H_{t|y}h\Big),

(141)

where

\displaystyle T_{10}^{p_{|t,y}}(h)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\gamma}\mathrm{tr}\big(\Lambda_{t}^{-1}\{\dot{\Lambda}_{t}(h)\}\big)-\frac{1}{2\gamma}\nabla\mu_{t}^{\prime}h

\displaystyle T_{30}^{p_{|t,y}}(h)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\gamma}h^{\prime}H_{t|y}\Lambda_{t}^{-1}\Big(\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{3}\mu_{t}(h,h)+\frac{1}{2}(y-\mu_{t})K_{30}(t)(h,h)\Big)-\frac{1}{2\gamma}h^{\prime}H_{t|y}\Lambda_{t}^{-1}\{\dot{\Lambda}_{t}(h)\}H_{t|y}h

\displaystyle T_{21}^{p_{|t,y}}(h,y^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\frac{-(y^{\prime}-\bar{u}_{t^{*}})}{2\gamma}h^{\prime}H_{t|y}h

\displaystyle T_{11}^{p_{|t,y}}(h,y^{\prime})

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}(y^{\prime}-\bar{u}_{t^{*}})\nabla\mu_{t}^{\prime}h.

Local expansion of pairs intensity.

Combining (
139
) and (
141
) gives our local expansion of the pairs intensity around
h=0,y^{\prime}=\bar{u}_{t^{*}}
:

\displaystyle\bar{\rho}^{{\rm pair}}(t,y,t+h,y^{\prime})

\displaystyle:=\det(H_{t|y})\cdot\det(\bar{H}_{t})\cdot\frac{(1+T_{10}^{{\rm pair}}(h)+T_{01}^{{\rm pair}}(y^{\prime})+T_{30}^{{\rm pair}}(h)+T_{21}^{{\rm pair}}(h,y^{\prime})+T_{11}^{{\rm pair}}(h,y^{\prime})}{\sqrt{(2\pi)^{d+1}\gamma^{d+1}\det(\Lambda_{t})}}

\displaystyle\times\exp\Big(-\frac{1}{2\gamma}(y^{\prime}-y)^{2}-\frac{1}{2\gamma}h^{\prime}\bar{H}_{t}\Lambda_{t}^{-1}H_{t|y}h\Big)\cdot f_{Y_{t},\nabla Y_{t}}(y,0),

where

\displaystyle T_{10}^{{\rm pair}}(h)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{tr}\big(H_{t|y}^{-1}T_{1}^{H}(h)\big)+\mathrm{tr}\big(\bar{H}_{t}^{-1}T_{1}^{J}(h)\big)+T_{1}^{p_{|t,y}}(h)

\displaystyle T_{30}^{{\rm pair}}(h)

\displaystyle=T_{3}^{p_{|t,y}}(h)

\displaystyle T_{01}^{{\rm pair}}(y^{\prime})

\displaystyle=(y^{\prime}-\bar{u}_{t^{*}})\cdot\mathrm{tr}({\bar{H}_{t}}^{-1}\Lambda_{t})

\displaystyle T_{21}^{{\rm pair}}(h,y^{\prime})

\displaystyle=T_{21}^{p_{|t,y}}(h,y^{\prime})=\frac{-(y^{\prime}-\bar{u}_{t^{*}})}{2\gamma}h^{\prime}H_{t|y}h

\displaystyle T_{11}^{{\rm pair}}(h,y^{\prime})

\displaystyle=T_{11}^{p_{|t,y}}(h,y^{\prime}).

12.4
Intensity of location and height

We now marginalize over
(h,y^{\prime})
in
\bar{\rho}^{{\rm pair}}(t,y,t+h,y^{\prime})
to compute an approximation
\bar{\rho}^{{\rm carve}}(t,y)
to the marginal intensity
\rho^{{\rm carve}}(t,y)
. Integrating over
h\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
y^{\prime}\in(u,\infty)
, the approximate marginal intensity we obtain is

\displaystyle\int_{u}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\bar{\rho}^{{\rm pair}}(t,y,t+h,y^{\prime})\,dh\,dy^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{\det(H_{t|y})\cdot\det(\bar{H}_{t})}{2\pi\gamma}}\cdot f_{Y_{t},\nabla Y_{t}}(y,0)\cdot\int_{u}^{\infty}\Big(1+\frac{1}{2}(y^{\prime}-\bar{u}_{t^{*}})\cdot\mathrm{tr}(\bar{H}_{t}^{-1}\Lambda_{t})\Big)\cdot\exp\Big(-\frac{1}{2\gamma}(y^{\prime}-y)^{2}\Big)\,dy^{\prime}

\displaystyle\approx\sqrt{\frac{\det(H_{t|y})\cdot\det(\bar{H}_{t})}{2\pi\gamma}}\cdot f_{Y_{t},\nabla Y_{t}}(y,0)\cdot\exp\Big(\frac{1}{2}(y-\bar{u}_{t^{*}})\mathrm{tr}(\bar{H}_{t}^{-1}\Lambda_{t})\Big)\cdot\int_{u}^{\infty}\sqrt{\frac{1}{2\pi\gamma}}\exp\Big(-\frac{1}{2\gamma}(y^{\prime}-y-\frac{\gamma}{2}\mathrm{tr}(\bar{H}_{t}^{-1}\Lambda_{t}))^{2}\Big)\,dy^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\det(H_{t|y})\cdot\det(\bar{H}_{t})}\cdot f_{Y_{t},\nabla Y_{t}}(y,0)\cdot\Psi\Big(\frac{u-y-\frac{\gamma}{2}\mathrm{tr}(\bar{H}_{t}^{-1}\Lambda_{t})}{\sqrt{\gamma}}\Big)\cdot\exp\Big(\frac{1}{2}(y-\bar{u}_{t^{*}})\mathrm{tr}(\bar{H}_{t}^{-1}\Lambda_{t})\Big)

\displaystyle\approx\sqrt{\det(H_{t|y})\cdot\det(\bar{H}_{t})}\cdot f_{Y_{t},\nabla Y_{t}}(y,0)\cdot\Psi\Big(\frac{u-y-\frac{\gamma}{2}\mathrm{tr}(\bar{H}_{t^{*}}^{-1}\Lambda_{t^{*}})}{\sqrt{\gamma}}\Big)\cdot\exp\Big(\frac{1}{2}(y-\bar{u}_{t^{*}})\mathrm{tr}(\bar{H}_{t^{*}}^{-1}\Lambda_{t^{*}})\Big).

Taylor expansion of
\det(\bar{H}_{t})
about
t=t^{*}
and
\det(H_{t|y})
about
t=t^{*},y=\bar{u}_{t^{*}}^{\gamma}
gives

\displaystyle\det(H_{t^{*}+h|y})

\displaystyle\approx\det(\bar{H}_{t^{*}}^{\gamma})\Big(1+\mathrm{tr}\big(\{\bar{H}_{t^{*}}^{\gamma}\}^{-1}\dot{H}_{t^{*}|\bar{u}_{t^{*}}^{\gamma}}(h)\big)+(y-\bar{u}_{t^{*}}^{\gamma})\mathrm{tr}\big(\{\bar{H}_{t^{*}}^{\gamma}\}^{-1}\Lambda_{t^{*}}\big)\Big)

\displaystyle\det(\bar{H}_{t^{*}+h})

\displaystyle\approx\det(\bar{H}_{t^{*}})\Big(1+T_{10}^{\det}(h)\Big).

where
\bar{H}_{t^{*}}^{\gamma}=H_{t^{*}|\bar{u}_{t^{*}}^{\gamma}}
. Calculations similar to those used in the proof of Lemma
2
in Section
9.6
imply

\displaystyle f_{Y_{t},\nabla Y_{t}}(0,y)

\displaystyle\approx\Big(1-\frac{T_{30}^{\nabla Y}(h)}{2}+T_{10}^{\nabla Y}(h)+\frac{1}{2}(y-\bar{u}_{t^{*}}^{\gamma})h^{\prime}\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}}h\Big)\exp\Big(-\frac{1}{2}(y-\mu_{t^{*}})^{2}\Big)

\displaystyle\times\frac{1}{\sqrt{(2\pi)^{d+1}\det(\Lambda_{t^{*}})}}\exp\Big(-\frac{1}{2}h^{\prime}\bar{H}_{t^{*}}^{\gamma}\Lambda_{t^{*}}^{-1}(-\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}})h\Big).

Finally, the exponential term in
y
terms can be written as

\displaystyle\exp\Big(-\frac{1}{2}(y-\mu_{t^{*}})^{2}+\frac{1}{2}(y-\bar{u}_{t^{*}})\mathrm{tr}(\bar{H}_{t^{*}}^{-1}\Lambda_{t^{*}})\Big)

\displaystyle\approx\exp\Big(-\frac{1}{2}\big(y-{\mu}_{t^{*}}-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}(\bar{H}_{t^{*}}^{-1}\Lambda_{t^{*}})\big)^{2}+\frac{1}{2}(\mu_{t^{*}}-\bar{u}_{t^{*}})\mathrm{tr}(\bar{H}_{t^{*}}^{-1}\Lambda_{t^{*}})\Big),

(142)

up to relative error
{O}(\delta_{n}^{2})
. Altogether this yields the following first-order expansion of the joint intensity:

\displaystyle\bar{\rho}^{{\rm carve}}(t^{*}+h,y)

\displaystyle:=\sqrt{\frac{\det(\bar{H}_{t^{*}})\cdot\det(\bar{H}_{t^{*}}^{\gamma})}{(2\pi)^{d+1}\det(\Lambda_{t^{*}})}}\cdot\exp\Big(\frac{1}{2}(\mu_{t^{*}}-\bar{u}_{t^{*}})\mathrm{tr}(\bar{H}_{t^{*}}^{-1}\Lambda_{t^{*}})\Big)

(143)

\displaystyle\times\Big(1+T_{10}^{{\rm pair}}(h)+T_{01}^{{\rm pair}}(y)+T_{30}^{{\rm pair}}(h)+T_{21}^{{\rm pair}}(h,y)\Big)\cdot\Psi\Big(\frac{u-y-\frac{\gamma}{2}\mathrm{tr}(\bar{H}_{t^{*}}^{-1}\Lambda_{t^{*}})}{\sqrt{\gamma}}\Big)

\displaystyle\times\exp\Big(-\frac{1}{2}\big(y-\mu_{t^{*}}-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}(\bar{H}_{t^{*}}^{-1}\Lambda_{t^{*}})\big)^{2}\Big)\cdot\exp\Big(-\frac{1}{2}h^{\prime}\bar{H}_{t^{*}}^{\gamma}\Lambda_{t^{*}}^{-1}(-\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}})h\Big),

where

\displaystyle T_{10}^{{\rm carve}}(h)

\displaystyle:=\frac{T_{10}^{\det}(h)}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}\Big(\{\bar{H}_{t^{*}}^{\gamma}\}^{-1}\dot{H}_{t^{*}|\bar{u}_{t^{*}}^{\gamma}}(h)\Big)+T_{10}^{\nabla Y}(h)

\displaystyle T_{01}^{{\rm carve}}(y)

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2}(y-\bar{u}_{t^{*}}^{\gamma})\mathrm{tr}(\{\bar{H}_{t^{*}}^{\gamma}\}^{-1}\Lambda_{t^{*}})

\displaystyle T_{30}^{{\rm carve}}(h)

\displaystyle:=-\frac{T_{30}^{\nabla Y}(h)}{2}

\displaystyle T_{21}^{{\rm carve}}(h,y)

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2}(y-\bar{u}_{t^{*}}^{\gamma})h^{\prime}\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}}h.

12.5
Density of height

We obtain an approximation
\bar{\rho}^{{\rm carve}}(y)
to
\rho^{{\rm carve}}(y)
by marginalizing over
h
in (
143
). In marginalizing over
h
all of the first order terms cancel:

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}T_{10}^{{\rm carve}}(h)\cdot\phi_{0,\bar{H}_{t^{*}}^{\gamma}\Lambda_{t^{*}}^{-1}(-\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}})}(h)\,dh=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}T_{30}^{{\rm carve}}(h)\cdot\phi_{0,\bar{H}_{t^{*}}^{\gamma}\Lambda_{t^{*}}^{-1}(-\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}})}(h)\,dh=0

by symmetry, while

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}T_{21}^{{\rm carve}}(h,y)\phi_{0,\bar{H}_{t^{*}}^{\gamma}\Lambda_{t^{*}}^{-1}(-\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}})}(h)\,dh=-T_{01}^{{\rm carve}}(y).

Thus marginalizing over
h
in (
143
) yields

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{\det(\bar{H}_{t^{*}})}{\det(-\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}})}}\cdot\frac{\exp\Big(\frac{1}{2}(\mu_{t^{*}}-\bar{u}_{t^{*}})\mathrm{tr}(\bar{H}_{t^{*}}^{-1}\Lambda_{t^{*}})\Big)}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\cdot\Psi\Big(\frac{u-y-\frac{\gamma}{2}\mathrm{tr}(\bar{H}_{t^{*}}^{-1}\Lambda_{t^{*}})}{\gamma}\Big)\cdot\exp\Big(-\frac{1}{2}(y-\mu_{t^{*}}-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}(\bar{H}_{t^{*}}^{-1}\Lambda_{t^{*}}))^{2}\Big)

\displaystyle:=\bar{\rho}^{{\rm carve}}(y).

Our approximation to
p^{{\rm carve}}(y)
is in turn

\bar{p}^{{\rm carve}}(y):\propto\bar{\rho}^{{\rm carve}}(y)\propto\Psi\Big(\frac{u-y-\frac{\gamma}{2}\mathrm{tr}(\bar{H}_{t^{*}}^{-1}\Lambda_{t^{*}})}{\gamma}\Big)\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\cdot\exp\Big(-\frac{1}{2}(y-\mu_{t^{*}}-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}(\bar{H}_{t^{*}}^{-1}\Lambda_{t^{*}}))^{2}\Big),

as given in (
40
).

12.6
Density of location given height

We have given one local expansion of the joint intensity of height and location in (
143
). Pushing the
T_{21}^{{\rm carve}}(h,y)
term into the exponent incurs
\widetilde{O}(\delta_{n}^{2})
error, and yields the alternate local expansion:

\displaystyle\tilde{\rho}^{{\rm carve}}(t^{*}+h,y)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{\det(\bar{H}_{t^{*}})\cdot\det(\bar{H}_{t^{*}}^{\gamma})}{(2\pi)^{d+1}\det(\Lambda_{t^{*}})}}\cdot\exp\Big(\frac{1}{2}(\mu_{t^{*}}-\bar{u}_{t^{*}})\mathrm{tr}(\bar{H}_{t^{*}}^{-1}\Lambda_{t^{*}})\Big)

\displaystyle\times\Big(1+T_{10}^{{\rm carve}}(h)+T_{01}^{{\rm carve}}(y)+T_{30}^{{\rm carve}}(h)\Big)\cdot\Psi\Big(\frac{u-y-\frac{\gamma}{2}\mathrm{tr}(\bar{H}_{t^{*}}^{-1}\Lambda_{t^{*}})}{\sqrt{\gamma}}\Big)

\displaystyle\times\exp\Big(-\frac{1}{2}\big(y-\mu_{t^{*}}-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}(\bar{H}_{t^{*}}^{-1}\Lambda_{t^{*}})\big)^{2}\Big)\cdot\exp\Big(-\frac{1}{2}h^{\prime}H_{t^{*}|y}\Lambda_{t^{*}}^{-1}(-\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}})h\Big).

Taking this and dividing by
\bar{\rho}^{{\rm carve}}(y)
, we are left with

\displaystyle\Big(1+T_{10}^{{\rm carve}}(h)+T_{01}^{{\rm carve}}(y)+T_{30}^{{\rm carve}}(h)\Big)\cdot\sqrt{\frac{\det(\bar{H}_{t^{*}}^{\gamma})\cdot\det(-\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}})}{\det(\Lambda_{t^{*}})\cdot(2\pi)^{d}}}\cdot\exp\Big(-\frac{1}{2}h^{\prime}H_{t^{*}|y}\Lambda_{t^{*}}^{-1}(-\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}})h\Big)

\displaystyle\approx\Big(1+T_{10}^{{\rm carve}}(h)+T_{30}^{{\rm carve}}(h)\Big)\cdot\sqrt{\frac{\det(H_{t^{*}|y})\cdot\det(-\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}})}{\det(\Lambda_{t^{*}})\cdot(2\pi)^{d}}}\cdot\exp\Big(-\frac{1}{2}h^{\prime}H_{t^{*}|y}\Lambda_{t^{*}}^{-1}(-\nabla^{2}\mu_{t^{*}})h\Big)

\displaystyle=\Big(1+T_{10}^{{\rm carve}}(h)+T_{30}^{{\rm carve}}(h)\Big)\cdot\sqrt{\frac{\det(G_{t^{*}|y})}{(2\pi)^{d}}}\cdot\exp\Big(-\frac{1}{2}h^{\prime}G_{t^{*}|y}h\Big),

which is the expression for
\bar{p}^{{\rm carve}}(t^{*}+h|y)
given in (
41
).

13
Technical results

13.1
Gaussian tail behavior: univariate

For any
a>0
,

\frac{a}{a^{2}+1}\phi(a)\leq\Psi(a)\leq\frac{\phi(a)}{a}.

(144)

Suppose
Z\sim N(0,1)
. Using (
144
) and integration by parts,

\int_{a}^{\infty}(y-a)\phi(y)\,dy\leq\frac{\Psi(a)}{a}.

(145)

Arguing recursively, it can be shown that for any
p\in\mathbb{N},p\geq 1
, there exists a constant
C
depending only on
p
such that

\int_{a}^{\infty}|y-a|^{p}\phi(y)\,dy\leq C\cdot\Psi(a).

(146)

Finally, the following lemma bounds the relative error in perturbations of the Gaussian survival function.

Lemma 14
.

For any sequence
u_{n}\in\mathbb{R}
and
\varepsilon_{n}\to 0
,

\frac{|\Psi(u_{n}+\varepsilon_{n})-\Psi(u_{n})\exp(-u_{n}\varepsilon_{n})|}{\Psi(u_{n})\exp(-u_{n}\varepsilon_{n})}=O(\varepsilon_{n}/u+\varepsilon_{n}^{2}).

(147)

If additionally
u_{n}\varepsilon_{n}\to 0
, then

\frac{|\Psi(u_{n}+\varepsilon_{n})-\Psi(u_{n})|}{\Psi(u_{n})}=O((u_{n}\vee 1)\varepsilon_{n}).

(148)

Proof.

The claims are obviously true if
u_{n}\not\to\infty
, and we thus focus on the case where
u_{n}\to\infty
. We write out

\displaystyle\exp(\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon^{2})\Psi(u+\varepsilon)\exp(u\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=\exp(\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon^{2})\int_{u}^{\infty}\phi(x+\varepsilon)\exp(u\varepsilon)\,dx

\displaystyle=\int_{u}^{\infty}\phi(x)\exp(-(x-u)\varepsilon)\,dx

\displaystyle=\Psi(u)+\int_{u}^{\infty}(\exp(-(x-u)\varepsilon)-1)\phi(x)\,dx

For all
x\in(u,\infty)
we have that
0\leq 1-\exp(-(x-u)\varepsilon)\leq(x-u)\varepsilon
, and therefore

\Big|\Psi(u)-\exp(\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon^{2})\Psi(u+\varepsilon)\exp(u\varepsilon)\Big|\leq\varepsilon\int_{u}^{\infty}(x-u)\phi(x)\,dx\leq\frac{\varepsilon\Psi(u)}{u},

the latter inequality following from (
145
). Consequently,

\frac{\Big|\Psi(u)-\Psi(u+\varepsilon)\exp(u\varepsilon)\Big|}{\Psi(u)}\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{u}+\Big|1-\exp(\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2})\Big|\frac{\Psi(u+\varepsilon)\exp(u\varepsilon)}{\Psi(u)}\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{u}+C\Big|1-\exp(\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2})\Big|,

the latter inequality holding as
\frac{\Psi(u+\varepsilon)\exp(u\varepsilon)}{\Psi(u)}\leq C
for all
\varepsilon
sufficiently small. This proves (
147
), with (
148
) following since
|1-\exp(-u\varepsilon)|\leq u\varepsilon
.
∎

13.2
Gaussian tail behavior: multivariate

Consider a multivariate Gaussian
Z\sim N_{d}(0,G^{-1})
, and let
B(0,r)
be a ball centered at
0
of radius
r
. For any vector
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and array
A\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d\times d}
,

\mathbb{E}\Big[(\sum x_{i}Z_{i})\cdot\mathbf{1}(Z\in B(0,r))\Big]=0,\quad\mathbb{E}\Big[(\sum A_{ijk}Z_{i}Z_{j}Z_{k})\cdot\mathbf{1}(Z\in B(0,r))\Big]=0.

(149)

Now consider
U\sim N_{d}(0,I)
. The following is a consequence of a standard Chernoff bound: for any
\eta>0
, letting
r_{n}=\sqrt{(2+\eta)\log n}
, for all
n
large enough so that
r_{n}\geq d
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(\|U\|\geq r_{n}\big)\leq C\{(2+\eta)\log n\}^{d/2}n^{-(1+\eta/2)}.

(150)

13.3
Maximum of a Gaussian process

We record the
Borell-TIS inequality

(
2
)
, slightly restated for our purposes, to bound the asymptotic probability that the supremum of a Gaussian process exceeds some threshold
u_{n}
. Let
X\sim N(0,C)
be a mean-zero Gaussian processes over
\mathcal{T}
, with covariance kernel
C
, and suppose
X
is bounded a.s. over
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathcal{T}
. Let
\sigma_{\mathcal{A}}^{2}:=\sup_{t\in\mathcal{A}}\mathrm{Var}[X_{t}]
and
m_{\mathcal{A}}:=\mathbb{E}[\sup_{t\in\mathcal{A}}X_{t}]
. For any
\eta>0
,

\mathbb{P}\Big(\sup_{t\in\mathcal{A}}X_{t}>u\Big)\leq\exp\Big\{\Big(\frac{1}{4\eta}-\frac{1}{2}\Big)\frac{m_{\mathcal{A}}^{2}}{\sigma_{\mathcal{A}}^{2}}\Big\}\cdot\exp\Big(\eta u^{2}-\frac{u^{2}}{2\sigma_{\mathcal{A}}^{2}}\Big).

(151)

The following consequence will be useful for our purposes: suppose
K(t,\cdot)\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for all
t
,
\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\|K(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C_{0}<\infty
and
\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathrm{Var}[X_{t}]\leq\sigma^{2}<\infty
. Then there exists a constant
C
depending only on
C_{0}
such that
\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\sup_{\mathcal{A}\subseteq B(t,1)}m_{\mathcal{A}}\leq C
. Consequently, there exists a constant
C(\eta)
depending only on
\eta
(and
C_{0}
) such that

\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\sup_{\mathcal{A}\subseteq B(t,1)}\mathbb{P}\Big(\sup_{t\in\mathcal{A}}X_{t}>u\Big)\leq C(\eta)\exp\Big(\eta u^{2}-\frac{u^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\Big).

(152)

13.4
Matrix perturbation

The following are standard results in matrix perturbation theory. Consider sequences of matrices
A_{n},E_{n}
such that
A_{n}
is invertible for all
n
, and for which
\|E_{n}\|/\|A_{n}\|\to 0
. For all
n
sufficiently large,

\|(A_{n}+E_{n})^{-1}-A_{n}^{-1}\|\leq 2\|A_{n}^{-1}\|^{2}\|E_{n}\|.

(153)

A first-order Taylor expansion of
\det(A+E)
about
E=0
yields the following: if
\|E_{n}\|/\|A_{n}\|\leq 1/2
, then

|\det(A_{n}+E_{n})-\det(A_{n})|\leq 4\det(A_{n})\frac{\|E_{n}\|}{\lambda_{\min}(A_{n})},\quad|\det(A_{n}+E_{n})-\det(A_{n})(1+\mathrm{tr}(A_{n}^{-1}E_{n}))|\leq 8\det(A_{n})\frac{\|E_{n}\|^{2}}{\lambda_{\min}(A_{n})^{2}}.

(154)

Validating asymptotic theory

\alpha
.

7.1
are robust to the choice of
\alpha
, we plot the empirical distribution of all quantities, for
\mu_{0}=3
(Figure
5
) and
\mu_{0}=11
(Figure
6
).

Distribution of candidate quantities for location (top row) and height (bottom row), when

u

.

One-dimensional experiment.

The results of an experiment where
d=1
are shown in Figure
7
. In this experiment, the signal and covariance kernel are

\mu_{t}=\mu_{0}\cdot K(0,t),\quad K(s,t)=\exp\Big(-\frac{(s-t)^{2}}{2\cdot 0.15^{2}}\Big).

(155)

Again we vary
\mu_{0}\in\{3,4,\cdots,11\}
and consider thresholds
u\in\{\mu_{0}-2,\mu_{0},\mu_{0}+2\}
. We draw very similar conclusions as in the two-dimensional experiment presented in Section
7.1
, except the agreement with asymptotic theory is even closer.

Figure 7
:
Distribution of candidate pivots for height (top row) and location (bottom). Different columns corresponds to different thresholds
u
. Details of experimental setup and takeaways are in the main text.

14.2
Conditional coverage

Wide signal.

Figure
8
presents the results of an experiment analogous to that of Section
7.2
, but where the signal is

\mu_{t}=\mu_{0}\cdot\exp\Big(-\frac{\|t-0\|^{2}}{2\cdot 0.25^{2}}\Big),

i.e. it is wider than the covariance kernel. The relative performance of methods is qualitatively similar to those given in Section
7.2
, except the coverage of all methods is much poorer at the smaller signal strengths. This is because the curvature of the signal is much smaller.
15
discuss a similar phenomenon when conducting inference for location without selection, and offer a solution that calibrates inferences using Monte Carlo rather than asymptotic theory. It would be of interest to see whether this method could be incorporated into the post-selection inference framework considered in this paper, to improve performance in challenging low-curvature settings.

One-dimensional experiment.

Figure
9
presents the results of an experiment analogous to that of Section
7.2
, but with the one-dimensional data generating process defined in (
155
). The relative performance of methods is qualitatively similar to those given in Section
7.2
.

Figure 8
:
Comparing miscoverage and width of non-randomized, carve, and split methods for peak inference, in the wide signal experiment described in Section
14.2
. Top two rows correspond to inference for location, bottom two rows to inference for height.

Figure 9
:
Comparing miscoverage and width of non-randomized, carve, and split methods for peak inference, in the one-dimensional experiment described in Section
14.2
. Top two rows correspond to inference for location, bottom two rows to inference for height.
